FOREIGN ASSISTANCE ACT OF 1966 TUESDAY, MAY 3, 1966 HOUSE OF REPRESENTATIVES, COMMITTEE ON FOREIGN AFFAIRS, 

Washington, D.C.

The committee met, pursuant to adjournment, at 10:14 a.m., in room 

2172, Rayburn Building, Hon. L. H. Fountain, presiding.

Mr. FOUNTAIN. The committee will come to order. 

The committee meets this morning in open session for a continuation of the hearings on the pending foreign aid bills, H.R. 12449 and H.R. 12450. 

We will hear representatives of the American Farm Bureau Federation. 

Mr. John C. Lynn, legislative director of the Farm Bureau Federation is our first witness. We are delighted to have you with us, Mr. 

Lynn. You may proceed with your prepared statement and in whatever way you prefer.

STATEMENT OF JOHN C. LYNN, LEGISLATIVE DIRECTOR, AMERI- CAN FARM BUREAU FEDERATION 

Mr. LYNN. Thank you very much, Mr. Chairman. I apologize for the bad voice. The change from heat to air conditioning always gives me trouble.

Mr. FOUNTAIN. We have that problem, too, from day to day here. 

Mr. LYNN. With your permission I would like to file the statement for the record as presented and highlight, if I may.

Mr. FOUNTAIN. Without objection the statement will be made a part of the record. 

(The prepared statement of John C. Lynn is as follows:) STATEMENT OF THE AMERICAN FARM BUREAU FEDERATION PRESENTED BY JOHN C. LYNN, LEGISLATIVE DIRECTOR, AND GUSTAVE BURMEISTER, ASSISTANT LEGIS- LATIVE DIRECTOR 

We appreciate the opportunity to present the views of the American Farm Bureau Federation with respect to authorizations for the fiscal 1967 foreign aid program.

Farm Bureau is the largest general farm organization with a voluntary paid membership of 1,677,820 families in over 2,760 County Farm Bureaus in 49 states and Puerto Rico. These families are much concerned, as are many other groups of citizens, with respect to federal budget deficits, the national debt, our balance of payments, and foreign policy decisions because all of these problems have a direct bearing on their well being and prosperity. Through a program of policy development at county, state, and national levels, they have given thoughtful consideration to these problems, and the official voting delegates of the member State Farm Bureaus concluded in part as follows: 

The United States should demonstrate to the world that a dynamic, 739

740 

FOREIGN ASSISTANCE ACT OF 1966 

private, competitive enterprise system is a major factor in contributing to a more abundant life for all people. This requires: 

World investment.

Movement of goods and services among nations. 

Abandonment of policies which restrict the production and distribution of goods and services. 

The United States is an example of the progress possible in a free society. 

Our assistance to less developed nations should be based on carefully prepared, long range plans on which there is mutual agreement and which give proper consideration to the ability of these nations to utilize this aid.

We favor the use of food to assist less developed nations seeking to move toward goals of economic growth, prosperity, and peace. We recommend a program to offer such assistance in a manner that will provide for constructive changes in existing programs.

Among the changes most needed are: (1) To shift the emphasis from surplus disposal to supplying the commodities found to be needed in agreements with the nations to be assisted. (2) To provide that the commodities to be shipped under the program shall be purchased through regular market channels. (3) To require that the recipient nations develop and adhere to acceptable plans showing how food acquired through the program will contribute to economic growth and development. 

The general goals of our Marketing Food for Freedom proposal would be maximum producer freedom, minimum government involvement, market determination of farm commodity prices, and expanding use of our productive resources. This approach would not only serve the interests of developing nations but would permit a phase-out of present price support and adjustment activities.

The above policy resolution reflects a strong belief that major changes should be made in the magnitude and direction of our foreign aid effort. It is gratifying to note that the President's recent message on foreign aid includes many of the concepts that we have advocated for some time. Two paragraphs in that message are of particular significance: 66 **The United States can never do more than supplement the efforts of the developing countries themselves. They must supply most of the capital, the know-how-and the will to progress. If they do we can and will help. If they do not, nothing we can supply will substitute. Nothing can replace resources wasted in political or military adventures. "For the essence of economic development is work-hard, unremitting, often thankless work. Most of it must be done by the people whose future and whose children's futures are directly at stake. * *

We believe that the Congress should continue both its annual review and its direction of the operation of this program. However, we could support authorizations in this area for more than 1 year's duration if they are based upon sound long term plans for individual nations.

The new spending authority requested (NOA) in the budget message totals about $3.4 billion ($2.5 billion for economic aid and $0.9 billion for military assistance). This amount compares with $3.36 billion authorized and $3.2 billion appropriated by the Congress for fiscal 1966. Recently a fiscal 1966 supplemental for economic assistance authorizing $415 million for economic assistance and $375 million for military assistance has been enacted to assist in the war in Vietnam.

It is evident that AID continues to have difficulty in developing sound programs based upon the needs and desires of the recipient countries and the foreign policy objectives of the United States. It appears that there still are excess funds in the pipeline and that a further tightening of requirements and expenditures can be made.

Experience clearly demonstrates that we cannot buy friends and cooperation with money and that we cannot remake people or governments in our own image. 

We should continue to try to help those who need and want our help, making available only such amounts as they can use efficiently and effectively. 

We should give assistance only on the basis of soundly conceived plans that show good possibilities of achievement.

We should insist that private enterprise, both domestic and foreign, be encouraged in countries that are seeking aid for economic development.

FOREIGN ASSISTANCE ACT OF 1966 

741 

We should continue to insist that our economically strong allies assume responsibility for aid to the areas where they have a major trading and political interest.

We believe that the money should be fitted to plans based on sound, well thought out development programs, rather than the plans to the money. 

Another passage from the President's message is as follows: 

*** It must be clear that the principle of our assistance is cooperation. 

Those who do not fulfill their commitments to help themselves cannot expect help from us * * * "" 

MILITARY ASSISTANCE 

Last year we questioned whether military assistance should be included as a part of the foreign aid program or as a part of the appropriation for national defense. We note that the responsibility, operations and funds have been transferred to the Department of Defense. We also note that this year the President recommended this separation and sent to the Congress two bills-one for economic aid and one for military aid.

Most of the military assistance is to countries on the periphery of the communist bloc. Greater care must be taken to limit this aid to countries which are willing and able to fight for freedom and against communist aggression. For example, Indonesia, Egypt and Syria have taken our assistance and then moved definitely toward close cooperation with the communists. India and Pakistan have used our military aid to fight each other, as have Greece and Turkey. 

In view of this situation, we recommend that the military assistance program be carefully reappraised and that the authorization for fiscal 1967 be held to a minimum level. It is our belief that many of the countries now assisted can cover their own requirements and a much larger proportion of the costs of international military headquarters and agencies, including infrastructure development. We note that all Vietnam costs are excluded from the $917 million requested for fiscal 1967. The amount, therefore, indicates that the plans for the balance of the world are to be continued at the usual level. We believe the military assistance program in India, Pakistan, Greece, Turkey, Western Europe and Japan should be sharply curtailed and that the total amount for 1967 can be reduced by at least $217 million. 

DEVELOPMENT LOANS 

The Foreign Assistance Act of 1961, as amended, included an authorization for development loans, totaling $1.5 billion for each of the fiscal years 1965 and 

1966. The Administration requests a 5 year authorization of $1,250 million per year, but requests an appropriation of only $665 million for fiscal 1967. Despite the sharp cuts in appropriations in recent years the unobligated balance at the beginning of fiscal 1967 is estimated at $365 million. Thus, it is evident that the loan program is not nearly as large as indicated by past authorizations. We certainly oppose a 5 year authorization of the magnitude requested. 

Under this program repayments of loans and interest earned remain in the fund for future loans. Repayments plus annual increments from new appropriations have created a capital loan fund of more than $4 billion, and the appropriations for fiscal 1967 will add further to this amount. 

It is interesting to note that the United States has six major direct foreign aid loan programs in operation, with loans outstanding totaling about $12 billion as of the end of fiscal 1965. By the end of fiscal 1966, this total is scheduled to exceed $13.6 billion. The programs are as follows:

FOREIGN ASSISTANCE ACT OF 1966 

In addition, there are available funds of the Eximbank, totaling $9.0 billion, and the dollar pledges of the United States as capital to the International Monetary Fund, the International Bank for Reconstruction and Development, the

International Development Association, the International Finance Corporation, the Inter-American Development Bank, and the Asian Development Bank, which add to a total of $16.5 billion. Therefore, in all of these institutions there is more than $25.0 billion of U.S. public funds available for foreign assistance loans. The interest earned and repayments from loans by all of these institutions are available for reloaning. Also there is about $600 million of P.L. 480 foreign currencies becoming available annually for loans to recipient countries, and $300 million for grants.

On the basis of the Administration's request, the unobligated balance, and receipts, new development loan obligations for fiscal 1967 totaling $864 million are planned. This compares with $630 million scheduled for fiscal 1966. We believe that the development loan program can be held to a much lower level and that the estimated unobligated balance, totaling $365 million, can be reduced. 

If these suggestions are followed, a new authorization of $465 million should be ample for fiscal 1967.

ALLIANCE FOR PROGRESS 

The Administration's request is for a 5 year authorization for the Alliance for Progress amounting to $850 million per year through fiscal 1971, with the proviso that $100 million each year shall be available for grant aid. It is proposed that the appropriation for fiscal 1967 be $455 million for loans and $88 million for grants. These amounts compare with $435 million and $75 million respectively appropriated for fiscal 1966.

In addition to the authorizations and appropriations for grants and loans given above, a total of $2.2 billion has been provided for loans through the Inter- 

American Development Bank since the beginning of the Alliance for Progress to date. This amount includes the $750 million increase in the Bank's Fund for 

Special Operations enacted last year. 

In fiscal 1965 about $477 million was obligated for new loans, and it is planned that this level will be increased to about $506 million in fiscal 1966 and $520 million in fiscal 1967. We believe that conditions warrant a loan program in Latin America no higher than $425 million for fiscal 1967. On this basis, the loan program can be financed with new obligational authority of about $325 million plus a reduction of about $100 million in the unobligated balance which is estimated at $152 million. For grant aid we recommend $60 million. Expenditures for grants during the last few years have been at the level of about $80 million per year, but more and more of the work can now be carried on by local resources.

We also believe that American private enterprise could supply nearly all of the development capital required if the Latin American governments provided a favorable climate. This would be particularly true if private enterprise was encouraged to use the increased loan resources of the Inter-American Development Bank and the investment guarantee program. 

ECONOMIC GRANTS OUTSIDE LATIN AMERICA 

The economic grant program includes funds for technical cooperation and economic development, American schools and hospitals abroad, supporting assistance, contingency fund, international organizations, and administrative expenses. 

The spending authority requested for these categories totals $1,260 million, or $74 million less than was appropriated for fiscal 1966, after adjustment for the $415 million increase in the fiscal 1966 supplemental for supporting assistance in Vietnam and the contingency fund. For fiscal 1965, $912 million was appropriated.

It is noted that $231 million is included for technical cooperation and development, $747 million for supporting assistance, $70 million for the contingency fund, and $11 million for American schools and hospitals abroad-a total of $1,059 million for these four categories. As we have indicated before, these funds actually are used for practically the same purposes. Therefore we consistently have recommended that they be merged into one fund and labeled "development grants." We so recommend again and suggest that the authorization for fiscal 1967 be reduced to $690 million. It should be noted that

this amount, together with a large carryover, will be more than adequate to cover the anticipated expenditures in fiscal 1967, now indicated at $965 million. We doubt that the indicated rapid expansion in obligations for Vietnam can be achieved, and we believe that substantial economies can be effected elsewhere if the President's new proposals are followed. We note also that about $315 million of the 1966 supplemental will be carried over into fiscal 1967. 

Development grants are intended primarily for technical assistance, demonstration, and training to provide developing countries the knowledge with which to increase their standard of living. This program has been in progress in many countries for more than a decade, but it is difficult to measure how much has been achieved. We believe that more of the foreign currencies generated by the new Food for Freedom program can be used for these purposes. 

The Peace Corps apparently is popular in the developing countries. We believe that it is serving much the same purposes as the old technical assistance program and that in some cases it is more effective and efficient. 

Last year we suggested that these programs be combined so that similar functions in the same country can be coordinated. We again so recommend. 

Supporting assistance is another form of development grant made to bolster the economy and political stability of countries receiving military assistance. 

It is indicated that most of these funds are spent in four countries-Vietnam, Laos, Thailand, and South Korea. Here again we believe that more assistance to these areas can be achieved through the Food for Freedom program. 

We strongly recommend that the programming of grant aid be made on the basis of a sound plan for each country where there is a real need and that funds be provided only to fit the plan. If this were done, there would be little need for a large contingency fund. As was demonstrated this year a supplemental authorization can be enacted if the need is urgent. Thus the contingency fund can be reduced to a minimum.

The request includes $140 million to be contributed by the United States to international organizations for various and sundry purposes. For fiscal 1967 it is proposed that the United States contribution to the United Nations De velopment Program, United Nations Peace Keeping (Emergency Forces), and the Indus Basin Development Fund be increased.

Other programs included here are the U.N. Technical and Operational Assistance to the Congo, United Nations Relief and Works Agency, United Nations Children's Fund, and the 

World Food Program. It should be noted that many of these programs duplicate our own bilateral aid programs. Also it should be noted that planned expenditures in fiscal 1967 total $145 million. We believe that $90 million of new spending authority will be ample for fiscal 1967. 

We are concerned that the U.S. contribution to most of these programs is out of proportion to the control we have over them and that they are not very effective. We should resist the attempts of a large number of small countries, which contribute a small proportion of the funds, to extend and enlarge these programs over the objections of those contributing the major share. 

For administrative expenses the request is for $60.7 million for fiscal 1967, compared with $58.3 million in fiscal 1966. We note that this figure rises each year, although the program level has declined. We believe that under the President's new proposals-helping only those who are willing to help themselves the program level will be decreased and that, therefore, the administrative costs can be decreased. We recommend a total of $55 million for fiscal 1967. 

MARKETING FOOD FOR FREEDOM 

We favor the use of food to assist less developed nations seeking to move toward goals of economic growth, prosperity, and peace. We recommend a program to offer such assistance in a manner that will provide for constructive changes in existing programs.

For many years the United States has been supplying substantial quantities of food to less developed nations from surpluses accumulated under our price support programs. Programs such as Public Law 480, which have made this possible, have been of considerable value both to the United States and the recipient nations. Changes in approach could greatly enhance the value of these programs.

This program could be adequately financed from funds now being appropriated for price supports, direct payments to producers, and Public Law 

480. It should:

(1) Be designed to work in harmony with the free market system domestically and to foster expansion of world trade on a commercial basis. (2) Be designed to reduce the costs of government farm programs by providing markets instead of subsidy payments and other price support activities for affected commodities-and not just to add another program. (3) Provide that quantities of commodities to be shipped under the program shall be established and announced far enough in advance to enable farmers to make needed adjustments in production and marketing plans. (4) Provide that once an agreement is reached on the commodities a given nation is to receive, that nation shall be authorized to select the class, grade, and quality of product; determine the markets through which purchases are to be made; and make its own transportation arrangements. (5) Provide that the United States shall not relinquish authority or decisionmaking responsibility to an international agency. 

Last year we recommended that the authorization and appropriation for Food-for-Peace (P.L. 480) be transferred from the Department of Agricul ture to AID. 

It is clear that the so-called Food-for-Peace program is for all practical purposes a foreign aid program. It has become an important tool of foreign policy and has become less and less a surplus disposal operation. Agricultural commodity aid provided through P.L. 480 represented nearly 50 percent of total net U.S. economic aid supplies to underdeveloped countries in the last few years.

The shift of appropriations and program responsibility to AID is especially important at this time because of the new policies and proposals enunciated by the President in his Foreign Aid, International Health and Education, and Food for Freedom messages. 

The spending authority requested for the four titles of P.L. 480 for fiscal 

1967 totals $1,630 million compared with $1,658 million for fiscal 1966. 

Details by titles follow:

In addition, we are recommending to the Congress that $500 million be authorized to continue P.L. 480 for one year-largely for cotton, rice and tobacco, which are not included in our proposal for a Marketing Food for Freedom Program, and that for 1967-68 approximately one-half of the compensatory payments to farmers participating in the wheat and feed grain programs-about $2.0 billion-be used to finance the special foreign food aid programs for these grains.

CONCLUSIONS 

You may note that we have recommended some sharp cuts in authorizations for fiscal 1967. With the transfer of the appropriation for Food for Freedom program (formerly P.L. 480), totaling $1,630 million, and our proposed authorizations for economic assistance for AID, totaling $1,685 million, there would be available to AID for foreign economic assistance the total of $3.315 million for fiscal 1967. We sincerely believe that food aid can be made to play a more important role in our economic assistance programs to the developing nations. This will be very necessary if the President's proposals are

carried out properly. We believe that our aid programs are spread over too many countries. If the program of assistance is limited only to those where the leaders and the people are sincere in their efforts to increase their food supply and really undertake a sound development program, the overall cost to the U.S. will be lowered to the levels we recommend. In such cases we believe great progress can be achieved. 

Again we say, let's fit the money to sound workable plans, not the plans to the money.

Mr. LYNN. After a few remarks I am going to call on Mr. Burmeister to highlight some of our suggestions as to the budget for the foreign. assistance program for fiscal year 1967. 

We have been coming before this committee for many years and making recommendations with regard to the foreign aid program, beginning back some 10 or 12 years ago when we first suggested Public Law 

480 type programs, now known as Food for Peace.

We are concerned, as I am sure the members of this committee are, with the continued vast Government expenditure both at home and abroad, and we will try to demonstrate a little later where we think some reductions can be made in this authorization and still fulfill the needs of those countries seeking our aid. We were very delighted, Mr. 

Chairman, with certain points made in the President's message having

to do with this program in that many of the things in this message have been advocated by Farm Bureau to this committee over a long period of time.

On page 2 of the statement we have quoted two paragraphs from the President's message dealing with this matter. We were delighted to see the word "work" inserted in the administration's presentation to the Congress with regard to foreign aid. 

We have reflected many times to this committee that we don't believe there is enough emphasis given to the matter of work by the aid recipients, if you please, or do more to try to help themselves. We are pleased with this part of the President's message, and feel that it will mean a great deal as we move forward. 

An idea we have presented for 3 consecutive years to the committee is outlined very briefly on page 3 of our statement in the middle of the page, where we say that we believe "the money should be fitted to the plan based on sound, well thought-out development programs, rather than the plans being fitted to the money." Let me just elaborate on that briefly because I think this is the most constructive suggestion we have to offer.

Too often after the Appropriations Committee has provided the money for foreign aid, and we have the feeling, and after some very close checking well, the administrators of this program are too inclined to go out and get a lot of plans to fit whatever money that happens to be appropriated.

What we are suggesting here, Congressman Fountain, is this: Let's take India, for example, and let me add that we were a little disappointed that the United States rushed out to assist India, before getting some commitments. I don't mean to say that we opposed the assistance to India; but we thought this was a good opportunity to get some things done in India that we could get done by the use of our food and aid.

Briefly, here is what we mean by fitting the money to the plan. Take 

India, for example. Let's decide what is in the best interests of the 

United States and our cooperation with India. Is it to supply them food, or is it to help develop their country so they can produce more food for themselves? Make this a 10-year plan or a 5-year plan, if this is what is required. Then each year the administrative agencies of Government would come before this committee and demonstrate to you what progress they are making on the plan that you and, let us say, the AID administrators develop for India. 

Then this committee would recommend that the Appropriations Committee, based on your expert knowledge, fit the money in 1967 to the progress that they have made in carrying out the plan. 

This is contrasted to the way things have been done. In many cases we continue to pour out aid to people who kick us in the pants daily. 

We don't think this is in the best interest of the United States. 

We have at least four good examples of countries that we have helped guide in their economies, and I guess they are the best friends we have. Japan, Germany, Formosa, and Spain, and, if you please, we have not hesitated to help direct the aid programs in these countries, and we believe it has paid off both for us and for them. 

In contrast with that take Indonesia, for example. We continue to aid Indonesia. We continue to aid Egypt, and many of these coun-

TT tries for which we would like to see a little harder rule laid down. We should at least know which side they are on. 

On page 3 of this statement the President, again in his message, said: 

It must be clear that the principle of our assistance is cooperation. Those who do not fulfill their commitments to help themselves cannot expect help from us. 

This is stated very well. We are in thorough accord with this statement. 

Mr. Chairman, with your permission, I would like to ask Mr. Burmeister to review briefly our specific recommendations as to the amounts of money, and then I will come back briefly on food for freedom.

Mr. FOUNTAIN. We are delighted to have you with us, Mr. Burmeister. You may proceed.

STATEMENT OF GUSTAVE BURMEISTER, ASSISTANT LEGISLATIVE DIRECTOR, AMERICAN FARM BUREAU FEDERATION 

Mr. BURMEISTER. Thank you, sir.

With regard to military assistance, certainly we are not experts in this field. We have watched the military assistance program over the years. Certainly we were for it when the idea was to help countries build up a military establishment, particularly on the periphery of the Communist-threatened nations.

I think we have done a magnificent job in building up the military program in Europe. We are a bit put out, though, when we see some of the results. For instance, I looked at the 1965 program, and I find that we are still assisting in Europe to the tune of nearly $50 million, in Greece, in Turkey, and in India and Pakistan, and even some in 

Japan. 

As far as Europe is concerned, and Japan, we think those countries certainly can carry their own load. Their economies are developed now to where they certainly can carry most of their military programs. 

We don't feel that the United States should be putting any more dollars into that area.

For Greece, Turkey, India, and Pakistan, well, we have seen Greece and Turkey fighting each other over Cyprus, and Pakistan and India fighting each other. If that is the way they are going to use our military assistance, then I think we should start withdrawing.

In this year's request the administration says that the military assistance for Vietnam, Thailand, and Laos, that particular area is carried by the Defense Department and is not in this program. That was $500 million in 1965.

The total program in 1965 was $1.3 billion. That would get us down to $800 million. Then if you took out Europe, Greece, Turkey, India and Pakistan, Japan, we would have another $300 million. This would get us down to $500 million.

We are recommending $700 million for military assistance for 1967. 

We think this would be ample to carry on whatever is necessary in the areas that need our assistance and help in developing a military program.

For development loans, here again India, Pakistan, and Turkey have been receiving a large share, plus Brazil, Mexico, Nicaragua, Thai-

land, Nigeria, Tunisia, those are the countries that have been receiving most of the assistance through development loans. I want to point out, on page 5 of our statement, we have a number of different loan pro- grams through the AID process. And through the end of 1965 there was around $12 billion invested in these kinds of loans. It will be $13.5 billion by the end of 1966, and maybe $14.5 billion by the end of 

1967, fiscal years.

In addition, we have all these international loaning agencies which is outlined on page 5 of which the United States is a major contributor in the capital, with a total of some $25 billion for loans to underdeveloped countries.

In addition, the Public Law 480 generated currencies from sales under title I and title IV are available to loan back to the countries for economic development.

We note also that, although we have not provided the total amounts requested in recent years for this loan program, there is still a big unobligated balance to be available as of July 1, 1966, of $365 million. 

We are suggesting that the request of $665 million, be cut back to $465 million, which, with the big carryover of unobligated funds, would provide a sufficient amount for development loans in fiscal 1967. 

In the same sort of reasoning with respect to the Alliance for Progress, we suggest a cut from the requested amount. 

Moving on to economic grants for outside Latin America the amount requested is $1,260 million. We again recommend that the technical cooperation and development, the supporting assistance, contingency fund, and the assistance for American schools and hospitals be combined into one program, that the Peace Corps also be combined with this group, that a coordinated program be developed. We think we would get more for our money and we could save money in this operation. The details are given here in the statement. 

Likewise we suggest cuts in our contributions to international organizations. Many of the programs carried on here are duplications of the programs that we are carrying on under our bilateral operations. So we think this can be reduced some. Then, if this level of a program should be instituted, the money that is needed for administrative expenses could be cut back.

Sir, on the last page there is a table which summarizes our proposals and shows that it will not cut back the proposed expenditures in any of these areas in fiscal 1967. We have to start reducing now if we are to get down to the level we suggest. 

One other thing-and I am sure Mr. Lynn is going to bring this out in more detail—we think our food program could be made a cardinal part of our aid program, that the currencies generated under title I and under title IV or whatever titles are provided in the new program could expand the aid program tremendously. 

Thank you, sir.

Mr. LYNN. We don't want to take too much time, Mr. Chairman, but we have outlined our ideas with regard to marketing food for freedom. The administration's recommendation had to do with food for freedom. The word "marketing" in our title is significant. We think the time has come for the food for freedom or food for peace program to be a part of the AID program and administered by AID.

You will recall that this is where the program was at one time. We take some responsibility for getting it into Agriculture, but we believe that it is now principally an AID program and that we would hope it would be made a part of that agency. 

În summary, we would again emphasize that we ought to do more long-term planning, as the President recommended in his message, but that the Congress ought to review the progress being made in any particular country annually in meeting some of these standards that we hope you will set in this authorization legislation this year. 

Thank you very much, Mr. Chairman, for listening to us.

Mr. Frelinghuysen.

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

Mr. LYNN. Let me try to restate our position. This authorization and long-time plan gets a little confused. If we determine that we are-use India, for example, and I am using it purely as an example, if we determine it is in the best interests of the United States and the world to assist India over the next 10-year period, we would hope this Congress would set up some guideposts to be followed, if aid is to be granted. Certain standards to be met for example with regard to agricultural production, with regard to the market system, with regard to trying to establish some free enterprise and so on. 

Let's suppose you unofficially estimate that it is going to cost a half billion dollars a year. This would not necessarily be in the plan. 

Then the Administration would come forth each year with what they judge to be the progress that has been made, based on these standards

that you have set. Then you would determine annually the authorization for the next fiscal year and then follow with the appropriation based on progress made. 

You might determine, for example, that they had failed in three points out of seven, and therefore only x dollars would be made available because they did not meet these standards. It is the same thing we do with the Federal highway programs, with Federal aid to education, with all of these programs. It is long-time planning but annual review for appropriations.

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. A great many domestic programs-and this may be where the confusion begins-do have long-term authorizations, or reasonably long-term authorizations. It is my personal feeling, however, that in the foreign aid field the international situation changes so dramatically, the relationship between this country and an individual country changes so unexpectedly, that it would be unwise to make too definite a commitment too far ahead. 

You have indicated that we might well be willing to make some kind of a commitment to India which would be substantial, and which would be subject to their performance as to certain criteria that we establish between ourselves and India. The fact of the matter is that we can't tell what the future holds with respect to almost any of these countries. 

You are advocating a withdrawal of what we have been providing or what we may be providing for some of these countries because they may have decided to argue amongst themselves. Well, this isn't giving those countries much assurance about the continuity and the nature of the U.S. commitment.

You suggest we can withdraw at will and despite the fact that danger on the borders of a country like Turkey still remains, you, as I understand it, are advocating cutting back our military aid program to countries on the periphery of the Soviet Union if they see fit to argue with one another. We might be endangering our own security and their security if we follow that advice. I don't wish to argue, but it does make it difficult, I think, to be an armchair strategist in cases like this.

STATEMENT OF MRS. VIRGINIA M. GRAY, EXECUTIVE SECRETARY, CITIZENS COMMITTEE FOR UNICEF

(The prepared statement of Mrs. Gray is as follows:) STATEMENT OF MRS. VIRGINIA M. GRAY, EXECUTIVE SECRETARY, CITIZENS COMMITTEE FOR UNICEF 

Mr. Chairman and Members of the Committee, I am Mrs. Virginia M. Gray,

Executive Secretary of the Citizens Committee for UNICEF. The Citizens Com mittee for UNICEF is an informal clearing house of legislative information serving a number of national organizations which support continued participation by the United States in the work of UNICEF, the United Nations Children's Fund. These organizations have authorized me to present the following joint statement in support of a contribution of $12 million by the United States for Fiscal 1967 (the amount proposed by the Executive Branch) and, in addition, to request a special contribution of $1 million in this next year for the reasons and in the manner indicated later in this statement. The names of the organizations authorizing this presentation are listed at the end of the text. 

UNICEF's 20th Anniversary 

On December 11 of this year, UNICEF will celebrate its 20th anniversary. 

This date will mark a generation of service to children. The first babies and small children, who may owe their very lives to UNICEF's timely aid, have now reached maturity and are taking their places in their communities. These first beneficiaries of UNICEF aid received emergency assistance: food, clothing, medi- 

Yet this simple humanitarian undertaking, born quietly in the aftermath of World War II, marked a new dimension in the development of civilization; it was a recognition of mankind's united responsibility for all children wherever they may happen to be born. cine.

From a relatively small beginning in 1946-in which several members of this Committee, past and present, played a major role the program to aid the world's children has brought life-saving aid to millions of children and mothers. 

In 1965 UNICEF was assisting 543 projects in 118 countries. In its nearly 20 years of service UNICEF has treated 11 million cases of trachoma, over 1 million cases of leprosy, 37 million cases of yaws, and has given 162 million TB vaccinations. In addition to these specific treatments to individual cases, UNICEF has helped to establish and equip over 30,000 maternal and child health centers and subcenters, and has provided scholarship and training allowances to thousands of nurses, auxiliary health and social workers where the extent of the benefits to children are incalculable.

The Nobel Peace Prize Award 

In dramatic tribute to the accomplishments of the Children's Fund, the Nobel Committee of the Norwegian Parliament announced on October 25 that its 

1965 award of the Nobel Peace Prize would go to UNICEF in recognition of its contribution to world peace. The selection of UNICEF to receive this distinguished award marks the 8th time in the long history of the Nobel awards that the honor has gone to an organization; it is the second time that the prize has gone to an agency of the United Nations. The news of the award met worldwide acclaim. It was received by officials of UNICEF itself as an indication that the policies that have guided its actions over the years are basically correct. More importantly, however, it was accepted by UNICEF officials as a challenge to strengthen their activities for the greater fulfillment of children's needs.

U.S. Government Contribution to UNICEF 

The unanimous acclaim with which the announcement of the award was greeted did not increase the United States contribution to UNICEF, however. 

This fiscal year will mark the 8th consecutive year that the Executive Branch is recommending a contribution of $12 million to UNICEF by the United States. 

The organizations listed below endorse this recommendation with a full understanding and sympathy for the State Department's position in this matter. The 

Department's expert knowledge of the pledges already made or anticipated from governments other than the United States has led them to accept the strong probability that UNICEF will find it difficult to raise the necessary sums to match $12 million from the United States at 40% during the present year. With a few spectacular exceptions, such as Sweden which has increased its contribution by 50%, there are few other countries in a strong enough position economically to follow such an outstanding lead. The general upward trend in support continues, however.

Our organizations request this Committee and the Congress to make, in this year of the Nobel Peace Prize Award, a special contribution of an additional 

$1 million as tangible recognition for the outstanding work that UNICEF is doing for world peace. This $1 million contribution would be in every sense a gift-with no strings attached. It would be exempt from the computations of the 40 (United States) to 60 (other countries) matching ratio. We respectfully urge your support of this proposal. 

Why an Increase is Justified

A considerable increase in the U.S. Government contribution to UNICEF above the $12 million level would seem well justified. The following reasons suggest an increase far beyond our request for a special $1 million, but at this time we can only relate the reasons to this request for a modest increase: 

1. Without doubt, the most compelling reason for giving aid is the enormous need of children. Many basic threats to child development have scarcely been recognized, let alone attacked. In addition to the present ongoing programs that we have discussed in past years, there is an area of child needs that is only beginning to be seen. This concerns the problems of the very young child, the child aged 1-6. It is estimated that there are 400 million children in the world, aged 1-6. Of these, 200 million live in the developing countries which are being assisted by UNICEF. These children, weaned from their mother's milk but too young to go to school, frequently suffer from grave malnutrition in these most formative years of their whole life. Recent research has developed evidence that many abilities, motor skills, even intelligence, may be affected by nutritional deficiencies during these early years, defects that no amount of future treatment can overcome. Here, then, is one area where much remains to be done. 

2. The United States, although the largest contributor to UNICEF in dollar totals is not at all the leading contributor on a per capita basis. We rank only fifth, after: Cents per capita

Sweden. 

113 Switzerland_ Norway. 

New Zealand__ 

12 United States.. 7.9 

1 Soon to be 20 cents with its increase. Cents per capita 7.5 6. 2

Looked at in another way, to cite again the example of Sweden, its contribution to UNICEF represents 2.2% of its foreign aid budget of $65 million. The corresponding percentage for the United States is almost too small to report: 4/10 of 1%. 

3. There is an inevitable competition for limited resources among various programs. During these years when the United States contribution to UNICEF has remained at the same figure, our contributions to other international organizations have increased. This is not to suggest that we are opposed to the accomplishments under the other programs, nor to deny that they also indirectly perhaps benefit children in the long run. It is merely to point out the stark fact that the world's most precious resource, its children, could use a larger share of the present investments. 

4. In his AID message, President Johnson has stressed his conviction of the importance of channeling more foreign aid through multilateral sources and his recommendation that the foreign aid programs help those nations that help themselves. UNICEF is an organization to which these objectives could appropriately be applied. Increased aid from the United States is directed to the international lending agencies such as IDA and the World Bank, but not to the Children's Fund. UNICEF aid, from its beginning, has required a country to match in goods and services the international aid it receives. One could say that UNICEF has been a pioneer in its emphasis on self-help, which the President so rightly approves.

In conclusion, we should like to refer again to the point made in our testimony last year: the fact that there is a vast amount of aid to children that cannot even be planned until the resources to carry out the programs appear to be within reach. The figure mentioned last year was $9 million. The need still exists for these additional resources. A top UNICEF official has said that UNICEF could usefully allocate at least $50 million per year instead of the approximately $35 million now going into its current work. One of the ironies of this situation is that while UNICEF helps millions of mothers and children each year, it does so by spending less in a whole year than the governments of the world spend on armaments in two hours of a single day. In a speech on the occasion of the awarding of the Nobel Peace Prize, the former Chairman of the UNICEF Board suggested "that each abortive disarmament conference set itself a penalty-a contribution to UNICEF of the equivalent cost of one submarine or a dozen fighter planes. Surely today's children are the central factor in the strategy for peace and survival."

The following national organizations have authorized me to present the foregoing statement in support of the contribution of a $12 million (as the regular U.S. contribution) and in addition a special contribution of $1 million by the

Y. W. C. A., National Board

Mrs. GRAY. Two of our national organizations that ordinarily join in supporting this statement have not had a chance to reply because of the short notice that we have had for this hearing. May I ask that 

I add those names as supporting organizations if that is received before the hearings are printed?

Mr. FASCELL. Without objection we will do that, Mrs. Gray. If they get here before the record is closed, we will include them. 

Mrs. GRAY. Thank you very much. These organizations which have authorized me to present this statement are listed on the last page of my testimony. They have authorized me to support the contribution of $12 million by the United States for fiscal 1967 which is the amount proposed by the executive branch and, in addition, they have asked me to request a special $1 million contribution in this year for the reasons and in the manner indicated later in the statement. 

This year, on December 11, UNICEF will celebrate its 20th birthday, which marks really a generation of service to children. This simple humanitarian undertaking really marked a new dimension in the development of civilization. It was a recognition of mankind's united responsibility for all children wherever they may happen to be born.

From its relatively small beginning in which several members of this committee, both past and present, played a major role, the program to aid the world's children has brought lifesaving aid to millions of children and mothers. In 1965 UNICEF was assisting 543 projects in 118 countries.

Statistics are often misleading and always rather boring, I am afraid; so I will not attempt to enumerate all of the statistics that are listed here, nor that we could list if the committee had time or the inclination to listen, about the number of cases of children who have been helped around the world. It really is a remarkable total. It goes to well over 50 million children.

In the last year also UNICEF was awarded Nobel Peace Prize for its contribution to world peace. This award was received by officials of UNICEF as an indication that the policies which UNICEF has pursued over the years are basically correct; and even more important, it was accepted by UNICEF officials as a challenge to strengthen their activities for greater fulfillment of children's needs in the future. 

The unanimous acclaim with which the announcement of the award was greeted did not increase the U.S. contribution to UNICEF, however. This fiscal year will mark the eighth consecutive year that the executive branch is recommending a contribution of $12 million to UNICEF by the United States. The organizations listed below endorse this recommendation with a full understanding and sympathy for the State Department's position in this matter. The Department's expert knowledge of the pledges already made or anticipated from governments other than the United States had led them to accept the strong probability that UNICEF will find it difficult to raise the necessary sums to match $12 million from the United States at 40 percent during the present year. 

Perhaps I should correct that to say to match an increase over the $12 million in the current year. The general trend, however, in 

Government contributions, is upward. A number of countries are increasing their contributions. Sweden is the most spectacular example with its 50-percent increase which has been pledged for 1966. Other countries are attempting to increase their contributions. 

Our organizations request this committee and the Congress to make, in this year of the Nobel Peace Prize Award, a special contribution

1 of an additional $1 million as tangible recognition for the outstanding work that UNICEF is doing for world peace. This $1 million contribution would be in every sense a gift, with no strings attached. 

It would be exempt from the computations of the 40 (United States) to 60 (other countries) matching ratio. We respectfully urge your support of this proposal.

We feel it would be an important way of demonstrating U.S. approval of the acclaim which UNICEF has so justly earned. There are a number of reasons why such an increase seems justified. Without doubt, the most compelling reason for giving aid is the enormous need of children. Many basic threats to child development have scarcely been recognized, let alone attacked. In addition to the present ongoing programs that we have discussed in past years, there is an area of child needs that is only beginning to be seen. This concerns the problems of the very young child, the child aged 1 to 6.

We know that there is much work that can't be done because resources are limited, but we feel much more should be done. 

It is estimated that there are 400 million children in the world, aged 1 to 6. Of these, 200 million live in the developing countries which are being assisted by UNICEF. These children, weaned from their mother's milk but too young to go to school, frequently suffer from grave malnutrition in these most formative years of their whole life. 

Recent research has developed evidence that many abilities, motor skills, even intelligence, may be affected by nutritional deficiencies during these early years, defects that no amount of future treatment can overcome. Here, then, is one area where much remains to be done. 

May I make a personal observation at this point in my testimony, Mr. Chairman?

Since appearing before this committee before, I have had the privilege of visiting some of the UNICEF work-I might add at my own expense, and I have visited, I have seen the shanty towns on the edge of Istanbul, some huts leaning up against the ancient Great Wall of Istanbul, which is so famous in history, shanty towns where the huts are built of flattened out pieces of tin, where one wonders if the slightest tremor would knock the whole thing down.

Yet these are places where families with their children are coming into the city, attracted by the supposed lure of job opportunities, where there is hope for the parents to make a better life for their children. 

Yet these pitiful conditions are not a place where it is safe for the children to be. Water is not available. They are crowded beyond our imagination; even in the poorer sections of this country I have never seen anything like this.

Also I have seen the children and their mothers in the clinics in Tunisia where they are being taken for care. I have seen the hope and the fear in the eyes of the mothers and the children as the child is examined by a pediatrician that is provided by the cooperation of World Health, Government, UNICEF and the supporting organizations. 

This is something that can't be put into statistics, but one sees the hope of these people for their children as they bring their children to the clinic, often waiting for hours to have their turn to have their child examined by the doctor.

I have talked with the doctors, the pediatricians in these clinics who have made impassioned pleas, unfortunately to me, because I was a

private citizen and I had nothing I could give them in return, except my sympathy, but there were pleas for increased laboratory equipment, equipment with which they could expedite their tests of children, which were not available then, but which they hoped could be added.

There is so much that needs to be done. 

A second reason for our feeling that the United States could justifiably increase its contribution is the fact that the United States is not, contrary to much popular thought, the largest contributor to UNICEF in per capita figures, even though we are in the gross amount, as such. 

The United States ranks only fifth, per capita. We rank after 

Sweden, after Norway, New Zealand, and Switzerland.

If we look at it in another way, the United States percentage of the contribution to UNICEF as expressed as a part of the economic budget which this committee is considering for foreign aid is only four-tenths of 1 percent. Certainly that is a very small amount to give to children.

Of course, there is an inevitable competition for limited resources among various programs.

During these years when the United States contribution to UNICEF has remained at the same figure, our contributions to other international organizations have increased. This is not to suggest that we are opposed to the accomplishments under the other programs, nor to deny that they also indirectly, perhaps benefit children in the long run. It is merely to point out the stark fact that the world's most precious resource, its children, could use a larger share of the present investments.

In conclusion, we should like to refer again to the point made in our testimony last year-the fact that there is a vast amount of aid to children that cannot even be planned until the resources to carry out the programs appear to be within reach. The figure mentioned last year was $9 million. The need still exists for these additional resources.

A top UNICEF official has said that UNICEF could usefully allocate at least $50 million per year instead of the approximately $35 million now going into its current work. One of the ironies of this situation is that while UNICEF helps millions of mothers and children each year, it does so by spending less in a whole year than the governments of the world spend on armaments in 2 hours of a single day. And an even more dramatic figure might be the comparison of the fact that the U.S. contribution at present of $12 million is less than the world spends in 1 hour of a single day for armaments. 

In a speech on the occasion of the awarding of the Nobel Peace 

Prize, the former Chairman of the UNICEF Board suggested that each abortive disarmament conference set itself a penalty-a contribution to UNICEF of the equivalent cost of one submarine or a dozen fighter planes. 

Surely today's children are the central factor in the strategy for peace and survival. 

Thank you.

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

I should like to welcome Mrs. Gray back to the committee. It is always a pleasure to hear her testimony.

I would like to begin by asking whether you have read this morning's 

New York Times, Mrs. Gray. I assume that perhaps you haven't had a chance. There is a front-page story which may be of interest with respect to the Shah of Iran's outright gift of approximately $700,000 to UNESCO as a contribution from Iran to the fight on illiteracy. 

The newspaper article indicates that the assessment of Iran to UNESCO comes to only about $23,000, but the Shah suggests that this figure of $700,000 is the equivalent of 1 day's military budget for his country. He feels if other countries would make a similar gesture there might be substantial amounts raised. 

Whether it is true or not I don't know, but the article points out that the United States is estimated to be spending about $165 million. a day for military purposes, which would be over twice the entire budget of UNESCO. So perhaps we better take this suggestion with reservations.

However, this does seem to be in line with your suggestion that we should make a voluntary contribution to another international agency, and my question really revolves around the desirability of this approach. 

Let me say to you that I am thoroughly sympathetic to what UNI CEF is doing, and I feel we certainly should be generous in this area. 

But your suggestion is not that we make a gift with strings attached? 

There would be no necessity for others to contribute in addition to what we contribute? As I understand it, you do not want to have a 

40-to-60 ratio, which would mean that we would only provide this additional aid if other countries came up with 60 percent more? 

Mrs. GRAY. That is correct, Mr. Frelinghuysen, for several reasons.

It is primarily a matter of accounting. Of course, other countries are increasing their aid, their contributions to the central account.

The 40 to 60 matching ratio applies to the central account of UNICEF. 

Therefore, contributions by national committees or other organizations within the country, voluntary private contributions, are not included at all. The best estimates which UNICEF has been able to prepare do not indicate that UNICEF can do better than match 12 million, possibly a little bit more, but not very much more, because of various problems.

Some countries are eager to increase their contributions but they have exchange difficulties which reduce the dollar value of the contribution. It just looked as though this would be one way in this particular year to make possible an increase in a gift to UNICEF that we hope would be an inspiration, an example of our leadership.

I believe that gifts should be gifts, and not have strings attached in any case. But we are not fighting that 60 to 40 matching ratio any more. We have accepted it and have agreed with the State 

Department's position that it has been a stimulant to other countries to increase their matching.

The problem is, I think, more in the area where there are so many demands on the country for funds for matching. Our bilateral aid program requires matching. Our other multilateral programs require matching. The country, no matter how eager it is to help itself, has very severe limitations on this. 

Loans of course that are repayable, loans that are investments for future building, that will yield revenue, we can see the way those can be repaid. But it is very difficult to compute in terms of a child exactly what an investment in a child is going to yield. 

I know in our school aid programs we have had many citations of figures that people with a high school diploma will earn so many thousands of dollars more in their lifetime than one who drops out. 

You can't be precise about this investment in children. I think we all know that the investment in children is one of the most important investments that can be made, not only in humanitarian terms, but in financial returns as well, even if we can't measure it precisely. 

Another point that I think the committee might be interested in knowing is that the Norwegian people who are so enthusiastic about the announcement of the Nobel Prize award immediately started raising money to match what the Nobel Prize amount is estimated to be. It is approximately $54,500. The people of Norway have now matched that as another voluntary gift to UNICEF as a way of showing their approval and their support and interest. 

Mr. FRELINGHUYSEN. As a practical matter, of course, there is a very real problem with respect to the differences of view between the so-called have-not nations and the have nations in international agencies. There is an understandable reluctance on the part of some of the have countries to approve budgets that constantly increase, which impose a major burden on them and not on the have-not countries. 

The have-not countries, without an equivalent burden, feel the necessity to expand more rapidly than do the have countries. I would suppose that the only way really to develop a common responsibility is through the matching approach. I would guess that if vou utilize the voluntary route too much you would have a destruction of the feeling that everybody must at least participate to some extent.

I would suppose, generally speaking, if we use the multilateral approach to reach children or to help illiterates, that we ought to try to do it in such a way as to develop an awareness and responsibility on the part of the other countries. 

Mrs. GRAY. I agree with your views, Mr. Frelinghuysen. I do not consider this in any sense an attempt to establish a precedent, to break away from the general program of matching. It is just that matching more than $12 million this year looks impossible. It looked impossible last year. We did not ask for it last year, but this year our organizations in fact have been pressing me to express their view in this matter.

(The information follows:) 

The following information, requested by Mr. Fulton at the Hearing on May 3, is taken from an official document of the United Nations Economic and Social 

Council (E/ICEF/542) dated 9 April 66 and entitled "General Progress Report of the Executive Director" of UNICEF.

CURRENT PROBLEMS NEEDING MORE UNICEF ASSISTANCE 

Although the full range of needs is far from known, countries are seeking more UNICEF co-operation at the present time in regard to the following problems affecting children and youth: a) Youth. In developing countries many children have to seek work before the normal age for completion of primary school. Much more extensive vocational and para-vocational training for industrial work is needed both in school and out of school, and more UNICEF and ILO help is required. 

Systems of rural primary education need to be established, in co-operation with UNESCO and FAO, with a curriculum that includes preparation for the transformation of traditional agriculture. The prevention of delinquency, and minor's courts would benefit from more assistance. b) School-age children. More assistance is needed, along with the technical advice of UNESCO, for teacher training; and for the adaptation of the curriculum to help integrate children into the economic development of their country, including such aspects as the use of one's hands, health and nutrition education, the education of girls, etc. Ways need to be found and demonstrated to help parents develop realistic expectations about the benefits of schooling; as the proportion of children going to school increases, parents have to understand that only a limited number of school leavers can find white-collar work. Countries need help in meeting the material needs of children in schools, for a water supply, school meals, school health services and reading glasses, and assistance with decent clothing for a proportion of pupils. The large numbers of children who are out of school, either never having attended, or having dropped out too early to retain literacy, will be a large problem for the next decades and need some literacy training, some vocational training working hours, and some chance for gifted children to rejoin the educational system. In general, not enough means are yet available to avoid losing the talents of gifted children. c) Infants and young children are the hardest to help. Their needs tend to be approached purely from a humanitarian view-in fact incorrectly so, because much of the basis for the child's subsequent development is laid during these years. Assistance is needed, along with the technical services of WHO, to develop the infrastructure of rural health services, especially at their growing point, MCH services. A rough estimation is that not more than 

5 percent of young children past infancy are reached by these services. Even to double this percentage would require a large effort. Wide-spread undernutrition and malnutrition require mothers to be better informed, and food supplies to be increased, especially of protein and protective foods, and manufactured weaning foods. The lack of simple pre-school services is one of the reasons for children not adapting to school, and dropping out. d) Children in shanty-towns. The needs of young children are particularly acute in shanty-towns where many working mothers lack the means of having their children cared for during the day, and where the housing, material and social conditions are the worst imaginable for the formation of a new generation of city workers. Assistance is required, along with the services of the Department of Economic and Social Affairs of the United Nations Secretariat, to mobilize public and community resources. e) The rural child. Countries are seeking means to provide better services for rural children, as one means of slowing down the excessive movement to cities in advance of employment opportunities, and of teachning the skills required for producing more food. f) Handicapped children. In countries where only a small proportion of children in the upper-income brackets have the possibility to develop "normally", the vast mass of underprivileged children get priority over the specially handicapped. Nevertheless, more UNICEF help could usefully be given to national centres for finding the most appropriate methods of work in this field and for training personnel. g) Development areas. At the other end of the scale from the shantytowns, there are "development areas" where it is possible and desirable to make a new start with relatively good services for children. They include areas where irrigation is being introduced, land-reform areas, areas of new settlement after malaria eradication, and new industrial towns. Their planning has in common an assumption that people will change their habits

of life, and that the work force will deliver a higher-than-average level of skill. Hence there is a particular interest to develop the capacities of the rising generation, and this may be facilitated where the area is administered by a single special authority. h) "Special assistance." The Executive Board has taken note of the situation of the number of newly independent countries which do not have the personnel and resources to mount the basic structure of the health and education services on which any children's programme depends. Special assistance could be given to these countries to set up and operate an infrastructure for services in a few demonstration districts, and would be maintained over a period of years with the country gradually taking over all the expenses. Co-operation in this field would be greatly helped if bilateral aid could be given to the support of such projects. Training In all the above problems, one of UNICEF's greatest potential contributions is to assist with the costs of training of national personnel, not in the form of fellowships tenable abroad which are mostly furnished by other agencies, but through help to build up national and regional training facilities. It represents one of the greatest fields of need for additional funds. 

The following table gives a financial summary showing how $50 million might be used for the support of programmes UNICEF is already assisting, as modified to take account of the problems discussed above.

STATEMENT OF MRS. THOMAS KOUZES, MEMBER, NATIONAL PTA LEGISLATIVE ACTION COMMITTEE, NATIONAL CONGRESS OF PARENTS & TEACHERS Mrs. KOUZES. Mr. Chairman and members of the committee, I am 

Mrs. Thomas Kouzes, member of the Legislative Action Committee of the National Congress of Parents & Teachers (National PTA). I have been authorized to reaffirm our established policy of support for UNICEF (United Nations Children's Fund), and particularly to offer support of the recommended appropriation of $12 million as the U.S. 

Government's contribution to the UNICEF program for fiscal 1967, and the requested special contribution of $1 million additional. 

Since the inception of UNICEF in December 1946 to provide emergency postwar relief to needy children, the National Congress of Parents & Teachers has consistently supported this program. 

Our organization comprises approximately 12 million memberships in 50 States, in the District of Columbia, and among schools for American dependents overseas. It has been dedicated since its beginning, in 1897, to health, education, and welfare of American children. It has always been in the forefront in encouraging the development of UNICEF into a permanent organization concerned with the health and welfare of all children in the world. 

From UNICEF's small beginnings in 1946, the organization has brought lifesaving aid to millions of children and mothers. Today the first babies helped by UNICEF are celebrating their 20th year, as is UNICEF. This new generation of healthy young adults are stepping into the adult world because mankind 20 years ago marked a new

dimension in the development of civilization, when it recognized man's united responsibility for all children, everywhere. 

In these years of service, UNICEF has treated 14 million cases of trachoma, more than 2 million cases of leprosy, and 40 million cases of yaws; and has given 193 million vaccinations for TB. UNICEF has further established and equipped more than 30,000 maternal and child health centers and subcenters, and has provided health scholarships and training allotments to thousands of nurses and auxiliary health and social workers. The consequence of this care is incalculable. 

These children are the potential leaders of tomorrow's world, who will work alongside our children as adults in an increasingly interdependent world.

The work of UNICEF is indeed spectacular within the limitations of its budget; a dramatic tribute to these accomplishments was the award of the Nobel Peace Prize in 1965. This is but the second time that this distinguished award has gone to a United Nations organization, and only the eighth time to any organization. The award was accepted by the officials of UNICEF as an indication that its guiding policies are basically correct, and that the award is a challenge to strengthen its activities for the fulfillment of all children's needs. 

The UNICEF program is achieveing notable success among the participating countries in the best traditions of self-help. In recent years the average contribution by each country aided was $2.70 in services, materials, and funds for every dollar of UNICEF money spent.

Because of insufficient funds, however, some $9 million worth of UNICEF projects could not be considered last year, and it would seem that a considerable increase in U.S. Government contributions would be justified, particularly because of the population explosion and the dramatic impact for good of UNICEF.

For the past 7 years, however, our Government has recommended that our contribution be $12 million on the basis of the State Department's expert knowledge that pledges already made or anticipated from other governments would indicate difficulty in raising the necessary sums to match more than $12 million at 40 percent. This also obtains for the present year. Despite the spectacular exception of 

Sweden, which has increased its contribution by 50 percent, few others have followed this lead. The general upward trend in support does continue, however.

The United States is the largest contributor in dollar total, but not at all the largest contributor per capita. We rank, in fact, fifth; with Sweden first, with 13 cents per capita, which will soon be 20 cents; Norway, 12 cents; New Zealand, 7.9 cents; Switzerland, 7.5 cents; United States, 6.2 cents, or four-tenths of 1 percent of our foreign aid budget.

President Johnson, in his AID message, stressed his conviction that more foreign aid should go through multilateral sources and to those who helped themselves. UNICEF could be described as a pioneer in its emphasis on self-help, which has always been a requirement for aid in this program. 

We would, therefore, strongly support the request of the Citizens Committee for UNICEF that this committee approve and that the

Congress authorize a special contribution of an additional $1 million in this year of the Nobel Peace Prize, as a tangible recognition of the outstanding work of this organization for peace. This $1 million should be in every sense a gift with no strings attached and no requirements for matching.

We would suggest that such a contribution will express our hope for a peaceful world when an organization like UNICEF helps millions of mothers and children each year with less money than the governments of the world spend on armaments in 2 hours of a single day.

We hope very much these messages may be taken to strengthen UNICEF as it extends its invaluable assistance to millions of children and youth to prepare them for useful lives in this rapidly changing world. 

Thank you very much.

STATEMENT OF LARRY BLACKMON, PRESIDENT, NATIONAL ASSOCIATION OF HOME BUILDERS 

Mr. BLACKMON. Thank you, Mr. Chairman and distinguished members of the committee.
