(The prepared statement of Mr. Blackmon is as follows:) STATEMENT OF LARRY BLACKMON, PRESIDENT, NATIONAL ASSOCIATION OF HOME BUILDERS I am Larry Blackmon, President of the National Association of Home Builders. 

I am a builder of homes and apartments in the Fort Worth, Texas area and in other cities in the United States. 

With me here today are Dr. Nathaniel H. Rogg, executive vice president of the

National Association of Home Builders, and Edmund N. Wise, counsel for inter national housing of the NAHB.

The National Association of Home Builders is the trade association of the home building industry, with more than 45,000 members organized in 396 local and State affiliated associations in all 50 States, in Puerto Rico, the Virgin Islands, and the District of Columbia.

Many of our members have participated in international housing programs, and many more are interested in participating in the future. It is our belief that the wide experience and research that both our Association and our industry members have developed over the years can be of considerable assistance in the development of more and improved housing in the less developed areas of the world.

In previous years, we have submitted our views on legislation similar to that which you are presently considering. Our interest in the bill presently pending before your Committee relates specifically to the continuation of the authorization for investment guarantees for housing in Latin America under Section 224

and the use of the authorization under Section 221(b) (2) for housing guarantees in other less developed areas of the world. The specific sections of the bill to which my comments this morning are addressed are contained in Title III, Section 104 (a) (2) beginning on line 23, page 3 of the Committee Print, and Section 

104 (b) beginning on line 3, page 4.

The National Association of Home Builders has had an interest in this program from its inception in 1961 and believes that the program offers the best opportunity for American private business to participate in the development of private housing in Latin America and elsewhere. 

There has traditionally been considerable misunderstanding about the role of housing in economic development. In recent years it has become generally recognized that housing is an important means of raising the productivity of the labor force which, in turn, is an essential factor in the economic development of an underdeveloped country. No longer is private home ownership viewed as merely a reward resulting from the effective economic development of a country, but rather it is considered a basic ingredient in a successful economic development program. Further, it is well known that the private ownership of property is a significant stabilizing factor in less developed countries. As a consequence, meaningful programs to construct housing can do much to promote democratic change. This program, as conceived by the Congress, and developed over the past years has made rapid strides in this direction. 

I. USE OF HOUSING GUARANTEE AUTHORITY Section 221 (b) (2) of the Foreign Assistance Act provides for guarantees of housing projects throughout the less developed countries of the world. Section 

224 specifically provides for guarantee projects in Latin America. The program was initiated in Congress for Latin America in 1961, and later expanded to the other areas. Each year increasing authorization was approved for these deserving and worthwhile programs and the programs expanded each year until 1965. 

Despite the directions of Congress to implement the programs during 1965, the Agency for International Development (AID) processed no projects beyond the amount developed during 1964. It is for this reason that we appear this year to request Congress to forcefully direct AID to carry out the housing guarantee programs in the manner and amount approved by the Congress in 1965 and ignored by AID this past year. 

A. Section 224-Latin America 

The housing guarantee program in Latin America was initiated in Congress in 1961 to be patterned after the FHA program in the United States. Congress has committed increasing support to the program: increasing authorization from $10 million to $60 million in 1962; in 1963, from $60 million to $150 million; and in 1964, from $150 million to $250 million. While the program was slow getting started, the Congress has provided strong support for its effective growth and expansion.

At this time there are over $90 million of projects under contract for close to 17,000 housing units. Another $75 million has been committed for 12,000 units and $75 million more for 15,000 units is being reviewed. This total of $240 million is slightly under the $250 million authorized by Congress in 1964. 

Despite the fact that Congress increased the authorization in 1965 by $150 million AID did not use any of the new authority. In fact, its policy controlling processing projects under Section 224 (b) (1)-the private demonstration housing projects-is that any project not consummated will not be replaced by a new worthwhile project. The AID limit now is $240 million and could, conceivably, go lower, but never reach the $250 million Congress authorized. 

In the face of the purpose of Section 224 and the experience under the program, the administration in 1965 introduced a bill which would introduce four new types of programs and eliminate the private demonstration housing program. 

The Foreign Affairs Committee, while agreeing to introduce these new types of programs, also insisted on the continuance of the private demonstration housing program. The Committee made its position clear in its report in which it stated: "The principal purpose of these amendments to Section 224 of the Act is to assure that the focus of the guarantee program under that Section will not only continue to provide support for private housing projects demonstrating U.S. techniques for building, financing, and marketing privately developed housing,

but will also stimulate development in Latin America of institutions similar to those which in the United States have so successfully mobilized private capital for the financing of new homes and other types of institutions important to the success of the Alliance for Progress. "The administration requested an increase of $100 million in the ceiling of the face amount of guarantees that may be outstanding at any one time. No proposal was made to the committee by the executive branch to continue support for selfliquidating pilot demonstration housing projects beyond the $250 million guarantee issuing ceiling that was already provided in the Act. Despite the AID's testimony that it no longer intended or desired to continue the self-liquidating pilot or demonstration housing projects, the committee specifically provides continuing authority for supporting this program, which is now being carried on so successfully. The committee also provides an additional $200 million of guarantee issuing authority so as to assure the availability of adequate issuing authority to cover the pilot or demonstration housing projects." [Emphasis added.]

The Committee increased the guarantee authority for housing under Section 224 to $450 million, of which a portion was to have been used for the original purposes of the Act, for the demonstration housing projects. Although the Administration had requested an increase of $100 million, Congress increased this to $150 million to make the $450 million total. The Conference Committee agreed with the position of the Foreign Affairs Committee that the original housing guarantee program should be continued. The Conference report specifically stated that the other programs should not have priority over the private participation in the development of demonstration housing projects in Latin America. 

Despite specific Congressional mandate to continue the private project program of Section 224(b) (1), AID not only did not use any of the authority during 1965, but recently announced policies specifically excluding the private demonstration housing program contained in Section 224(b)(1): 

The AID housing guarantee program had been aimed at providing moderately priced housing for those individuals with some source of steady income who could afford monthly payments on a house. It is in this middle income area that the private American home builder can best participate. 

Whereas, it is right and proper for the U.S. Government to use that amount of loan funds it believes feasible for assisting low cost housing, the role of assisting the middle class in achieving home ownership can be met by the private sector; the existing program has assisted in this endeavor. It has not only permitted American home builders to assist in building demonstration housing projects in Latin America but has encouraged these same builders to continue on beyond the guarantee program to participate and contribute to the expansion and development of the private home building industry in Latin America.

This Committee in its report of 1962 summarized this position very well. 

The report stated: 

"Measures to raise housing standards of middle and lower-middle income families must be undertaken through the stimulation of private initiative and private investment. The plight of families comprising this segment of the population is as serious as that of low income families. To solve the problem of those whose income is adequate to warrant homeownership without government subsidy, it is essential that sources of mortgage credit be developed. 

Until such time as a reservoir of savings can be accumulated internally, longterm capital must be attracted from institutional investors in the United 

"The Committee believes the housing guarantee program can be utilized to accomplish the objectives of the Alliance for Progress. Instead of governmentto-government loans and grants, as are used for low-cost housing, private investment sources can be relied upon to assume that part of the responsibility which properly belongs in the private sector." 

Time and again, Latin American officials and other Latin Americans interested in this program have pointed out the great error in neglecting those in the growing middle income class and concentrating all efforts on only those in the low income level. The point has been stressed that the potential political and business leaders, those with education, ability and aspirations, emerge

from this growing middle class and if their needs cannot be met, a serious threat is posed to economic, political and social stability. 

In short, we need to provide effective assistance for the middle income group as well as the low income group, and private capital can, and should, be unleashed to help meet the staggering housing needs. 

The authority for the AID guarantee program is intended, in the language of the Act. ***** to facilitate and increase the participation of private enterprise in furthering the development of economic resources and productive capacities of less developed friendly countries and areas." 

The Latin American housing program was created "to stimulate private home ownership" in Latin America.

With the elimination of housing projects under Section 224 (b) (1), the American home builders are effectively eliminated from the program. Other than the worthwhile labor unions projects, the only participation of the American private sector would be lending of funds. 

As AID has not used any of the authorization of last year, we are not requesting any increase in authorization. However, it is requested that of the $150 million, $100 million should be set aside for the continuation of the private housing program under section 224 (b)(1) and that $50 million should be permitted to be utilized for experimental type programs. 

It is inconceivable that AID would ignore the specific directives of this Committee and the Congress, but this was the result of the AID policies con cerning Section 224. 

Recommendation 1 

To assure the continuation of the private demonstration housing projects under Section 224(b)(1), the Act be amended in Section 224(c), to state, after the figure "$400,000,000" "Provided, that $350,000,000 be used for the purposes of Section 224(b) (1).”

B. Section 221(b) (2)—Other Areas of World 

Housing guarantees, similar to those in Latin America under Section 224, have in a few instances been granted in other areas of the world. From the very inception of the Foreign Assistance Act of 1961, such guarantees were permitted. Prior to the end of 1963 there had been no housing guarantee activity outside of Latin America. However, in 1963, AID agreed in principle to use authority under Section 221(b) (2) for housing guarantees in areas other than Latin America; and, this Committee in 1964, requested AID to expedite promptly applications for such guarantees in other areas. 

AID administratively set aside $50 million for this purpose in 1964. In 

1965 the Committee recommended that there be set aside in Section 221(b) (2) of the Act, $150 million for fiscal year 1966 for housing guarantees, and, after Conference, $125 million was set aside. This was an increase over the $50 million that AID had administratively set aside for housing guarantees under the Section. 

It was expected, since the authorization was for fiscal year 1966, that projects would be reviewed by AID for this amount. However, AID determined that it would utilize only the $50 million of the authority administratively set aside in 1964 and that this limit would be extended through fiscal year 1967. This would mean that through 1967 only $50 million of projects could be approved throughout the rest of the world other than Latin America, the same amount that had been set aside in 1964. As the Congress authorized up to $125 million for 

1966, it would appear reasonable that AID would, at the very least, permit the commitment for feasible projects for the amount of $125 million during 1967. 

To date, since the inception of the program outside of Latin America, only five projects for $23 million have been authorized in the African region, two projects for $10 million in the Far East and no projects have been authorized in the Near East-South Asia region. Considering the directions of the Committee last year, it is incredible how AID did not move forward at all. And in one area, Near East-South Asia, AID did not even accept application. 

Even as AID expresses concern over insufficient funds, a program using private funds not government funds-is being frustrated. The authority exists to guarantee private investment in new housing developments in the less developed countries.

Recommendation 2 

As a result of AID not expediting the use of the $250 million in the Latin American program prior to 1965, and the failure to use any of the authorization added to Section 221 and Section 224 by the Congress in 1965, the Act should be amended, in section 221(e), to state. "It is the sense of Congress that the authority provided in Section 224(b)(1) and Section 221 (b) (2) for housing projects be utilized as rapidly as is consistent with responsible administration.”

II. INTEREST RATES 

The guarantee program has had constant problems due to the Treasury constantly establishing interest rates for the program below a figure that is marketable for U.S. long-term investors. It is an accepted fact that interest rates must be high enough to be attractive to private lenders. However, the long-term U.S. lenders in the guarantee program have become disillusioned because they cannot rely on any consistent criteria for determining interest rates. 

It should be noted that even with a marketable interest rate and other charges, the cost to Latin American homeowners under the housing guarantee program are much more favorable than are otherwise available to these people. In addition, the interest rate return to the U.S. long-term lender is comparable to the return received on mortgages financing new housing in the United States. 

AID must receive Treasury approval for each change in interest rates and long delays have taken place from the moment that funds are not available at existing rates until the time the Treasury approves an increase in the rate. 

Projects have been delayed for as long as six months due to this fact. 

The rate of return for an investment in an AID housing guarantee project must be predicated on the then current money market and the interest rate for such guarantee must be based upon an acceptable standard. 

Recommendation 3

The constant time delays in setting new interest rates for housing guarantee projects can be eliminated by amending the Act at the end of Section 

221 (c), to state, "Provided that any loan investment for housing projects under Sections 221 (b)(2) and 224 shall be eligible for guarantee if the interest rate on the investment shall not exceed the then maximum permissible interest rate plus ½ of 1% on commitments issued by the Federal Housing Administration for insurance of mortgages on single family dwellings.” 

III. SALES PRICE CEILING 

Considering the purpose for which the program is designed and the fact that arbitrary limitations are not workable in dealing with individual Latin American countries, AID should revise its policy of placing the same arbitrary price limitation on housing units to be constructed for all countries of Latin America under the four new type experimental programs.

This policy is neither realistic nor feasible. An examination of housing prices in Latin America will readily show that comparable housing, for instance, in Caracas, Venezuela, is not the same as that, say, in Lima, Peru or 

Tegucigalpa, Honduras. 

Recommendation 4

Other than in Section 224(b)(1), AID be permitted to establish housing sales price limits in Latin America, but with limits dependent upon the particular conditions of each country.

IV. BUILDER QUALIFICATIONS 

AID has had certain difficulties during the first years of the program, such as many applicants were not experienced builders despite the fact that the program was designed to capture the experience and ability of the American home builder. For this reason, the NAHB believes that AID would be assisted by a requirement that U.S. participants in the guarantee program be individuals or groups that have been qualified to do business before the FHA or have comparable qualifications. This would also apply to any foreign builders that may participate in the program. In this manner AID would be assisted in receiv-

ing applications from those able to contribute the best of the American home building industry to Latin American countries. 

Recommendation 5

U.S. or foreign participants in the housing guarantee program shall be qualified to do business before the FHA or shall have comparable qualifications. 

V. ADMINISTRATION OF PROGRAM 

Increased efficiency in the program could result with more effective division of the authority between the operations of AID and the operations of FHA. 

With the steady increase in the size of the program, the FHA should be required to increase its efficiency as well as the AID. Duplication could be avoided by vesting in AID all foreign policy aspects concerning the program, such as determining eligible countries and establishing the limits on the amounts of guarantees permitted for each country. In addition, the U.S. 

Embassy in the particular country should approve each project. 

With this limitation in mind, FHA should be vested with the technical approval authority for all individual projects without the involvement of the many committees and other offices in AID, as well as the interdepartmental committees, such as the Development Loan Committee and the National Advisory Council. The specific risk guarantee program of AID has operated extremely well over the years without the requirement of individual project approval by various agency and interagency committees. It is suggested that 

AID could streamline procedures within strict policy limitations, and with the agreement on the method of establishing an interest rate, projects could be administered by the FIIA. The director of the responsible office in FHA could grant the project commitment in the same manner as a district office director of an FHA office in the United States and coordinate with only the Housing and Urban Development Division in Latin America and comparable offices in the other geographical regions. 

Recommendation 6

AID should furnish policies for the operation of the program to the FHA and once policies are determined, all technical aspects of the project should be considered by FHA, and that project approval, barring a foreign policy reason, should be approved by the director of the FHA project examination office.

VI. BALANCE OF PAYMENTS 

One of the stated reasons of the Administration regarding the change in the type of housing guarantee program was the balance-of-payments problem. The home building industry is very mindful of the balance-of-payments problem and credit problems in the United States. In fact, there is no industry that is affected more by the present fiscal problems in the United States than the home building industry.

However, as it is shown in the accompanying exhibit, the housing guarantee program under Section 224 (b) (I) contributes very little to this balance-ofpayments problem. At the end of the first three years of a housing project, a maximum of 12% of funds invested is a deficit, as is shown in the exhibit. 

This is the maximum point. In fact, after six years, there is a plus factor in the U.S. balance of payments rather than a deficit. 

From the beginning of the sixth year, the program is an enormous positive contributor to the U.S. balance-of-payments situation. Thus, in ten years it would have made a net contribution of almost $250 million to the U.S. balanceof-payments gap. At the time all the loans would have been repaid, the program would have contributed perhaps as much as $850 million, and certainly not less than $500 million to the credit side of the U.S. balance-of-payments ledger. 

The accompanying exhibit shows that the house guarantee program is the most positive factor of foreign assistance in contributing favorably to the U.S. balanceof-payments problem.

The exhibit also shows a comparison between the housing guarantee program and AID program loans. It is shown that the program loans after a five-year period create over 15 times the balance-of-payments drain as housing guarantees. 

And after five years, the housing guarantees show a favorable balance-AID program lending takes 15 years.

AID states it needs more funds for the foreign aid program. The housing guarantee program requires no appropriations, creates a more favorable impact on the balance of payments than AID loans, and at the same time permits the participation of U.S. private enterprise in building such needed housing in the less developed countries. 

We firmly support the suggestion made in the recent address in Mexico City on April 27 by the Secretary of Treasury, Mr. Henry Fowler, speaking to the annual meeting of the Inter-American Development Bank, in which he stressed the point that in Latin America there is a unique opportunity to make use of the U.S. Government's private investment guarantee program. The NAHB is offering the assistance of its member builders in carrying out this suggéstion; this offer is supported by the fact that the guarantee program has a much more favorable effect on the balance of payments than other types of direct U.S. 

Government loans.

It is inconceivable that when private enterprise tries to participate in a worthwhile foreign aid program that it receive such short-shrift. And it is more inconceivable when considering the fact that Congress last year specifically directed AID to include the private home building section in its housing guarantee program. AID has eliminated from its program those most professionally qualified, those who in 1965 built close to 70% of the housing units in the United States. It is for these reasons that we request that the Committee include specific legislative provisions thereby requiring AID to comply with the will of the Congress.

For these reasons and mindful of the fact that Congress last year authorized AID $150 million more in Section 224 and $125 million in Section 221, and that Congress directed specifically that AID continue the private housing guarantee program under Section 224(b)(1), we request the Congress to put into the Foreign Assistance Act language to implement its directions to AID which were ignored in 1965. (Exhibit to statement of National Association of Homebuilders on Foreign 

Assistance Act of 1966) THE HOUSING GUARANTEE PROGRAM AND THE UNITED STATES BALANCE OF PAYMENTS 

The Agency for International Development (AID) decision to use only a very limited portion of its housing guarantee authority is the direct result of the Treasury Department's concern over the impact of this program on the U.S. balance of payments. Prior to the intercession of the Treasury Department, AID contemplated a continuous and expanding Latin American housing guarantee program. Thus, for 1964, AID requested $100 million of additional authorization and a further request for the same amount was made in 1965. The 

Congress, in fact, authorized $150 million in 1965 for Latin America and set aside $125 million of authority for the rest of the world. Moreover, it was originally the executive branch position that this authorization be used as rapidly as projects could be processed. The present constriction and slowdown in the program results from the Treasury Department's misgivings about the program's effect on the U.S. balance-of-payments situation. 

The members of the National Association of Home Builders are also vitally concerned about U.S. balance-of-payments problems. They are prepared to cooperate in every way to improve this situation. It is submitted, however, that the impact of the housing guarantee program on the U.S. balance of payments has been grossly misunderstood and reflects a fundamental misconception on the part of the executive branch.

We believe it can be demonstrated that the housing guarantee program creates favorable balance of payments effect of the entire foreign aid program of the United States. We further suggest that on balance-of-payments grounds the program should be enlarged-not cut back and slowed down. 

The AID housing guarantee program guarantees the long term financing furnished by U.S. investors for housing in the less developed countries. It is analogous to the U.S. Federal Housing Administration program for housing in this country. For the purpose of analyzing the balance-of-payments effect of the program, the following assumptions are made, as a basis for the graphical presentation on page 7.* The basis for each assumption is indicated: *The analysis is based upon the Latin American program, but is also true to a lesser extent for housing guarantees in the rest of the world.

(1) A program of $100 million per year in Latin America is the minimum desirable operating level. We understand that this level of operation coincided with the AID long-range planning prior to the determination to cut-back the program. (2) The guaranteed loans would be for terms averaging 20 years and would not include grace periods. The interest rate is calculated at a 

52% return to the U.S. investor. These assumptions are based on current 

AID commitments. It is understood that the interest soon will increase to 

54% and, on this basis, the positive effect is increased accordingly. The debt service factor is the most positive component in the favorable balance of payments effect created by the housing guarantee program. (3) The $100 million per year would be disbursed equally over a fiveyear period starting in 1967. While this is somewhat of an oversimplification of the timing of disbursements, it does not distort the conclusions, (4) The funds disbursed by the U.S. lenders will be used to reimburse builders for the direct and indirect costs of construction, land acquisition, urbanization, and builder's profits. AID's own estimate is that in the aggregate not less than 20% of the total amount disbursed would be retained by U.S. builders as reimbursement for U.S. placement fees and profit. Stated in an alternative fashion, 20% of the total amount disbursed would represent U.S. dollar costs and would not represent any adverse effect on the balance of payments. (5) The annual guarantee fee paid to AID in dollars would average 12% of the principal amount of the outstanding loans. This assumption is based on AID's experience to date and, while some minor shift in fees may take place, the change would not significantly alter the conclusions. (6) Reserve fund payments by home owners would average 1% of the principal amount of loans outstanding and would be transferred in dollars to banks in the United States. This is based on AID experience to date and is a conservative figure, since in some countries the charge has been as high as 32%. (7) The countries involved in AID housing guarantee projects buy a substantial portion of their total imports in the United States, and return almost immediately to the United States over 60% of all dollars received for purchases of U.S. goods and services. These assumptions are based on discussions with AID officials and were used by AID itself in analyzing the balance-of-payments impact from AID program loans as well as housing guaranties. This 60% does not represent a balance-of-payments drain. The remaining 40%, of course, does.

This analysis of the effect on the balance-of-payment problem, as it relates to the housing guarantee program, is shown in the graphical presentation on page 7 and produces the following conclusions: 

1. The maximum adverse balance of payments occurs in the third year of the program and the maximum balance-of-payment deficit is $36 million. 

2. Beginning in the sixth year the program will create a substantial positive effect for U.S. balance-of-payments. In ten years a net contribution of $250 million will be made to the U.S. balance-of-payments. At the end of twenty years the U.S. balance of payments will show the contribution to the 

U.S. of $850 million by the housing guarantee program. 

3. This tremendous contribution to U.S. balance-of-payments position would be accomplished at no cost to the U.S. taxpayer under a soundly administered program. Fees collected for guarantees, as in the FHA program, would provide income to the U.S. Government during the five-year period of construction in the hypothetical analysis and over 150,000 housing units would be constructed in Latin America. 

It is worthwhile to compare balance-of-payments results from the housing guarantee program and those of AID's "program" loans. These loans provide dollars to the recipient country, which dollars may be spent for such purposes as the recipient desires. While AID frequently requires the borrower collect normal commercial import documentation to demonstrate that the dollars have been "spent" in the United States, this is, in AID's own terminology, a "cosmetic" device. It is designed to permit AID to state that program loans give rise to U.S. procurement.

What occurs, in fact, is that normal commercial documentation for goods which would have been imported in any event, are collected and the loan proceeds

attributed by AID to these imports. AID then considers that the loan was used for U.S. procurement. In fact, such loans give rise to no more (nor less) U.S. procurement than would an equivalent number of free dollars under the housing guarantee program.

It is acknowledged that "tied" procurement used with program loans in Latin America has no meaningful economic impact and that the dollars are freely available for such purposes as the recipient desires. AID Administrator Bell, in his presentation before the Senate Banking and Currency Committee on 

March 9, 1965, in discussing "tied" procurement, stated that: “The true net economic effect of foreign assistance on our balance of payments cannot be measured so simply *** under a tied loan *** the country may use the tied dollars to buy goods that it would have otherwise bought with dollars it already owns. This is the so-called 'substitution' effect, *** aid financed imports are substituted for imports that would have been bought with free dollars. To this extent, the effect of tied aid on the U.S. balance of payments is understated."

Program lending to Latin America is now at an annual level in excess of $250 million per year. However, for the sake of comparing the balance-of-payments effect of program lending with the projected housing guarantee program, we assume a figure of $100 million per year for five years. Program loans are normally made for a term of 40 years with a ten year grace period. The interest during the grace period is 1%, and is 2% thereafter.

The assumption by AID on the use of program loàn dollars by Latin American countries is the one that was used in the analysis of the housing guarantee program (i.e. 60% is normally returned to the United States for goods and services). 

At the end of five years the net adverse balance-of-payments impact from the assumed level of program lending is in the order of $190 million. For the comparable housing guarantee program the net loss at the end of five years is only $10 million-less than 6% of the adverse impact of the program loans! In slightly over five years, repayments under the housing guarantee program exceed the balance-of-payments drain. To achieve this equilibrium point with program lending it takes about 16 years. At the time program lending reaches the point where its net impact is just beginning to be favorable, the housing guarantee program has already returned over $600 million in net balance-of-payments gain to the United States!

Based on the foregoing analysis, it is submitted that the balance-of-payments objections to the housing guarantee program are completely without foundation. 

Indeed, the housing guarantee program has highly desirable U.S. balance-ofpayments effects. In fact, as seen, it creates a much better effect than AID program loans.

The executive branch has authority available without need of appropriations to operate this program. If the executive branch is really concerned about foreign aid and its impact on the balance of payments, and is forthright in its support of developing housing overseas through private enterprise, it will operate the housing guarantee program as directed by Congress in order to use the authorization legislated by Congress.

Mr. BLACKMON. I am Larry Blackmon, president of the National 

Association of Home Builders. I am a builder of homes and apartments of Fort Worth, Tex., area, and in other cities in the United 

States. 

With me here today, as the distinguished chairman indicated, I have 

Dr. Rogg, our executive vice president, and Ed Wise, who is counsel for International Housing of NAHB. 

Some of our members have participated in international housing programs and many more are interested in participating in the future. 

It is our belief that the wide experience and research that both our association and our industry members have developed over the years can be of considerable assistance in the development of more and improved housing in the less-developed areas of the world. 

The National Association of Home Builders has had a continuing interest in the AID housing guarantee program from its inception. 

Our interest in the legislation before your committee relates specifically to the authorization of investment guarantees for private housing projects in Latin America under section 224 (b) (1) and the use of similar authorization under section 221(b) (2) for the other lessdeveloped areas of the world. 

Despite the fact that AID has been asking Congress for increased appropriations for government foreign assistance lending, it has not utilized guarantee programs. This, in the face of the fact that in 

1965 Congress authorized $150 million to provide for more housing guarantee authority in Latin America and $125 million in authoriza tion for the rest of the world. AID did not use any of this authorization last year.

The Latin American program was the creation of Congress and offered promises of success in each year of its operation so that Congress increased its authorization from $10 million in 1961 to $400 million in 1965. The program has operated without using any government funds and without cost to the government. From its inception to the present the program has operated to guarantee privately owned housing projects constructed by U.S. and foreign homebuilders, patterned after the FHA program in the United States. 

Last year AID proposed four new programs to replace the private housing demonstration program. Despite the administration pro posal, the Foreign Relations Committee and the Foreign Affairs Com mittee directed the administration to continue the private demonstration housing program under section 224 (b) (1). The conference report specifically states that the other programs should not have priority over the private participation in development of demonstration housing projects in Latin America. 

This committee made clear its position in its report last year, when it stated:

That despite the AID's testimony that it no longer intended or desired to continue the self-liquidating pilot or demonstration housing projects, the committee specifically provides continuing authority for supporting this program, which is now being carried on so successfully. The committee also provides an additional $200 million of guarantee issuing authority so as to assure the availability of adequate issuing authority to cover the pilot or demonstration housing projects.

In the face of this congressional mandate, AID last month issued policies specifically excluding the private demonstration housing pro-

gram contained in section 224 (b) (1). This effectively eliminates private American home builders from participating in an FHA-type private home building program created by the Congress. 

It is inconceivable that AID would ignore the specific directives of this committee and the Congress but this is the result of the recently announced AID policies concerning section 224. 

Since the AID has ignored the intent of Congress in 1965, we request that the act be amended so that $100 million of the authorization of 1965 be committed for private demonstration housing projects under section 224 (b) (1) to make a total of $350 million for this program. 

On top of the fact that Congress provided additional authorization of $150 million in 1965 that has not been used by AID, the AID policy includes a restricton that any project not consummated under the $250 millon previously committed by AID for section 224 (b) (1) private demonstration housing projects will not be replaced by a new project. 

This means that AID will also not use the entire $250 million that was authorized by Congress.

Under section 221 (b) (2), housing projects, AID set aside $50 million in 1964 for guarantee of housing projects in other areas of the world. In 1964 this committee requested AID to expedite promptly applications for housing guarantees under section 221(b)(2). In 

1965 the committee set aside $150 million of authorization for the program, and after House-Senate conference, the figure $125 million was put into the act.

However, AID determined that it would only use $50 million set aside in 1964 and this authorization would be extended through 1967. 

At the very least the congressional authorization in 1965 of $125 million should be used in fiscal year 1967. 

As a result of AID ignoring the directives of Congress contained in its reports in 1965, and not expediting the use of all of the $250 million in the private demonstration housing program of Latin America, nor using any of the additional $150 million authorized by Congress for this program, and the fact that AID was determined not to use the full $125 million set aside in section 221 (b) (2) for housing projects, we request that the Congress amend the act to provide that the authorization already provided in these two sections be utilized as rapidly as is consistent with responsible administration. 

The NAHB believes there are other minor improvements that can be made in the program so that it will operate much more efficiently than it has in the past. We wish to focus on one of these points in particular as it has delayed the operations the most. 

The guarantee program has had constant problems due to the fact that interest rates are established below a figure that is marketable for U.S. long-term investors. It should be noted that even with a marketable interest rate and other charges, costs to Latin American homeowners under the housing guarantee program are much more favorable than are otherwise available to these people. Furthermore, the interest rate return to the U.S. long-term lender is lower than the return received on mortgages financing new housing in the United States.

Under the housing guarantee program, AID must receive Treasury approval for each change in interest rates and these delays have been 60-929-66--pt. 54

as great as 6 months. The rate of return for an AID housing guarantee investment must be based on the current money market and the rate of interest for these guarantees must have set guide lines. Therefore, we suggest that the act be amended to permit an interest rate equal to the current rate plus FHA mutual insurance fee. 

Our written statement contains several other technical aspects such as a more realistic sales price ceiling, builder qualifications, and more efficient administration of the program. We request that the committee examine these proposals in more detail, as contained in the written statement.

I wish to touch on one point that has been a stated reason by the administration for failure to implement the housing guarantee program. It is claimed by the administration that this failure is based on balance-of-payments problems. This reason is not valid. 

An exhibit accompanying our statement shows graphically that the housing guarantee program is an enormous positive contributor to the U.S. balance-of-payments situation. Assuming $100 million annual housing guarantee program for 5 years, in 10 years there will be a net contribution of almost $250 million, and in 20 years there will be a plus factor of $850 million. 

Not only that, but the housing guarantee program is a far better balance-of-payments investment than AID program loans. Over a 

5-year period, program loans create 15 times the balance-of-payments drain as housing guarantees and where the housing guarantees show a favorable balance after 5 years, AID program lending takes 15 years.

AID states that they need more funds appropriated for the foreign aid program. The housing guarantee program requires no appropriations, creates a more favorable impact on the balance of payments than AID loans, and at the same time, permits the participations of U.S. private enterprise in building much-needed housing in the lessdeveloped countries.

We firmly support the suggestion made in the recent address in 

Mexico City on April 27 by the Secretary of Treasury, Mr. Henry

Fowler, speaking to the annual meeting of the Inter-American Development Bank, in which he stressed the point that in Latin America there is a unique opportunity to make use of the U.S. Government's private investment guarantee program. The NAHB is offering the assistance of its member builders in carrying out this suggestion; this offer is supported by the fact that the guarantee program has a much more favorable effect on the balance of payments than other types of direct U.S. Government loans.

It is unbelievable that when private enterprise tries to participate in a worthwhile foreign aid program that it receives such treatment. 

And it is more inconceivable when considering the fact that Congress last year specifically directed AID to include the private homebuilding section in its housing guarantee program. AID has eliminated from its program those most professionally qualified, those who in 1965 built close to 70 percent of the housing units in the United States. 

It is for these reasons that we request that this committee include specific legislative provisions thereby requiring AID to comply with the will of the Congress. 

Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

Mr. FASCELL. Thank you, Mr. Blackmon. We appreciate your :statement in support of the investment guarantee program for housing. 

The association has a long history in helping to originate this program and supporting it.

You are correct, of course, in your statement that despite the congressional mandate, the Department has announced in testimony before this committee that the authorization which was provided for last year would not be used beyond the $250 million; and as a matter of fact that they would not use the full $250 million authorized. And furthermore that the housing investment guarantee authorization for other parts of the world, would not be used and that which had been authorized for fiscal year 1966 would be extended to fiscal 1967. 

We appreciate the suggestions you make for amendments. I inquired of the departmental witnesses when they testified about the idea of a flexibility for interest yield tied to FHA interest rates. They indicated they would support it. I don't find much comfort in that because if they are not going to use the authorization, what good is there to have a flexible interest yield. 

However they indicated they would support congressional enactment of the flexible interest yield provision. We will consider the other amendments carefully, such as builder qualifications. The re- :striction, the limitations or the nonuse of the authorization and the limitation of it to only new programs has been testified to by the Department was brought about as a result of recommendations of the President's committee on the balance of payments. I would like to inquire, Dr. Rogg, if you could give us the details on the summary analysis that Mr. Blackmon made on the balance-of-payments effect of the housing investment guarantee programs as compared with the AID program generally. What are the criteria which he used to make that comparison.

STATEMENT OF NATHANIEL H. ROGG, EXECUTIVE VICE PRESI- DENT, NATIONAL ASSOCIATION OF HOME BUILDERS 

Mr. RoGG. The comparison is made, Mr. Chairman, on the basis of some assumptions which AID has provided us with plus the return to the United States of the principal and interest of the money loaned under these guarantees.

Under AID's assumptions 20 percent of the dollars spent abroad will utilize American merchandise and there will be 20 percent of that spent in the United States for equipment and materials. In addition, on the basis of the amortization tables used in this program, it is figured within 5 years, allowing for a 20-percent return on materials, within 5 years the total dollar investment will have been returned to the 

United States. 

Mr. FASCELL. That is under the investment guarantee program?

Mr. ROGG. Yes. It takes 15 years on AID loan programs to see a comparable dollar return to the United States. We are using the amortization tables on the individual payments and the materials that go into the projects. There is a chart on the back of our testimony which supplies this particular information. 

Mr. FASCELL. Thank you for giving us that criteria. I would ask one other question. The argument has been made between economists,

classic and others as to the prior need for classical type of economic development in the underdeveloped countries rather than construction and housing. 

What is your opinion on that?

Mr. ROGG. That argument has been shot down so often that it often doesn't bear taking target on it again, but I would like to, if you don't mind. 

Mr. FASCELL. You have a few minutes.

Mr. ROGG. The arguments hold you don't get any return in a developing country from investing in housing and what you must do is put your money into productive factories and the like. It ignores completely the contribution that is made to a nation's welfare by providing for good housing for its inhabitants and what happens when you do that. It has been the subject of a number of conferences that have been held in this country and elsewhere. 

I think there has come about generally in economic circles a realization that the classical doctrine is really not applicable under these circumstances. The improvement in standards of living and what it does to the nation's productivity and stable governments is regarded as adequate economic reason, and I am ignoring some of the values of political contribution, for supporting programs of this kind in economic theory.

Mr. FASCELL. We have a case in point in our own country. 

Mr. RoGG. I think we do.

Mr. FASCELL. One other question, and that has to do with the success of the pilot demonstration program. As I conceived the idea there it was to demonstrate the desirability of home construction from a developmental standpoint and social standpoint. It was also to create an interest of local capital and labor and to demonstrate to them the most modern means, methods, skill, and talent that was available to mass produce housing.

From everything I have read, I assume this demonstration program has been successful in Latin America, has it not? 

Mr. BLACKMON. Yes, sir; it has. We would like to, I think, bring to the chairman and this committee the feeling that where American builders and builders of the particular nation join hands, that we feel that it makes a real partnership and the techniques that we have been able to develop over the years is passed on to them and they then continue to use them.

We think there is a lot of good that can come from this kind of relation with the local builders and the local financiers and suppliers of materials.

Mr. RoGG. We think, sir, it hasn't reached the extent which it can. in view of the limitations which have been placed on the program. 

Mr. FASCELL. You anticipated my next question. It has been stated many times that in political terms, social terms, and economic terms, Latin America is not a monolithic society but rather is made up of related but different independent-sovereign nations. To what extent has the pilot program been implemented in how many countries, if you know? And would it be useful if a pilot program was put in at least one place in each country? 

Mr. BLACKMON. Mr. Chairman, that we would heartily endorse and this is expanded in our testimony, what Congress has authorized

should be administered. To answer your question, since it was started in 1961 there have been 15 projects in which contracts have been signed, and the projects authorized and not under contract, there are 16. There are some 19 that are still not authorized. 

Mr. FASCELL. Do you have a list of countries in which those projects- 

Mr. BLACKMON. Yes, sir. 

Mr. FASCELL. You concur with the proposition that there ought to be at least one demonstration project in each country? 

Mr. BLACKMON. Yes. I think there should be--if the funds are available, there should be several because as you know some of these countries are rather large, and we have two or three demonstrations that I think would be very helpful. There has been some criticism as to maybe building a moderate-priced home, but I say to this committee that AID should look, or the State Department should look at depth at the people we are trying to house in these countries. 

These are people who have college degrees. They are professional people, but yet do not have adequate housing. I think this has a terribly demoralizing effect on these people that yet they are making money but they can't acquire decent and sanitary housing. They are the leaders of the future in these countries. 

I think there is a very definite need to house these people as well as there is some of our lower income people. So we need a variety of housing in a variety of locations. Since this is such a successful program and since it can mean so much to this country in fulfilling the obligations which President Kennedy and President Johnson have stated is our policy in these countries, it seems to me there should be no delay in getting on in doing the job, which we so many times have promised.

I realize there might be some concern as to the number of dollars that should be expended annually, but let's decide what that amount should be and then do it, rather than continue to study and delay. 

I have been in this program for some 4 years. It is a continual delay and frustration. If we are only going to do $100 million a year, let's do $100 million, but let's do it and let the people know we mean business down there.

Mr. FASCELL. Our investigation discloses that the average time to process an application is somewhere between 2 and 3 years, so I can understand your frustration. 

Mr. BLACKMON. I can assure you, you are correct on that, because of my own personal knowledge. 

Mr. FASCELL. Mr. Roybal. 

Mr. ROYBAL. Am I correct in assuming the demonstration housing program is directed at the needs of the emerging middle-income group in Latin America?

Mr. BLACKMON. It is two groups, for lower middle and middle income--I think you have to take the country and the conditions that exist. If I might explain that a little.

There is a particular country that I know of that AID loans the country itself money, direct loans; that particular country furnishes the land and the urbanization and builds the house and sells it to people on a 3 percent basis. They go up to $5,000. They do not have sufficient funds to cover all the housing that is needed in this category.

Certainly private enterprise, I think you would agree, could not compete with housing this particular group when they subsidize the land and urbanization and have a 3 percent interest. This is more in line with our interest rate on the 221 (d) (3) program in this country. 

Country by country you have to analyze what that particular country is doing in housing. 

Mr. ROYBAL. Has AID placed a ceiling- 

Mr. BLACKMON. They discourage any moderate housing. I am talking about loans of $7,000, $8,000, $10,000. 

Mr. ROYBAL. Would you say that $5,000 would be the highest that 

AID would approve?

Mr. BLACKMON. Under this new program AID has put guidelines into effect of $5,000 is all that they would approve. I say this is unrealistic because there needs to be flexibility based upon the country,. and what the cost of land is. In many of these countries a lot costs. $2,500 and that only leaves a limited amount in which to build your home. 

Mr. ROYBAL. When you make reference to low cost housing, are you talking about housing to be made available to people out in the slum areas in various parts of Latin America? 

Mr. BLACKMON. Yes, sir. As you know and I know, there is a large shortage of homes and many people are forced to live in poor conditions. They could afford better housing; they just do not have the financial vehicle in order to have long-term mortgages. Many of them have the money for down payments and a desire and will to build, and be in a better home and better living conditions, but long-term mortgage money is not available to provide this for them. 

Mr. ROYBAL. Is the National Association of Home Builders actually interested in providing homes for slum dwellers in Latin American countries? 

Mr. BLACKMON. Yes, sir, I would welcome the opportunity just as we do in this country.

Mr. ROYBAL. The rates you have to pay for money would have to be favorable and under certain favorable conditions you would be able to go into Latin America, that is your membership, and provide housing that these people could afford. If this were the case $5,000 would be more than these people could probably pay in a lifetime. 

What are you talking about? What is your suggestion with regard to the price category that a house could be built for people in slum areas in let's say Peru? 

Mr. BLACKMON. I think you have to look at the situation. Certainly a lot of people in those countries can not afford $2,000 or $5,000 houses just as in this country. I would have to say to you gentlemen that I have been involved for many years in working in undeveloped countries. I was involved with our government as an advisor on the economic recovery of Japan. I see many opportunities in these countries just as the economic recovery of Japan. 

There is a group of people that can now qualify for homes that should have homes. But as you put other people to work in building these homes and building furniture and equipment that goes into the homes, there are additional people that are then able to qualify for homes because of employment that they don't have now and because of the raising of the standard of living.

To me this is a concept which we should foster and try to encourage, that is, the economic increase of their productivity down there to where the people would be able to afford more products and better homes as their economy grows and expands. 

As you know the home building in this country has been a great factor in that and it would be a great factor in any one of those countries if given the opportunity. What we are saying is that there has been a lid put on what you gentlemen have enacted into law, to the point they are not administering it. We feel some steps should be taken if they won't abide by legislation, then maybe through legislation to get this accomplished, because it is important. 

I have been in several of the countries down there and we have made a lot of statements of how we were going to do things to bring about economic improvement. But yet there is very little action being taken. 

I think it is time that we do something about it. 

Mr. ROYBAL. That is all. 

Mr. FASCELL. Mr. Fulton. 

Mr. FULTON. I want to thank you for the statement. I believe this is an excellent program and we should continue it. It is the kind of a program that works with private industry first, and secondly it implements self-help locally in these countries. I believe it has worked well. 

I, for one, would be glad to be one of the Members who pushes the program and tries to make it work more efficiently. 

Mr. BLACKMON. I have been close to many of you gentlemen's lives and I have some very dear friends in your delegation, and I realize some of the problems that they too are confronted with. I wish to add my compliments to this committee for its strong support, in the past, of our views. We know that Congressmen Fascell, Roybal and 

Fulton have contributed much to the successful legislation in the past. 

Mr. FASCELL. We have a quorum call. We are going to have to leave now. I want to ask you one final question. Assume that there is a line of thinking that exists, which maintains that home construction beyond a pilot program should not be undertaken by the American. home builder, and that pilot program is all right, but after all once you plant the seed capital and the seed idea then it is time to go on home and leave the entire matter to the local people. 

I am just wondering about whether the association would support a guideline, an administrative guideline, which limited American home. builder's participation to joint ventures?

Mr. BLACKMON. Yes, sir. The demonstration program as I envision it was to get the program started. I see nothing wrong with a relationship that blooms out of this kind of operation, where you have a joint venture operation with American builders and the local builders in any particular country.

We will be happy to work and our services are available to try to assist any of these countries in furthering home ownership and home building. 

Mr. FASCELL. Mr. Blackmon, I want to thank you. I want to say the committee has expressed itself very forcefully on this subject. I am quick also to say that without the continued assistance, effort, and strong support in the administration by the National Association of Home Builders, I am not sure that we are going to move off dead center on this program.

So notwithstanding whatever action this committee may take this year with respect to the suggestions you have made, if the committee were to act favorably on them it will still require tremendous effort on your part. We recognize that and I, for one, appreciate your willingness to continue. 

Mr. FULTON. May I make a statement on that? The Republican side of this committee, unanimously, at this time says that without the adequate past support and the future support of the committee, Mr. Dante Fascell of Florida, first you would not have had the program probably, and secondly, it would have not have been sufficient.

Mr. FASCELL. We might as well make the record complete. For your strong support of this program throughout the years we thank the gentlemen. The meeting is adjourned. (Whereupon, at 12:20 p.m., the committee adjourned.)
