WEDNESDAY, MAY 4, 1966 HOUSE OF REPRESENTATIVES, COMMITTEE ON FOREIGN AFFAIRS, 

Washington, D.C.

The committee met, pursuant to adjournment, at 10:25 a.m. in room 

2172, Rayburn House Office Building, Hon. Thomas E. Morgan 

(chairman) presiding.

Chairman MORGAN. The committee will come to order. 

The committee meets this morning in open session on a continuation of the foreign aid bills, H.R. 12449 and H.R. 12450.

Our first witness this morning is the representative of the AFL- CIO. Appearing for this organization this morning is Mr. James F. 

Doherty, accompanied by Mr. William Doherty, Jr., Adiministrator,

American Institute for Free Labor Development. 

Mr. Doherty, you have a prepared statement. You may proceed, sir.

STATEMENT OF JAMES F. DOHERTY, LEGISLATIVE REPRESENTA- TIVE, AMERICAN FEDERATION OF LABOR AND CONGRESS OF INDUSTRIAL ORGANIZATIONS Mr. JAMES DOHERTY. Yes, Mr. Chairman. 

At the outset I would like to extend the apologies of Andrew Biemiller who was scheduled to testify but was unable to be here this morning. Mr. Biemiller has flown to Detroit to attend the funeral of his personal friend who recently died, unfortunately, Senator Pat McNamara. 

Mr. Chairman, my name is James F. Doherty. I am legislative representative for the AFL-CIO. I am accompanied by William C. 

Doherty, Jr., Administrator of the American Institute for Free Labor

Development. 

I am appearing here today to reaffirm the long-standing support of the AFL-CIO for this Nation's foreign aid program. 

Since the beginnings of foreign aid at the end of World War II, U.S. foreign aid programs have helped improve economic and social conditions throughout the free world.

The United States has continued to provide economic and military support to help the weaker nations against the threat of Communist subversion. This threat was very serious in a number of countries because of their lack of adequate democratic institutions. In this situation, the United States has recognized its duty to aid the build- 791

ing of free nations by extending a humanitarian hand to strengthen the health and economy of the free world.

We urge that Congress continue to include economic aid in its program of assistance to other nations, and we urge that appropriate mechanisms be found to assure the highest effectiveness of our economic aid in building strong, free societies in developing nations. 

The Communist enemy can never be defeated by military means alone. Along with adequate military measures, there must be sound large-scale programs for improving the conditions of life and labor and for developing democratic institutions. 

One of the lessons of Vietnam, where American fighting men are dying in battle is that lags in social and economic development produce political instability, poverty, and frustration which invite Communist aggression.

We in the labor movement know well that foreign aid will not solve all problems in underdeveloped nations. Political passions and aggressive military force can sweep away overnight the accomplishment of years of patient, laborious work and the accomplishments of millions of foreign aid dollars. 

There are no rewards without risks-and the risks we take in our foreign aid efforts are infinitesimally small in comparison to the potential rewards-stability, progress, and peace. We know there are risks. But we also know that our foreign aid program makes a vital contribution to the cause of human welfare, economic progress, and political stability in the newly developing nations of the free world.

Therefore, we support the aid expenditures called for in the present bills, and we are prepared to support sums going well above the level of the current requests. 

In past years the AFL-CIO has repeatedly subscribed to the target of 1 percent of gross national product for foreign aid that was suggested by a United Nations resolution in December 1960, and reaffirmed in December 1961. This country voted for that resolution, Mr. 

Chairman, and the United States also supported a recommendation in July 1965, by the OECD's Development Assistance Committee that the developed countries should strive to meet the 1 percent goal. 

However, the present bills call for less than one-half of 1 percent for total aid, and for economic aid alone only 0.34 percent of our GNP. 

We recognize that aid totals must depend on availability of sound projects and on existence of suitable political conditions in the recipient countries, rather than on some arbitrarily set figure. Nevertheless, it seems clear that one-half of 1 percent of our wealth is not enough for a program which offers so much hope for peace and progress.

The level of aid must also depend on the ability of the developing countries to absorb the capital provided. There is no sense pouring money in where skills to put it to work are missing. Most authorities agree, however, that the World Bank was on solid ground in recently estimating that the developing nations could effectively use at least $3 to $4 billion more in aid than they are now receiving yearly. 

A third determinant of the proper level of aid is the amount of our balance-of-payments situation allows us to carry. In recent years more

than four-fifths of AID expenditures has returned to this country in orders for goods and services and the drain on our balance of payments as a result of aid has thus become negligible. 

From whatever angle the question is considered, it is clear that we can and must provide adequate levels of foreign aid. Recognizing this, the AFL-CIO Executive Council in February of this year called upon the House and Senate to take "prompt and favorable action on the President's request." Today we urge again that the Congress approve the requests, and, where needed, add to them. 

Turning to the specific aspects of this year's bill, Mr. Chairman, the 

AFL-CIO Executive Council has endorsed the President's move to separate the economic and military aid requests and to have aid authorized for a 5-year period. I should like to have the full text of the 

Executive Council's statement of February 25, 1966, included in the record of these hearings. 

(The document follows:) STATEMENT BY THE AFL-CIO EXECUTIVE COUNCIL ON FOREIGN AID, FEBRUARY 25, 1966, BAL HARBOUR, FLORIDA 

Since the end of the Marshall plan, our country has spent about $46 billion on foreign aid. Though millions of people have been helped thereby, the need for assistance to the nations striving to emerge from poverty continues. Toward meeting this need, President Johnson has asked Congress to appropriate $3,386 million for the new fiscal year. The executive council urges the House and 

Senate to take prompt and favorable action on the President's request. 

We welcome the President's new emphasis on increased appropriations for improving health, education, and production of food in order to overcome ignorance, hunger, and disease. For some time, it has been clear that the yearly foreign aid programs require better planning. The executive council calls upon 

Congress to enact into law the President's proposals to separate the economic and military aid programs and to have them authorized for a 5-year period in order to make possible their being planned more effectively.

We heartily support President Johnson's insistence that our help should go to countries which are "not hostile to us" and which provide "solid evidence that they are determined to help themselves." With these requirements in mind, no 

U.S. aid should be given to any government which encourages or engages in subversive movements and activities against our country and nations friendly to us. In this light, the Governments of Ghana and the UAR should not receive any American assistance until they cease and desist from such hostile acts against our country as their participation in and support of the recent so-called Havana Tricontinental Conference which was organized solely for furthering the armed overthrow and destruction of all governments that are opposed to Communist world domination. The Egyptian Dictator Nasser went so far as to send greetings to this congress of subversives and to invite them to hold their next meeting in Cairo.

We cannot stress too forcefully that any government which neglects sorely needed social reforms and gives priority to military adventures and subversive undertakings against other lands does not deserve American aid. Such neglect and adventures account, in no small measure, for the deteriorating conditions in a number of developing countries. Furthermore, our Government should do everything to make sure that its shipments of wheat under Public Law 480 are not taken for granted by any regime as an easy and convenient source of food. 

Such an attitude can only undermine the urgently needed efforts of the aided peoples to help themselves by raising their own food supplies. 

The executive council urges that our country should join the new Asian Development Bank which is to have predominantly Asian management and "contribute to economic development * * * by providing technical assistance and by serving as institutional nucleus for regional economic cooperation." It is encouraging that more than 30 countries have already signed the agreement or indicated their interest in joining this Bank for promoting economic progress in the world's most populous continent.

Mr. JAMES F. DOHERTY. The separation of economic and military aid into two bills will enable the public to understand better just how much money is going into economic development and how much into military aid aimed at making peaceful progress possible. 

The proposed 5-year authorization will allow more rational longrange planning in developing countries, based on the knowledge that the United States is committed to a general range of funding over the 5-year period. Elimination of annual authorizations will allow more detailed congressional reviews of specific program operations in nonauthorization years, while retention of yearly appropriations will maintain congressional control over the program. 

There are two parts of the foreign assistance legislation which are of particular concern for the AFL-CIO this year. One is section 224, which concerns the Latin American housing guarantee program. Last year the Congress increased the amount of guarantee authority from $250 million to $400 million, specifying that this amount would be used through 1967. This year's bill now would advance that date to 1971, and without providing any additional guarantee authority. The labor unions and other groups involved in this program doubt that the new $150 million in authority which was allocated last year for a 2-year period will be sufficient for 6 years. Therefore, we request that either the 1967 date be restored or that additional guarantee authority be granted for the years 1968 through 1971.

Even more urgent, Mr. Chairman, is a need to specify in the legislation some guidelines for setting the interest rates on loans under the Latin American housing guarantee program. 

AID has earmarked $67 million in guarantees for possible loans from pension and welfare funds of U.S. trade unions to provide lowcost housing for Latin American union members. $10 million has already been lent to the Graphic Arts Workers Union in Mexico City which has built 3,104 units now housing over 18,000 people. This was done through the American Institute for Free Labor Development, the AIFLD, a private group which strengthens free trade unionism in Latin America with the support of American labor, business, and Government. Other AIFLD projects are advanced almost to the construction stage in five more Latin American countries. 

These programs and others have been deadlocked by Government requirements that the interest rate on the loans not exceed a predetermined maximum. These maximum rates have been set unrealistically. When the market rate for investments of this type was about 

5.25 percent, the maximum rate permitted was 5 percent; the Government raised this maximum to 5.25 percent only after the market rate jumped to about 5.5 percent and finally now, when the market demands about 5.75 to 6 percent, the Government has set the maximum of 5.5 percent.

We understand just recently that this figure will be increased on new construction to 5.75 percent.

It is the AFL-CIO's position that 100-cent dollars be sent to Latin America for worker housing and that for projects of this type points or discounted loans are not the appropriate device to bring the yield up to the demands of the capital market. 

Mr. Chairman, this whole program, which has had strong congressional support, and which the AFL-CIO considers an outstanding

way of developing union-to-union, people-to-people relationships, cannot go forward unless interest rates on the guaranteed loans are allowed to fluctuate in accordance with those prevailing in the U.S. money market.

The present law states merely that the President may issue guarantees "on such terms and conditions as he shall determine," which at the moment means that the 5.5 percent limit set by the Treasury cannot be exceeded.

We strongly urge that the Congress amend this section of the law--section 224, subsection (b)-by inserting after the words "as he shall determine," the following language: subject to the following: authority shall be vested in AID to permit yields to investors in the program which shall correspond to the current official interest rate on FHA insured mortgages and which may exceed that rate by not more than 2 percent per annum whenever, in the judgment of AID, the FHA yield is in excess of the interest rate and a compensatory adjustment for the AID program is required.

This would allow the rates to change as the market fluctuates, which in turn would allow us to get on with the job of moving Latin American workers and their families from slums into decent housing. 

That was one purpose for which the Congress passed section 224, and it cannot be fulfilled unless the interest rate provision is amended. 

In regard to the question of AID fees for the housing guarantee program, the AFL-CIO would like to commend AID for its recent decision to reduce from 1 percent to one-half of 1 percent the AID guarantee charge for loans which have already been guaranteed by the host country government. This action will reduce the cost of home financing for the Latin American workers. 

The other portion of the fiscal 1967 aid proposals of special interest to labor is the level of technical assistance funding, for it is these funds that support AID's use of the special skills of labor and many other private sector groups.

The President has stated that he will request appropriations for fiscal 1967 of approximately $88 million in technical assistance funds for Latin America, and $231 million for similar programs in the rest of the world. We urge that these sums be increased, if possible. They should certainly not be reduced.

The AFL-CIO also feels that not enough of the technical assistance funds appropriated in the past have gone to help free labor unions in the developing countries. 

In its report to the Congress on fiscal 1965 operations, AID lists only 4.6 percent of Latin American technical assistance funds going to labor projects, and the worldwide total was only 1.8 percent. This latter figure is up from 1 percent in fiscal 1964, but it still seems far too low.

Mr. Chairman, there are few groups in the developing countries which have the political and economic importance of labor unions. 

They are often the only groups which exist to represent the interests of lower income families in societies where a small upper class has traditionally monopolized economic and political power. Free, non- 

Communist trade unions are the best starting point for developing the cooperatives, credit unions, and other private groups which form the true basis of economic and political democracy.

In traditional cultures, unions bring together those first exposed to modern production methods. In nations largely rural, unions comprise powerful urban organizations which can exert political influence far greater than their numbers alone would suggest. In all these ways, labor unions can be termed one of the leading sectors of modernization. The direction they take, be it democratic or dictatorial, responsible or demagogic, can often set the pattern which will be followed by the whole society as more and more groups come in touch with modern ways of life.

In view of this importance, it seems clear that a sizable share of technical assistance funds should be used to help free trade unions develop greater capabilities for exerting democratic and responsible leadership.

The AFL-CIO feels that one-tenth of technical assistance funds would be a reasonable portion. Of course, this goal cannot be reached by arbitrary allocations of funds without relation to sound, realistic projects. The capabilities of American labor organizations to provide assistance, and of free trade unions abroad to receive such aid, must be built up to the point that funds of this magnitude can be utilized effectively. 

We see this process well advanced in Latin America, where AIFLD's growing capabilities have boosted labor's share of technical assistance funds far above the worldwide average. Nevertheless, even in Latin 

America the share for labor is now only about half of what it should be, and we therefore urge this committee to endorse a steadily growing share of assistance to free trade unions in technical assistance appropriations.

The AFL-CIO wishes to commend AID for increasing stress on the importance of the private sector in the aid program as recommended by the Advisory Committee on Private Enterprise in Foreign Aid, generally known as the Watson committee.

The report of this committee praised AID for its "increasing use of contractors on the handling of special tasks," since this practice taps the great reservoir of special skills in the American private sector which are not directly available within government. The contract with the AIFLD is a case in point.

The Watson committee also called for the developing countries to "strengthen and extend their own private sectors and to build up many of the other institutions of a pluralistic society." The committee thought these ends could be reached by having AID finance links of foreign nonprofit groups such as cooperatives and labor unions with their counterparts in the United States, and by having the American groups help develop local labor and co-op financial institutions in developing countries. 

The AFL-CIO wishes to endorse these recommendations and call again, as we did last year for more loans to go directly to private groups in the developing countries rather than through their governments. 

Direct loans are more likely to reach the lower income groups which need aid the most, if they don't go through a "trickle down" process.

Direct loans also build autonomous private groups and thus promote economic pluralism, whereas government-to-government loans may promote statism. We hope this committee will endorse the concept of

direct lending to private groups of low-income people in the developing nations.

President Johnson's message on foreign aid proposes increased emphasis this year on agriculture, education, and health. The AFL- 

CIO endorses these new priorities, as we have long felt that too much weight has been put on industry and infrastructure, and not enough on human resources. Our poverty program is stressing the same fields as are now proposed for the aid program. 

These new social priorities are necessary. Development plans which do not raise immediate living standards will fail to provide the social and economic incentives which assure political stability. Economic progress and social justice go hand in hand. 

The AFL-CIO has constantly emphasized that the living and working conditions of the peoples of developing nations must show constant improvement for the mutual benefit of all concerned. AID is designed only to help start a process of sustainable economic growth. Continued social and economic change, both through self-help within the countries and through international efforts beyond that of AID, are essential.

Better wages and working conditions and better living standards for people do not automatically result from the inflow of capital or the development of a strong economic base. We must accelerate social progress in a variety of ways-through activities such as aid to develop strong, free trade unions and through the encouragement of social projects for housing and health. 

As the most recent AFL-CIO convention declared in a statement on 

Latin America: 

The working people of a Latin America can play a decisive role in speeding a better day for their respective homelands. Through AIFLD and other activities, we of the AFL-CIO will spare no efforts to help our Latin American brothers develop the self-reliance and initiative needed to insure the triumph of freedom and social justice. High rates of profits and low wages can never be a foundation for permanent national prosperity or expanding social justice. Only a strong responsible free and democratic trade union movement can assure sound and balanced economic development-increased productive capacities with higher purchasing power, increased possibilities for the people to consume the larger volume of industrial and agricultural production. 

In our own efforts abroad, the American labor movement has already been stressing the very programs now called for by the President. 

For example, in the field of food production the American Institute for Free Labor Development is now completing construction of three "campesino" service centers in the impoverished northeast section of Brazil. These centers will help agrarian union members to increase their productivity, thus raising their own living standards and easing the food shortage in the area.

It is noteworthy that the "campesinos" themselves set priorities for the activities in these centers which parallel those called for by the 

President: the farmers asked for agricultural extension services, preventive medicine, and education efforts such as literarcy and vocational training programs.

The work of AIFLD also stresses education. The institute has now reached over 40,000 Latin Americans with trade-union instruction, and last year expanded into training for rural workers in Central

'

America. These "campesinos" are being taught how to obtain their rights under land reform legislation and how to raise the productivity of their farms.

The African-American Labor Center, another private labor group supported by the AFL-CIO, is equally involved in educational work. 

This month a Trade Union Institute opened in Lagos to provide basic labor education to Nigerian workers. A motor drivers school is operating in the same city with the assistance of an AALC technician. 

In Nairobi, Kenya, an American technician is being supplied by the 

African-American Labor Center to expand a Tailoring Institute which is so popular that 300 applications were received for the 40 vacancies in the current class.

In the field of health, the AFL-CIO Impact Projects program in Latin America, administered by AIFLD, has so far launched four small projects to improve health and hospital facilities, and more are planned. In Africa the African-American Labor Center is developing a project to train the staff of an infant and child welfare clinic. 

The clinic building will be constructed by the local trade union movement. Thus the American private sector, spearheaded by American labor, has in many respects anticipated the priorities now being applied in the Government aid program. 

I think it is clear that the American labor movement takes a direct interest in foreign aid, not merely as a bystander, but as a participant in the national effort to spread democracy, economic well-being, political stability, and peace throughout the developing world. We are providing funds and we are sending some of the most qualified men in the American labor movement to serve abroad as needed, often in uncomfortable and even dangerous posts. We believe the recommendations we have made in this statement will enable us to do a better job in our part of the foreign aid program. 

In conclusion, Mr. Chairman, I want to stress again the strong support the AFL-CIO has for increased foreign aid expenditures. 

We appreciate this opportunity to express our support and our recommendations for improvements in the foreign aid program.

STATEMENT OF WILLIAM C. DOHERTY, JR., ADMINISTRATOR, AMERICAN INSTITUTE FOR FREE LABOR DEVELOPMENT 

Mr. WILLIAM DOHERTY. We think that the reason the program has been slow should not necessarily be laid at the doorstep of AID, Mr. 

Chairman. There has been a lack of consonance between the interest rates allowable by AID and the Treasury Department with the market in the United States, and U.S. trade union funds as well as any other long-term funds have to get what the market in the United States is willing to pay at a particular time.

There are many projects that are ready to go forward immediately, providing this problem can be resolved. If these projects are allowed to go forward, the rapidity with which the construction would take place would be increased and we feel then that with a 5-year stretchout you would find yourself in the latter 2 or 3 years of the program without sufficient guarantee funds to continue the program. 

Chairman MORGAN. Do you feel the fluctuation of the interest rates have more to do with the delay than the AID agency? 

Mr. WILLIAM DOHERTY. Yes. That has been our experience. We do have projects where all the technical and the social and the financial considerations have been taken care of. They have gone through all of the AID committees and the U.S. Government's interagency committees, but we have not been able to attract investment at an interest rate which is substantially below that allowed by the market. This is why most of the programs are now being delayed. 

Chairman MORGAN. Are you looking for a new decision by the 

Treasury Department? I know you have a personal interest in the program. Can we look for any change that will get this program off the ground in the next 2 or 3 years? 

Mr. WILLIAM DOHERTY. We make it clear in our testimony, and this is based on opinions of the AFL-CIO authorities in this field, that only if you allow some sort of reasonable comparison between this program and the domestic mortgage market will sufficient funds be attracted to the program to make it a success. 

We understand that last week the Treasury Department has decided to allow for new projects a 534-percent interest rate. We feel that if this committee will take the appropriate action and if the law is changed to allow a comparison to the local market rather than setting these rigid interest rate figures, and the market is thus allowed 

---

to come into play, the program has a much greater chance of being successful. 

Chairman MORGAN. You think there should be some amendments to this section? 

Mr. WILLIAM DOHERTY. We definitely do, Mr. Chairman. 

Chairman MORGAN. Thank you, Mr. Doherty.

Mr. Whalley.

Mr. JAMES DOHERTY. Yes, sir. We have by resolution, and in the normal labor press we have also adhered to this policy that foreign aid expenditures should approximate 1 percent of our gross national product. We follow the United Nations resolution on that subject. 

Mr. WHALLEY. Many of the people of our area in central Pennsylvania and south central, write to us asking us to vote against foreign aid. I think perhaps if you would inform them of your position it might help more Congressmen to vote in support of the program.

Mr. JAMES DOHERTY. I am grateful for that suggestion.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Zablocki. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. At the outset I would like to commend the AFL-CIO and the AIFLD in their efforts in Latin America which are proving so successful and so helpful. May I ask whether the AIFLD program is budgeted on an annual or a multiyear basis? 

Mr. WILLIAM DOHERTY. Congressman Zablocki, we budget on an annual year basis. It gives us some difficulty at times, because the 

Federal Government is budgeted on a fiscal basis. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. You want the Congress to authorize AID on a multiyear basis? 

Mr. WILLIAM DOHERTY. What we have done, in collaboration with the appropriate authorities of AID and the State Department, is this: 

We budget on an annual year basis, but we are projecting on a 2-year basis so they can have a better concept of what our needs will be. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Wouldn't you think your argument would be more impressive if you budgeted your own programs on a multiyear program. Then you could come to us and ask us to do likewise, rather than ask us to authorize the AID program on a 5-year basis, while you budget your programs annually? 

Mr. WILLIAM DOHERTY. We are, as I say, budgeting on a forward 

2-year basis. If it were to be the intention of Congress that programs would be on a forward basis of 5 years, I feel sure that our organization could subscribe to the desires of Congress. 

It wouldn't be too sensible for us to do it now, because it wouldn't be very meaningful. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Do you think your programs would be more effective if you had multiyear budgeting for AIFLD? 

Mr. WILLIAM DOHERTY. I think so.

Elimination of annual authorizations will allow more detailed Congressional reviews of specific program operations in non-authorization years

Mr. JAMES DOHERTY. We know that as a practical matter they do.

Mr. JAMES DOHERTY. It is not advisable because the review of these policies-

Foreign Affairs Committee?

Mr. JAMES DOHERTY. That is not our idea at all. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. But that would be the end result; would it not?

Mr. JAMES DOHERTY. This may be, at the end of the road, where the ultimate control is exercised, obviously. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. If you agree that this committee is better equippedlet me say very concretely, more knowledgeable and under our committee legislative structure is the authorizing committee which should set and formulate policy, you should review your position which, if followed, would weaken this committee's control over the foreign aid policies. 

Mr. JAMES DOHERTY. No, sir. I know you disagree with the sentence that precedes that, but these go hand in hand. We do not envision the Appropriations Committee, having control over foreign policy or these programs of AID. What we do say is that if this committee can review these programs on an annual basis, or during each session of Congress, then I think their recommendations and reports will have an influence on what the Appropriations Committee does. It should be the predominant influence. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Let's analyze your phrase that the yearly apropriations will maintain congressional control. What do you mean by that? 

What control? 

Mr. JAMES DOHERTY. We mean that the committee will be able to devote more of its time to the annual reports to Congress 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. The appropriations 

Mr. JAMES DOHERTY. Reviewing these programs. I am talking about the authorizations. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. I am referring to your statement that yearly ap-

propriations will maintain congressional control. How? What do you mean? 

Mr. JAMES DOHERTY. Just as I said before, what we consider would be the better situation would be that the House Foreign Affairs Committee can review the programs which it has authorized, and can make reports and recommendations. We think this will have an influence on the Appropriations Committee. 

Without that, we certainly would eliminate the business of the Appropriations Committee having the ultimate control over these. We don't think the Appropriations Committee should have control over the programs. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Then you would agree with me that our foreign aid program should not be determined on the basis of the amount a congressional committee sets as reasonable for foreign aid, cutting the amount requested across the board without any close attention to specific programs? Also, would you agree with me it is preferable that there be a study of the type of foreign aid programs which best suits our needs, not just determining the amount of money to be appropriated by Congress? 

Mr. JAMES DOHERTY. Yes. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. Your statement that 1 percent of our gross national product might be allocated for foreign aid, which would probably be spent helter skelter whether it is necessary or not adds to this theory that is going about that we need only appropriate a certain amount of money rather than look at the type of programs we are responsible for? 

Mr. JAMES DOHERTY. We have qualifications on that in our statement. We are not married to this 1 percent figure as an arbitrary figure. We think the money should be spent on sound programs carefully considered by the Congress.

Mr. ZABLOCKI. In reply to a question, you said it was the view of the AFL-CIO that at least 1 percent of our gross national product should be spent for foreign aid. 

Mr. JAMES DOHERTY. Yes, sir. We also say we recognize that these totals must depend on the availability of sound projects. 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. The people in the Appropriations Committee are the ones who are supporting multiyear authorization, due to the greater emphasis of the dollar sign, rather than the programs. 

Mr. JAMES DOHERTY. I would agree with you in that I think this 

Mr. ZABLOCKI. There lies the weakness of multiyear authorizations and I hope we do not give much attention to that particular testimony and recommendation of yours. 

Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Berry. 

Mr. BERRY. Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

Following up the questioning of Mr. Zablocki, what was the gross national product last year? 

Mr. JAMES DOHERTY. It approached $700 billion. 

Mr. WILLIAM DOHERTY. This year the projections are that it would approach $700 billion, Mr. Berry. 

Mr. BERRY. That will be about $7 billion for foreign aid. Where do you propose to get this money?

Mr. JAMES DOHERTY. From the funds of the United States. 

Mr. BERRY. We are already spending in the red at least from $5 to $12 billion, aren't we? And if we add this much more, what does this mean in terms of budgetary considerations? 

Mr. JAMES DOHERTY. I don't think it is proper to look at it, as it has been pointed out that $4 out of every $5 spent on foreign aid comes back to this country in the form of orders for goods and services. So you look 

Mr. BERRY. It builds up- 

Mr. JAMES DOHERTY. We must consider funding in connection with the economic impact here, sir.

Mr. BERRY. In other words, it is a subsidy to domestic industry; that is what you are trying to say? 

Mr. JAMES DOHERTY. Yes, sir; in a sense it is.

Mr. BERRY. Even if we have to borrow the money and pay interest on it down through the years, it is a good investment; is that what you are trying to say? 

Mr. JAMES DOHERTY. We think it is a terrific investment.

Mr. BERRY. It is a terrific interest rate we have to pay and it is going higher all the time, too. I was interested in your statement that in recent years four-fifths of the aid expenditures has returned to this country in orders for goods and services, and that, therefore, it is no drain on our balance of payments.

Is there any difference if we loan the money or give the money to some country and they in turn buy our goods? Is that any different than if we just bought goods ourselves with funds out of the Treasury and shipped them to that country? 

Mr. JAMES DOHERTY. Of course that would fly in the face of the concept that you are trying to let these countries help themselves; to encourage their own institutions- 

Mr. BERRY. I am talking about balance of payments. You said that this helps our balance of payments. 

How can it help? 

Mr. JAMES DOHERTY. I didn't say it could help it. I said there was not a significant balance of payments problem here since a great portion of the money was returned to the United States. 

Mr. BERRY. You said it was no drain on our balance of payments.

Of course it is, because the goods are being shipped out without cash return. Maybe in 50 years we will get the cash return or something; isn't that correct?

I was also interested in your statement at the bottom of page 3 when you are talking about housing in South America. Could you tell me how this program is doing down there? Are we making as many foolish-I should say bad loans as we are in FHA in this country? 

Mr. WILLIAM DOHERTY. I don't know whether the comparison,

Congressman, is appropriate. I will say that the housing program in 

Latin America is probably having the most important political effect of any of the programs that the AID is presently engaged in because the slum conditions, particularly in the urban areas, as I am sure you are aware, are much more miserable than anything you can envisage in this country. Whatever this country can do by making long-term credit available--and these are loan programs, they are hard-nosed

programs to the workers in Latin America, we should do. I think it is the best thing we can do to fight communism in Latin America. 

The repayments of the one large loan to Mexico are running at absolutely 100 percent. They are on time and the workers have paid monthly for the last 9 months. We don't know of any defaults that have been made on the loans in any of the countries where AID has currently guaranteed such programs. 

Mr. BERRY. Do you make loans in South America as well as Mexico? 

Did you make this loan, that the AFL-CIO made to Mexico? Is that to the Government?

Mr. WILLIAM DOHERTY. The loan is made with a guarantee of repayment, a gold guarantee on repayment, by the Mexican Government. 

It was made through the Government to the Graphic Arts Workers Union. They are coparticipants in the benefits of the loan. 

Mr. BERRY. This $67 million that you are talking about on the top of page 4, that goes for low cost housing for Latin American union members, must they belong to the union in order to get a loan for this housing? 

Mr. WILLIAM DOHERTY. This is not an absolute condition precedent.

They must need housing. Take, for example, the Mexican project we have referred to: there are 3,104 units in the project. Ninety percent of the families are union people. Ten percent are professional people. Of those 90 percent that are union members, they are members of a variety of unions. This we think desirable, because we wouldn't think that all truck drivers and all miners would want to live next to each other after working together all day long. 

Mr. BERRY. In your statement you said AID has earmarked $67 million for low-cost housing for Latin American union members. I would suspect that they would have to be union members- 

Mr. WILLIAM DOHERTY. It probably would have been more accurate to state for union-sponsored projects. In many housing projects sponsored by unions right here in these United States, by the Amalgamated Clothing Workers, for example, millions of dollars have been invested in these programs where in some of the projects as little as 30 percent of the people are union members. 

Mr. BERRY. Before my time has expired, Mr. Chairman, I want to ask one more question. In this resolution that was passed at your annual meeting you indicate that the AFL-CIO will spare no effort to help our Latin American brothers develop the self-reliance and initiative and so on and so forth.

Mr. WILLIAM DOHERTY. Financially? 

Mr. BERRY. Yes.

Above and beyond these contributions, the AFL-CIO Executive Council has already appropriated $100,000 for small impact projects. 

Some 44 of these projects have been approved within the past 18 months. These projects are designed to be of immediate assistance to needy workers.

These projects have been carried forward now in some 13 different countries, and they are both on an interest-free loan basis and on a

grant basis. These projects are baking cooperatives, clothing cooperatives, school construction- 

Mr. BERRY. This is $100,000?

Mr. WILLIAM DOHERTY. Yes. 

Mr. BERRY. This 

Mr. WILLIAM DOHERTY. I think the record should show, and Mr.

These expenditures never come back to the AFL-CIO. The foreign unions, of course, do not pay dues here. 

A very substantial part of the annual budget of the AFL-CIO is spent internationally.

Mr. JAMES DOHERTY. That is our thought. The problem with these welfare and pension funds there is that they are held for the benefit of beneficiaries who are going to retire in the United States. 

They may or may not be union members. These funds are trust funds. 

In order to attract this money into these projects in Latin America they have to be made attractive investments. The unions have a moral obligation, and in some senses a legal obligation, to find the best possible investments for these funds that they can.

It is our feeling that if the interest rate is made flexible and related to the American mortgage market, or the American interest rates, more of these funds will be attracted into the Latin American housing projects sponsored by the AIFLD.

Mr. O'HARA. As a rule money will not be loaned if the interest rate is below that which is the usual interest rate? 

Mr. JAMES DOHERTY. That is right, sir.

You must also bear in mind that these loans are guaranteed by the host countries, so the risk to AID is not analogous to that borne by FHA. AID's insurance comes into operation only after the host country defaults on the loan.

Mr. O'HARA. Does AID agree with your position? 

Mr. WILLIAM DOHERTY. It is difficult to determine whether AID agrees. We do know some of the administrators of this program are aware of this problem and sympathetic with our attitude. They feel this has been delaying their program also.

Many of the other groups involved in the program are similarly inclined. They feel that the real problem has been the lack of association of the program with the realities of the interest market here in the United States. 

Mr. O'HARA. It is such a good program I am sure our committee would wish to encourage it in every possible way.

One of the recommendations that you support is that the economic and military aid be separated. Do you really believe that your objective of having 1 percent of our Gross National Product earmarked for foreign aid could be reached by separating economic and military aid requests? 

Mr. JAMES DOHERTY. Again I hasten to point out this is a flexible

of no affiliate which has contested the AFL-CIO's opposition on foreign aid. These resolutions are passed by our conventions made up of delegates who represent the members back home. I don't know of any wholesale opposition within the labor movement to our position on foreign aid. 

Mr. ROYBAL. I have another question, and this is with regard to cooperatives. I think you recommended that funding be made directly to cooperatives in Latin American countries which I think would be a very good idea. 

What are you doing to encourage cooperatives in Latin America? 

Mr. WILLIAM DOHERTY. Congressman, in the field of housing, the overwhelming majority of the projects with which we are associated in some 15 or 16 different countries involve housing cooperatives whereby the union members and other workers have gotten together and formed a cooperative. They receive a global loan, which they pay back on a global basis. They administer their affairs within this cooperative. If they can get the loans directly they can cut out a lot of redtape in both countries, and they can feel much more like they are participating in the Alliance for Progress, rather than getting it from a paternalistic government. 

In the field of rural activities we have formed rural cooperatives and are working them in the field of marketing and in the field of education. Some of these rural cooperatives that have been formed and are developing are the owners of campesino service centers that we have built. In them we are developing all types of services in the field of fertilizer and other farming problems. Probably the most important type of cooperatives that we are working with in Latin. 

America are credit cooperatives. The credit system and banking system of Latin America has never been developed so that workers can get involved. Credit systems in Latin America are for the wealthy to exchange and borrow money from each other. Workers are afraid to go to banks. We are trying to form some sort of system of credit based on cooperatives whereby workers can save money. 

Our most successful effort in this regard has been in Peru where AID made a $6 million loan to a cooperative. This is a workers housing bank cooperative, a savings and loan type institution. 

Although it is only 18 months old and the average workers depositing are only making between $40 and $60 a month, the deposits are in excess of $2 million. They have been saving money and building their own homes at such a rapid rate that the AID money is only now, a year and a half later, coming into play in terms of being able to build viable projects.

The 11 members of the board of directors are all workers. They have proven their responsibility. They have proven that since it is their cooperative they have more pride in it. We would like to see 

AID make direct loans to organizations like this in the credit field throughout Latin America. Most of the activities, as I say, sponsored by AÏFLD and American labor, are in the support of a variety of cooperatives in a variety of endeavors.

Mr. ROYBAL. I examined the cooperative in Peru. I went through their financial statement and everything you have said is true. It is an excellent program. I hope the same thing can be done in other 

Latin American countries.

Mr. WILLIAM DOHERTY. To be very very frank with you, Congressman-and I again would have to check our roster to see what the native States of our personnel are-we do have a very high proportion of our people of Mexican or Latin ancestry. Of course, there are a lot of Latin Americans themselves who are on our staff. I would say we have more Americans coming from the State of New Mexico and Puerto Rico than from other areas. We do have some people from 

California. I would be happy to give you an updated list of the people of Latin ancestry who are working on our staff.

Mr. WILLIAM DOHERTY. I am not in a position to challenge that statement. I can assure you, however, that there has been no slight intended if there are no Californians, Mr. Congressman, in our staff.

STATEMENT OF MRS. DONALD BROWN, MEMBER OF THE NATIONAL PUBLIC AFFAIRS COMMITTEE, NATIONAL COUNCIL OF JEWISH WOMEN 

Mrs. BROWN. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

One of the major interests of the National Council of Jewish Women, an organization established in 1893, with a current membership of 123,000, in 329 communities is to promote effective international relations. We believe that U.S. foreign policy must be directed toward insuring conditions of peace in which the dignity of the individual everywhere will flourish in a climate of free institutions, political independence, and social and economic progress. 

In 1946 we adopted a resolution, which has been reaffirmed in principle at every biennial convention since then, which urges the United States to support economic development and expansion of economic opportunity throughout the world through assistance programs to

both new and developing countries so that they may achieve the economic, social, and educational standards necessary for independence and effective self-government, and by channeling such aid, where possible, through multilateral agreements. 

The continued support by this organization of the foreign assistance program since its inception as the Marshall plan in 1948 and throughout the ensuing years as it has become modified to meet the changing needs of the world is not only the expression of our strong belief in the validity of such a program. It also reflects our memberships' concern with the disadvantaged everywhere, a concern which has been the force majeure in planning our programs of service both at home and overseas. 

Much effort is put each year into a reevaluation of the AID program. This year our organization is understandably distressed at reports that the current pace of international assistance programs, both that of the United States and other industrialized countries, is not meeting the growing needs of the poor people of the world; needs made more acute by the rapidly growing population of the developing countries.

We were gratified, on the other hand, to see that the proposed AID program for fiscal year 1967 included the following recommendations designed to make assistance more effective:

Self-help: This organization favors U.S. insistence on self-help in recipient countries which are capable of entering into a development partnership. We would respectfully submit, however, that in many instances those countries most in need of help are the ones least able to help themselves. Any desire for self-help should certainly be encouraged and nurtured, rather than smothered by the threat of withholding assistance.

Let us also remember that unstable politics and outside threats have a tendency to distract one's energies from internal affairs. We would hope, therefore, that conditions for receiving assistance would be made on an individual basis, with careful consideration being given to the needs and potentials of each country. 

Programs in agriculture, health, and education: This organization strongly endorses AID's new initiatives in these three areas, which reinforced by the Food for Freedom Act as well as the International Health and International Education Acts, will represent a giant stride in meeting three of the most critical needs of the less developed world. 

Surmounting the problems of education, which has been of particular concern to us and which represents a major portion of our own overseas program, is considered by the National Council of Jewish Women as one of the most urgent requirements in building a foundation for international understanding and peace.

As an imperative to the above programs which, if successful, will increase the lifespan and decrease the death rate of the population in recipient countries, we see no alternative other than the adoption of strong family planning programs. Recognizing that assistance in this area can be given only on request, this organization, however, urges increased educational and advisory facilities being made available in the field.

Five-year authorization: The National Council of Jewish Women favors a 5-year authorization, with yearly review of appropriations,

for the economic aid program. We believe this is necessary to give validity and continuity to an assistance program emphasizing sustained long-term development in recipient countries. 

Multilateral aid and regional cooperation: This organization favors the recommendation to channel more aid through multilateral institutions and to encourage and support regional cooperation among donor and recipient countries.

Participation of the private sector: We were gratified to hear that much research is being done on ways of making use of American private resources and to encourage private investments abroad. The 

Committee on Technical Cooperation of the White House Conference on International Cooperation recommended several new ways by which

American businessmen and voluntary organizations could take a much larger role in foreign assistance, ways which seem to us worthy of most serious consideration. Although AID has already extended considerable support to nonprofit organizations involved in voluntary assistance programs, it would seem to us that additional efforts could be made in developing new "Partners of the Alliance" programs and in coordinating the programs among the agencies themselves and AID. 

In conclusion, the National Council of Jewish Women is of the opinion that in an era when our foreign policy is being critically analyzed by almost every segment of our citizenry, it is of utmost importance that the purpose and validity of our foreign assistance program be reaffirmed.

We have testified in the past as to the importance of correcting many of the misconceptions about the program. This year we feel that there is a greater need than ever before to have the program clearly and realistically understood not only by the people of the United States, but by those of the recipient countries as well. 

We urge, therefore, that this committee give favorable consideration to the Foreign Assistance Act of 1967. 

Chairman MORGAN. Thank you, Mrs. Brown. 

Mrs. Brown, referring to page 3 of your statement, your second paragraph about the 5-year authorization, I am sure you realize as a result of your short appearance before this committee this morning that the members present, and I can assure you the members absent, are not really sold on a 5-year authorization.

I think organizations like your own, should be alerted to the attitude here toward the 5-year authorization. Mr. Zablocki has indicated that a number of this committee feel that the 5-year authorization would increase the influence of the Committee on Appropriations on foreign aid.

Even though we have a yearly review of the program, it is difficult by legislative action to change a law when it is on the books. I favor some form of multiyear authorization. I have changed my position. on that. I am going to support a 2-year authorization rather than a 

5-year authorization.

I strongly disagree with the separation of the military and the economic part of the program. I wouldn't be surprised when this bill finally comes out of this committee-I hope sometime at the end of this month, or by the first week of the next month-that we will have a bill that includes both the military and the economic assistance, plus a 2-year authorization. This is my own personal opinion.

You are always a very 

Of course, I have to sell the other 35 members of the committee.

The next witness is the Most Reverend Edward E. Swanstrom, executive director, Catholic Relief Services, speaking for the American 

Council of Voluntary Agencies for Foreign Service, Inc. With 

Bishop Swanstrom is Bernard A. Confer, executive secretary, Lutheran World Relief; Frank L. Goffio, executive director of CARE; and James MacCracken, executive director, Church World Service Department, Division of Overseas Ministries, National Council of the Churches of Christ in the U.S.A. 

Bishop Swanstrom, it is a pleasure to welcome you again to the committee. You have a prepared statement and you may proceed, sir.

STATEMENT OF THE MOST REVEREND EDWARD E. SWANSTROM, EXECUTIVE DIRECTOR, CATHOLIC RELIEF SERVICES FOR AMERICAN COUNCIL OF VOLUNTARY AGENCIES FOR FOREIGN SERVICE, INC. 

Bishop SWANSTROM. My name is Bishop Edward E. Swanstrom. I 

am appearing here today as honorary chairman of the American Council of Voluntary Agencies for Foreign Service, Inc., and representing that organization. I also happen to be executive director of the Catholic Relief Services-National Catholic Welfare Conference, a member agency of the American council. Included among the 41 member agencies of the council are also such organizations as the American Friends Service Committee, the American Jewish Joint 

Distribution Committee, CARE, Inc., Church World Service, Tolstoy 

Foundation, and many others.

During its 22 years as a privately supported consultative and coordinating federation of the major voluntary foreign service organizations in the United States, representatives of the American council have been honored to meet with members of your committee on several occasions to urge support of foreign aid legislation, and also on occasion to suggest particular items which they deemed deserving of especial support. It is my privilege to represent the American council today in a similar capacity.

I should like to state at the outset that the member agencies of the American Council of Voluntary Agencies for Foreign Service, Inc., today, as in the past, wholeheartedly and unanimously support the principle of foreign aid as an instrument in promoting the welfare and development of needy people overseas.

The member agencies of the American council operate people-topeople service and assistance programs in over 100 countries in the developing regions of the Near East, the Far East (including intensive programs in southeast Asia), Africa, and Latin America. Their more than 600 American oversea personnel work side by side with far larger numbers of other nation and counterpart indigenous personnel, both paid and volunteer, in the operation of these programs, and also maintain close working relationships with U.S. Government foreign aid personnel.

They are thus in a position to observe and report at firsthand both the need of the peoples overseas whom they serve, and the efficacy of the efforts devoted to the alleviation of that need. These observations are matters for discussion and action in the appropriate committees of the American council, and form the basis for the deeply rooted and continuing conviction about, and support for, the humanitarian aspects of the economic foreign aid policies of their government. 

The member agencies of the American council would like today to speak particularly to three elements of the foreign aid bill currently before this committee. Two of these represent positions taken previously by the Congress, one of them soon after World War II and reaffirmed year after year, concerning the voluntary agencies' participation in programs of assistance to needy peoples, and cooperation with their government in humanitarian efforts of mutual concern both to the Government and to the people of the United States whom we both represent.

These positions have formed the basis of what the American council described first before this committee in 1956 as a "remarkable partnership" between the Congress of the United States "representing the people of America as their elected spokesmen in the Government" and the voluntary agencies "representing the people of the United States as their personal messengers of aid and good will overseas." This partnership has grown and prospered with mutual benefit over the years. 

1. The provision for U.S. Government ocean freight reimburse ment to the voluntary agencies on material aid supplies furnished through the contributions of their constituencies for distribution overseas, which was first made available to those agencies nearly 20 years ago, has had, and continues increasingly to have a "multiplier" effect on the efforts which the voluntary agencies can bring to bear on their great humanitarian task of alleviating suffering and promoting the development of human resources in needy areas of the world. We thank the Congress for their early understanding of this fact and their faith in the value and efficacy of the voluntary agencies' work. 

2. The provision for the use by the voluntary agencies of excess 

U.S. Government property in their oversea programs was welcomed by them in an earlier enactment of the foreign assistance law as a potentially valuable resource for the enrichment of their programs, particularly now when emphasis increasingly shifts from programs of simple relief to programs of development designed to eliminate the causes of the need for such relief. Although in the first years following the enactment of this provision there was some difficulty in working out mutually useful regulations for its implementation, the agencies are now hopeful that procedures will be developed to bring into full realization the potential of this provision.

3. Finally, the member agencies of the American Council of Voluntary Agencies for Foreign Service wish to commend to your favorable action that feature of the bill which provides for a 5-year authorization of the foreign aid program. The voluntary agencies have long been convinced that long-range programs of development, such as those contemplated under this bill, and whose long-range character is by definition an essential of the program, can most efficiently be planned and carried through when the law authorizing their implementation has a longer lifespan than it has, in general, previously had.

A question perennially before the Congress as it turns, each year, to the business of reenacting foreign aid legislation is, understandably, how long will the people of the United States continue to be willing to support these programs on behalf of faraway peoples in faraway lands.

The voluntary agencies of the American council have confronted this question with respect to their own oversea programs for many years. It is clear that in face of calamities, disasters, and emergencies where need is tangible and easily visualized, the American people respond with a generous outpouring of aid. 

But over the past decade, as the voluntary agencies have come to a growing realization that stopgap assistance to meet present need was not enough, and that the causes of need had to be met through programs of social and economic development, and as they presented these propositions to their constituencies, they discovered that the response of the people was not diminished. Contributions to the work of the voluntary agencies holding membership in the American council in money and supplies for their oversea programs have continued at a rate of nearly $200 million a year. 

In a position paper adopted by the board of directors of the American Council of Voluntary Agencies for Foreign Service in 1954, the agencies described their work in the following terms: *** An act of assistance on the part of a voluntary agency is an act of faith.

The council believes that continuation of the economic foreign aid program of the United States is also an act of faith-of faith in the people of the United States that they will understand the significance of these programs, and of faith in the people of the countries overseas whom we are helping to help themselves. 

Traditionaly it has been said that the willingness of Americans to come to the assistance of fellow men in need derives from the Judeo- Christian cultural background which is part of the American heritage. The basis for this willingness seems to us to be even more far reaching at the present time.

With the growing participation of voluntary groups in other countries of the world, it would seem to be evidence of a developing sense of human solidarity in the face either of some dangerous and frightening unknown future that mankind seeks to avert, or of some wonderful vision of a future that can be, which it shares. 

The member agencies of the American Council of Voluntary Agencies for Foreign Service have faith that the American people, when they are aware of the stakes and the opportunities, will support legis- 60-929-66—pt. 5—6

lation for well planned and viable programs of foreign aid for as long as the need for such programs continues. 

As President Johnson in his state of the Union message said last 

January: 

From the Marshall plan, to this very moment tonight, that policy (to help improve the life of man) has rested on the claims of compassion-and the certain knowledge that only a people advancing in expectation will build secure and peaceful lands.

The American people have supported that policy. 

And we have faith, based on experience, that the American people will agree with their President when he said, in presenting his proposal for foreign aid legislation last February thatthey (the steps he proposed) are informed by compassion and shaped by the history of two decades. They are proof of our devotion to the works of peace. 

They reflect our vision of a world free from fear and ripe with opportunity. 

They will shape the legacy we leave our children. 

Mr. Chairman and gentlemen, particularly in light of some of the things I have heard said here this morning and some of the things that I know you have heard before, I would like to have an opportunity to make a few personal observations as the Director of Catholic 

Relief Services, the American Bishops Foreign Relief Organization. 

Chairman MORGAN. You may proceed, sir.

Bishop SWANSTROM. Thank you. 

Gentlemen, it distresses me deeply to note that year after year our

Congress has been reducing appropriations for aid to the poor nations of the world at the very moment when world conditions would seem to indicate that our aid should be increased. 

Last year, the increase in the gross national product of these United States was greater than the combined income of 20 Latin American nations. Does not this great contrast force upon our consciences the realization that the immense wealth of our country carries with it a corresponding responsibility toward the poor of the world? 

As a responsible Christian leader, I cannot stand silently by nor can I agree that American aid to needy lands should be reduced. 

Indeed, I strongly urge that we take steps to increase our foreign aid to the point where it will be more effective in helping the poor nations to develop.

It is interesting to note that Japan has committed itself to devote 1 percent of the Nation's gross national product to programs of aid for developing countries. Should not the United States and all other wealthy lands of the North Atlantic do equally as much? 

Why were we willing to devote at least 3 percent of our gross national product to aid European countries under the Marshall plan, but now consider it necessary to cut back our aid to the poor nations of Africa, Asia, and Latin America to less than one-sixth of that amount?

I had the great privilege of being present at the four sessions of the Vatican Council during which the problem of the rich nations and the poor nations was discussed at length on numerous occasions. 

That great council document, the "Constitution on the Church in the Modern World," has as an almost constantly recurring theme, the responsibility that wealth carries with it. 

Side by side with the responsibility of wealthy nations is the responsibility of leaders of the developing countries to make the best

possible use of the help that we give them and to realize the vital importance of carrying our social and economic reforms that will be conducive to development. It is gratifying to note that this latter principle is stressed in the foreign aid program that has been proposed for 1967 in line with the President's own appeal to Congress in his foreign aid message.

In his widely quoted encyclical letter, "Mater et Magistra," the late great Pope John wrote:

Perhaps the most pressing question of our day concerns the relationship between economically advanced commonwealths and those that are in the process of development * * *. It is not easy for them to keep the peace advantageously if excess imbalances exist in their economic and social conditions. 

I recall vividly the day in St. Peter's in Rome when Pope Paul returned from his historic trip to the United Nations and came directly from the airport to the council hall. In his talk to the assembled bishops, he reiterated the noble sentiments of his predecessor when he said that the greatest threat to peace in the world today is the gap that exists between the rich nations and the poor nations. 

Gentlemen, in a few days I am to leave for Rome where we are to have a meeting to discuss the setting up of a new agency of the church which will concern itself with the problem of poverty and social justice in the developing countries. One of the tasks of this new agency will be to carry forward a broad program of education and inspiration designed to meet these grave problems. 

I think we have all fallen down on this business of education even here in our own United States. We hope that before the passage of too many years all people will come to understand and appreciate more fully the value and importance of the programs carried out by the U.S. foreign aid and by the agencies of the United Nations. 

When I appear before groups throughout the United States I continually say I wish you had an opportunity to go out as I do and see what the United States is really accomplishing in these countries. 

We hear all these criticisms. You never hear the good things about the problem.

I am convinced that with such a full understanding the Congress of the United States would have no difficulty in finding widespread support for an aid program such as we are discussing here today. 

I say that I think part of the fault is ours in not having done a better educational job up to this time. I insist we mustn't allow a few noisy criticisms of past mistakes to blind our people to the tremendous need that exists in developing lands and to the tremendous and long-term task that lies before us. 

I would be remiss, even at the expense of taking too much of your time, if I did not make two other pertinent observations.

The first is this: That up to the present I think that the aid administration has not gone as far as it might in implementing the provision that you wrote into the Foreign Assistance Act a few years ago which said: 

That it is the sense of Congressthat the administration-use to the maximum extent practicable the services and facilities of voluntary non-profit organizations registered with, and approved by, the Advisory Com mittee on Voluntary Foreign Aid.

I think doing most of our business through governments, we lose an opportunity to take advantage of all that the people-to-people organizations can do in many different phases. 

It is my hope that this could be reemphasized again this year in the deliberations of your committee.

My second observation is this: The more effective our foreign aid programs become in meeting the needs of the poor in these depressed areas, the less need there would be for any extension of family planning program aid beyond the research and study of population problems now authorized under existing legislation. 

I thank you for giving me that much time. If you would, I think these men who are with me would like to make comments of their own. 

Mr. Goffio is the president of the American Council and also head of the great CARE organization.

STATEMENT OF FRANK L. GOFFIO, EXECUTIVE DIRECTOR, CARE 

Mr. GOFFIO. I have a very brief statement. Thank you, Bishop.

Mr. Chairman, I would, as the executive director of CARE, like to fully support the statement made by Bishop Swanstrom in the name of the Council of Voluntary Agencies for Foreign Service. 

As we celebrate our 20th anniversary serving the concern of the American public, we know the stimulation that has been given to us by the foreign assistance made available through this committee has given us the ability to expand our ability to operate overseas. 

As many of you gentlemen know, after 20 years in an agency that has distributed something over three-quarters of a billion dollars in goods and services overseas, we stand today still receiving that same generous support of the American public, which as the bishop said, is reflected throughout all of these United States. 

I continue to be amazed after 19 of those 20 years that we continue to get that kind of support. The opportunity still exists for us to continue to do the kind of job that we think must be done. 

That is all I have to say. 

Chairman MORGAN. Thank you, Mr. Goffio.

STATEMENT OF BERNARD A. CONFER, EXECUTIVE SECRETARY, LUTHERAN WORLD RELIEF 

Mr. CONFER. Mr. Chairman, Lutheran World Relief, too, is, celebrating its 20th anniversary, having been active abroad for two decades now. We have given considerable study to the problems overseas in connection with our own involvement and have seen the development of the foreign aid program of the U.S. Government, and have observations concerning multilateral agencies, too. 

In view of our studies, I hasten to associate myself with Bishop Swanstrom in saying that we don't quite understand the need for the reduction of expenditure in foreign aid programs. In fact, as we look at the future, we foresee a need for an increase. 

We have on a number of occasions formally endorsed the policy of the Government in sharing resources and in promoting self-help. 

Your projects of helping other nations and people helping themselves.

In our addresses and articles, our men, in promoting our own work, appreciate the tremendous support of the U.S. Government to our people. 

A second point is that I think this is the committee where I should register a commendation to AID Administrator, Mr. Bell, who a year ago sent clear directions to his field missions instructing working other people toward a priority on projects in three fields, problems of food supply, population growth and malnutrition. From where we sit, we see that there is growing need in the world and that the menacingly great gap between the resources of the "have" nations and those that have not grows bigger all the time. 

We think this is a foreboding for the peace of the world. 

Thirdly, I would like to observe that we have great reservations about this emphasis that is in the present legislation that the sense of Congress would call for a maximum use of the facilities of voluntary agencies. We think that primarily the material welfare of people is the responsibility of governments abroad, and we question the wisdom of interposing as a matter of policy voluntary agencies between other peoples and American aid.

In my own agency we recently had a survey which indicates that in our 20 years of existence we ourselves have used government resources to the tune of 52 percent of our program. We wonder how high you can go and still preserve fully and promote the cause of voluntaryism. 

We think voluntaryism that has developed in our country is much to be cherished. We think this development needs fostering. 

We think the voluntary agency serves America's and their own best interests when they are programs reflecting in a major degree the free will gift of their private supporters.

My last point, Mr. Chairman, is to say our agency endorses the twoway approach to foreign aid by government, bilateral and multilateral. 

We would express the hope that in the years to come you would continue to strengthen the multilateral approach because as we foresee developments in Africa and Asia there will be additional and more emphatic call for this approach.
