THURSDAY, MAY 5, 1966 HOUSE OF REPRESENTATIVES, COMMITTEE ON FOREIGN AFFAIRS, 

Washington, D.C.

The committee met, pursuant to adjournment, at 10:15 a.m., in room 

2172, Rayburn Building, Hon. Thomas E. Morgan (chairman) presiding.

Chairman MORGAN. The committee will come to order. 

The committee meets this morning in open session for a continuation of the hearings on the foreign aid bills H.R. 12449 and H.R. 12450.

Our first witness this morning is Mr. Robert H. Cory, Jr., who is with the Friends Committee on National Legislation. 

Mr. Cory, you have a prepared statement, and you may proceed, sir. 

STATEMENT OF ROBERT H. CORY, JR., THE FRIENDS COMMITTEE ON NATIONAL LEGISLATION 

Mr. CORY. Thank you very much, Mr. Chairman. 

My name is Robert Cory. I am a resident of Scarsdale. I have the privilege of representing the Quakers at the United Nations as an observer accredited to the Economic and Social Council. 

I am now speaking on behalf of the Friends Committee for National Legislation, an organization supported mainly by Quakers. 

No single person can speak officially for the Society of Friends because of its democratic organization and cherished right of individual opinion, but Friends has always emphasized the dignity and worth of the individual and the importance of giving individuals a sense of taking part in determining their own destinies.

The Friends Committee has long supported those aid programs which help people to help themselves especially technical assistance and developmental aid. The committee has opposed military aid as an obstruction to the developmental process and to the growth of world law. But I am here today to stress the need for more U.S. developmental aid to help close the growing gap between the rich and the poor. I also wish to emphasize the urgency of channeling a larger proportion of our aid through multilateral agencies. We of course strongly support the President's request for $140 million for contributions to international organizations: the U.N. development program, UNICEF, world food program, U.N. Relief and Works Agency, U.N. 

Institute for Training and Research, Indus Basin Development Fund, et cetera.

The United States should increase the size of its aid program. I see the aid question in the perspective of the development decade. When 825

the U.N., with the encouragement of the United States, established the goal for the U.N. development decade of a 1 percent per year flow of assistance from the rich industrial nations to the developing nations, the target seemed modest. Many industrial nations were, by contrast, spending from 5 to 10 percent on armaments and some were spending more than 1 percent of their gross national product on space exploration.

I think there is some sense of disappointment. Many nations, the 

United States included, are spending less on aid than the target of the decade. We are hoping that all the industrial nations will take much more positive positions on this problem of human welfare. 

U.N. AGENCIES PROVIDE NEEDED TRAINING 

I have been in India and am impressed with the need for trained leadership. I feel the public is not aware of how important a role the 

U.N. and U.N. agencies are playing in the training of the sort of leadership that can stimulate local initiative. 

I have with me here today the "World Social Report," which is just being discussed at the U.N., and which is almost entirely focused on the problem of how we get this sense of initiative and self-help that is going to be so crucial, both in the lives of these nations and possibly for the political stability of the world. This and other U.N. studies it seems to me provide a new sort of, you might say "expertise" in the problem of training people, of counteracting the brain drain and getting people trained for the problems in their own countries. 

I am well acquainted with some of the training agencies, the United 

Nations Institute for Training Research, the World Bank's Economic 

Institute, the Asian Development Institute, and I am also somewhat acquainted with some of the projects now being financed by the U.N. program and fellowship studies and ongoing training groups. These programs are carried out by UNESCO, the U.N. Labor Organization, the FAQ, all with an effort to invest in the lives of people so that they themsleves can take the leadership in self-sustaining economic development.

I am, however, very much concerned that in all our programs, both bilateral and multilateral, we tend to neglect one of the greatest resources for development--that is the youth. In most parts of the world these youth groups form a greater proportion of the population than in the highly industrialized countries, and these young people need a sense of purpose and participation in their development activities.

The American Friends Service Committee has, through its work camp and youth corps' service, tried to help. The Peace Corps makes an important contribution, but I would like to suggest to the members of this committee that there should be greater thought given to international programs so that the young people of all nations could have this sort of experience and training. 

DEVELOPING COUNTRIES FIND MULTILATERAL AID MORE ACCEPTABLE 

I don't want to review this morning all the theoretical arguments for multilateral aid. I am convinced that international administration can set standards for local performance without arousing sus-

ན་ picion and the possible resentments that plague bilateral programs. 

It does seem clear to me that international organizations can call on a wider range of skills, because they do not have to operate their recruitment programs within one nation. But the point I would like to emphasize is perhaps the psychological problem, the problem of public response. I was talking just a few days ago to one of our workers from Kenya and he told me there was no question about appreciation among the people of Kenya, but he said that the Kenyans resented aid techniques which constantly emphasized with almost humilating insistence their recipient role. 

I have talked with other Quaker workers who are personally acquainted with development efforts in South America and Asia, and who say that the sense of self-respect in these countries, their desire for an independent foreign policy, creates an overwhelming preference for international types of multilateral aid. 

MULTILATERAL AID IS WELL ADMINISTERED 

Perhaps there are two questions in which any knowledge I may have may be of some help. One is the question of whether international agencies have in practice developed adequate techniques of administration, and secondly, whether these agencies could use more U.S. aid without losing their international character. 

On the first point I may say that over the last 5 years I have been deeply impressed with the changes in administration that have occurred in the development strategy at the U.N. I think the most crucial one has been the increase in the quality and quantity of U.N. resident representatives now stationed in 84 countries throughout the world. These are in a sense really economic ambassadors. I have known some of them personally. I learn of others through their reports. But I am convinced that they are increasingly able to coordinate the activities of many autonomous U.N. agencies and to integrate the U.N. contribution into the development planning of these recipent nations.

Their effect varies with local conditions. Their power depends upon persuasion more than upon ability to deliver massive aid. 

But they can and to my knowledge they do help these nations frame requests for international assistance so that this really becomes international seed money which can produce a harvest of self-sustaining growth.

MUTILATERAL PROGRAMS COULD USE MORE U.S. FUNDS 

I would suggest that valuable as multilateral assistance may be, it has to keep its international character. I realize that the United 

States does make a very substantial contribution and that there is a need for some of the other industrial nations to increase their contributions, and this can be best done by using matching funds. I am convinced that we could do more in this area, that this is not an immediate limitation on the American contribution.

The United States should not worry about the size of its contributions as long as it stays below 50 percent, but should center its concern on the sense of direction and adequacy of programs. Actually, as

I understand it, under present procedures, the United States supplies, for instance in the U.N. development program, only about 17 percent of the overall investment; that is, if all local contributions are included in the calculations.

For instance, I have the figures here that since 1957 in 604 major U.N. preinvestment projects, the local component was nearly 60 percent of the total cost. So while the United States might pay up to 40 percent of the U.N. cost, the U.N. financed only 40 percent of the project cost.

I am also aware of the possibilities for increasing the U.S. contribution to the International Development Association. This organization, as I understand its function, can make a reality out of some of the projects for which other U.N. agencies can do the preinvestment activities, and then these can be carried on, particularly, I think, with the help of funds through the IDA. 

I also know of the part that the United States has taken in the Asian Development Bank. I think we can take a more active part in working out new projects of proven feasibility under the regional economic commissions of the U.N. in Asia and Africa and Latin America. 

In conclusion, I would like to reaffirm my belief that the real war we face is the struggle against hunger, ignorance, and despair, that much of the aid must continue perhaps to be on a nationally sponsored program.

There are no real national frontiers to this human need and suffering. Multilateral aid, open to all members of the U.N., is in a very real sense a symbol of faith in the dignity of men. It carries a message of hope for a more peaceful world.

In strengthening the economic and social work of the United Nations, it is my conviction that the United States is affirming its basic heritage as a pioneer in the search for greater freedom and justice. 

So I believe that increasing the emphasis on foreign aid programs toward U.N. actions will give the United States the most important sort of influence in this world, and that is the reputation for moral responsibility for human suffering and human need. 

Thank you very much, Mr. Chairman.

Chairman MORGAN. Thank you, Mr. Cory. 

Mr. Cory, you speak of the Quaker project in India that you have visited. Would you give the committee some information about the type of project that the Quaker people have in India and in other countries?

Mr. CORY. This particular project has now been passed on to local leadership. It was worked out with the block development program in the very poor State of Maharashtra, where the Indian Government was just building with international help a major dam and needed to arouse the people as to how to use the irrigation facilities and improve their agriculture in that area.

Our little organization sent in a team. We worked for about 10 years, trying to develop a sense of self-sufficiency. We helped to build up some small cottage industries, and we had an agricultural experiment program.

Today we have shifted into an urban area in Baroda in India, where we are trying to develop community programs for the people who are

streaming in there from the countryside into the Indian cities. These are small programs, but again I would emphasize with the very small resources our organizations have, we have to put it into pilot projects that constantly emphasize turning over responsibility to local people as quickly as we can.

Chairman MORGAN. How many projects of this type do the Quakers sponsor overseas? Do you have many of them? 

Mr. CORY. I think at any one time we have 15 to 16. We have programs in South Vietnam now. We have programs in Peru, in 

Mexico, Colombia, in Tanzania and a study on population control in 

Hong Kong. 

Chairman MORGAN. You devote a great deal of your statement to supporting the international organizations contribution and giving an argument for channeling more economic aid funds into multilateral programs. 

I was interested in your statement and the argument that you presented for multilateral programs. Do you feel that the Congress would lose control over any funds appropriated if most of the economic funds were channeled through multilateral organizations? 

Mr. CORY. I don't feel, sir, that given the realities of the huge need that this is really the problem we are facing, especially since the amount of U.S. aid going through the U.N. totals only $140 million at the present time. There are some places where national programs perhaps have advantages, but in terms of our relationships with other countries at the U.N. and their unwillingness to be completely beholden to one country or another, the U.N. programs have a very unique significance. We have done a great deal so far to strengthen them. I would hope that we could do more.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Cory, if we were to turn over most of our economic assistance funds to U.N.-type multilateral organizations, this committee and other committees of the Congress would lose our authority and the opportunity for legislative oversight over these projects. We would be dealing with U.N. officials over which we have no jurisdiction. 

Mr. CORY. I think this is true in one sense, that once you put your contribution into an international program, it essentially becomes an international program.

However, I would just add these two points: The United States and the U.S. mission have been very helpful in having the U.N. develop an evaluation program, and I have been in a very minor way associated with this. They have just completed a very thorough evaluation of the U.N. program in Thailand and these are rather frank evaluations. 

So I would say you are not without an evaluating agency although it is international.

I would say secondly that I think in any program national or international, you essentially have to rely on the quality of the administrative control. It seems to me that once U.N. funds are appropriated, whether they are appropriated to the World Bank under George Woods or to the Development Fund with people like Hoffman, and David Owen and some of the very fine second echelon people, you have to have a certain confidence in their administration.

I would add one other point, that obviously the foreign aid process is a very difficult one to follow in detail. I think there will always be in all aid programs and perhaps a little more in international programs, places where we have hesitation and some degree of feeling that this is not completely under control. 

Yet the United States has a very big voice in the affairs of the U.N. and, in addition, most of these programs are arrived at by a sense of consensus. You just have to look at the average results. 

If you look at some of the U.N. programs in Korea, or some of the much smaller programs in South Vietnam, or programs going on in countries in Africa, you will find that these are places where the U.N. is doing a job that demands a sense of confidence. 

I would feel our investment there is in a sense a small one for a very big objective.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Cory, let's take the United Nations Tech nical Assistance Fund, to which we contribute 40 percent.

Mr. CORY. That is true. I think the U.N. is one of the places where we try to build bridges across conflicts. The Arab nations have problems because there are projects in Israeli territory under the Special. 

Fund. It seems to me that part of our long-range pledge is that we are not in an organization that is just an organization of very close friends. We are in an organization that involves participation by countries with whose policies--one of the most controversial ones right now is the Union of South Africa-our Government doesn't agree, but it seems to me that this is a very minor element and is something that we need greater public understanding of. 

I would myself feel that if I were a participating nation, no matter what my internal political system, if I were arbitrarily excluded from the benefits of the organization that I had joined, I would certainly want to protest this as a violation of the very principles of the United 

Nations Charter.

I, myself, of course represent an organization that believes that individuals should not be penalized for political reasons when there is a very basic question of human need. I would hope we were working toward a period in which some of these bitternesses could be moderated.

That is all, Mr. Chairman. (The following statement has been supplied for inclusion in the record :)

At the May 5 hearing before the House Foreign Affairs Committee, Rep. James 

Fulton invited the FCNL witness to respond in writing to the question of (A) whether the U.S. aid program should be used to implement foreign policy aims, and, if so (B) what percentage of aid activities could be safely set outside U.S. foreign policy considerations and channeled through the UN. (A) The answer as to whether the U.S. aid program should be used to implement foreign policy aims depends, in our view, on how one defines U.S. foreign policy aims.

One school of thought sees the United States as engaged in a nearly unending cold war with the Communist world in which peace is preserved by a precarious balance of terror and in which nation states compete for survival in a violent and often hostile world. Within this framework the foreign aid program is largely viewed as a cold war tool to help establish military allies, influence the foreign policy of other nations, pay for military base rights, affect the votes of other nations in the UN and other international bodies, and provide just sufficient amount of help to prevent widespread economic and social unrest in the less developed countries. Within this framework, aid through international organizations such as the UN has little meaning, other than as a public relations device to indicate U.S. support for programs which are generally popular among the less developed countries. We oppose using the foreign aid program to implement this kind of foreign policy. 

Another view, with which we identify ourselves, is that the national security interest of the United States in an age of nuclear weapons, ballistic missiles,

deadly gases and other weapons of mass destruction requires the rapid creation of a world system of law and order. The UN Charter to which our nation has subscribed, as well as many Presidential and Congressional declarations, stress the U.S. commitment to the goal of developing an international community of nations and a world safe for diversity. In this view, the United States cannot long maintain its security as an island of prosperity in a sea of human misery where so many lack the bare essetnials of food, medicine, education, and useful work. Within this context, military aid perpetuates the arms race, fosters local wars and military dictatorships in underdeveloped countries and lessens world security. Large amounts of economic aid are essential because, in the words of President Johnson, we cannot achieve peace and security as long as the "citizens of many developing nations walk in the shadow of misery.” 

In this context, aid channeled through international institutions such as the UN has special importance and relevance. Such aid strengthens internationalism and international institutions, fosters a sense of community and cooperation among nations and helps assure that aid programs are undertaken for constructive rather than divisive purposes.

We support aid programs which unqualifiedly seek to conquer hunger, poverty, disease and ignorance in the less developed countries. Such aid is in the national interest. It helps create a stable, prosperous world, the only kind of world in which this nation can find real security. 

The important question is not so much who receives the credit as who can do the most effective job of economic and social development. On this issue, international programs have impressive advantages for they encourage economic and social development for its own sake rather than as a subsidiary to some military or political policy. They encourage other developed countries to do more and they make it possible to require higher and more uniform performance standards by the recipient nations. (B) With regard to how much U.S. assistance to the less developed countries should be channeled through international organizations, it should be noted that only about one-fourth now moves through the World Bank, IDA, regional banks, UN agencies, etc. We are aware that this question has many angles. Congressional questions center on adequate U.S. control. UN questions center on how much any one nation may contribute without dominating the program and removing its essential international character. 

Nevertheless, we believe the amount of U.S. assistance to international development programs should be increased very substantially on the assumption that the U.S. goal is to support and strengthen international institutions and make most efficient use of world resources in the developmental process. It is difficult to suggest a specific percentage, but we believe the following factors are relevant : 

Technical assistance, which is relatively lower in cost and requires experts of varying capacities, should be channeled very largely through international organizations. U.S. programs might be confined to pilot projects which could develop new ideas and techniques not yet generally accepted and to control projects which would provide a check on the efficiency and effectiveness of international programs. 

Development of loans and grants which involve larger sums of money should be channeled to a greater degree through international agencies than at present, though probably not to the same extent as technical assistance funds.

Whenever the aid involves sensitive areas touching on internal security or difficult domestic reforms within developing nations, international agencies should be used. This would help relieve anxiety in neighboring countries and take the onus off the United States for requiring unpopular internal changes. Examples might include training of police forces and aid contingent on land, tax or fiscal reform, etc.

Some U.S. foreign assistance programs or requirements materially help subsidize export of U.S. products and services, promote the use of agricultural and other commodities, and encourage the private enterprise system. 

These programs might well be frankly recognized as beneficial to the United States for other reasons than strict economic development abroad and financed bilaterally as such.

The question of retaining adequate control and oversight over the wise use of U.S. contributions to international organizations is of course a very basic concern of the Congress. There would appear to be a number of safeguards.

eati stres ords the the 10 (1) The United States as a major, if not the major, contributor to international programs would certainly exercise considerable influence in the selection of the top administrators in whom confidence could be placed, and in the selection of projects. (2) The United States as a major participant can continue to strengthen the independence and competence of the international civil service thus encouraging recipient nations to help establish and implement high criteria for the planning and execution of UN projects. (3) State Department and U.S.-U.N. mission representatives would appear before Congressional Committees on a regular basis to justify programs. 

U.S. Aid Missions could do much more in many countries to work with U.N. 

Resident Representatives and recipient country officials in developing priorities for aid both bilateral and multilateral and in developing international means of evaluating the impact of outside assistance on economic development in these countries.

STATEMENT OF N. R. DANIELIAN, PRESIDENT, INTERNATIONAL ECONOMIC POLICY ASSOCIATION 

Mr. DANIELIAN. Thank you, Mr. Chairman, and Mr. Adair.

It is always a pleasure to appear before this committee which has the responsibility to review the administration's conduct of foreign policy. 

I have here a rather extensive statement. I am only going to use the first 10 pages, which happens to be a summary of the statement, and hope to place the rest of it in the record. 

Chairman MORGAN. Without objection, Dr. Danielian, it will be so ordered. 

Mr. DANIELIAN. The International Economic Policy Association represents a group of American international business corporations vitally concerned with the success of our foreign economic programs, in the interest of free world security and prosperity. 

SUMMARY 

I think it would be preferable at the outset to summarize our recommendations and the factual basis for them. These recommendations, though not unanimous, have wide acceptance in our organization. 

1. It is the conclusion of our organization that despite the "tying" of U.S. bilateral aid and other efforts by AID, the substitution of credit sales for former cash sales through "program" loans under the aid program is continuing to have an adverse impact upon the U.S. balance of payments substantially beyond the figures published by

AID. It is a fact that the United States in most recent years has been experiencing deficits in its recorded transactions with South America, Africa, Asia, including Japan, and international institutions. 

The countries of Western Europe have been earning surpluses, not in their trade and financial dealings directly with the United States, but from their trade and investment income in Asia, South America, and Africa. These are the areas where the United States is spending the largest amounts of foreign aid. 

In Latin America, the substitution effect of "tied" aid appears substantial. As table 4 indicates-table 4 is on page 19 of the manuscript-total Latin American imports from all sources were up $414 million on an average during the years 1961-64, compared with the 

1956-60 period.

Between these two periods, however, total Latin American imports from the United States were down by an average of almost $100 million. This occurred (1) in spite of the fact that total U.S. bilateral aid disbursements almost doubled from an average of $367 million during 1956-60 to an average of $652 million during 1961-64, on an increasingly "tied" basis; (2) in spite of additional dollar disbursements of $320 million by the Inter-American Development Bank; and (3) in spite of increased Latin American export earnings in the United States by an average of $220 per year in the 1961-64 period over the average for the years 1956-60.

In another tabulation, table 3, we have listed a number of aidreceiving countries where our export surpluses are nowhere near the total of our aid, indicating that these countries are spending some of their dollar resources, including aid, in other countries. 

Because of these and other facts elaborated in this statement, the association recommends that the U.S. aid program be reoriented from a line of credit to a real resource basis providing U.S. goods and technical services-for specific projects. 

The committee will recall that we presented specific language on this subject last year. We have refined this language and we are making suggestions for an amendment to accomplish this purpose. "Program" loans should be phased out as quickly as possible because of the adverse effects of this type of aid on the U.S. balance of payments resulting from substitution of aid credit purchases for cash purchases. 

2. We recommend that Congress impose a statutory limit of 25 years on all development loans, including Alliance for Progress loans, and on any loans which may be made under the proposed title VIII for aid to southeast Asia. We believe that a 25-year term would create a greater sense of responsibility in the recipient government toward the economic utilization of dollar-aid funds. 

I think it is interesting to learn that yesterday the House Committee on Agriculture voted overwhelmingly to reduce the time period on dollar credit sales of agricultural products from 40 to 20 years.

We are recommending that the Development Loan funds in all the different categories be reduced from the present 40-year limit to 25 years.

The vast majority of other OECD countries' bilateral aid is in the form of loans anywhere from 1 to 20 years. Tables 5 and 6 in this

statement show that the United States is the only country which extends significant credits on terms exceeding 30 years, and at concessional interest rates.

In a majority of cases these other countries lend at interest rates of 5 percent or more. It is perhaps useful to comment that the international discussion on whether one country or another is contributing 1 percent of its gross national product for this purpose is based on rather a statistically unacceptable comparison of hard credit supplied by other countries with the soft loans and grants that the United States makes. If we really put the comparison on what constitutes aid, you will find that the United States is contributing approximately 1 percent of its gross national product for foreign aid and other countries are not. 

3. We oppose, in principle and in practice, open-ended, multiyear authorizations for foreign aid. Definite authorization limits should be provided for those sections and titles of the bill which are open ended. A multiyear authorization deprives the House Foreign Affairs Committee and the Senate Foreign Relations Committee of effective annual review of the foreign assistance program. 

Long-range planning can and has been carried out on an annual authorization basis. The United States has extended foreign aid in the amount of $77 billion for economic assistance and $34 billion in military assistance over the 1946-64 period, and it is highly unlikely that assistance for sound economic development projects will be terminated capriciously. Nor do recipient countries have any sound reason to fear such capricious termination of aid. 

4. As table 2 in my statement indicates, Executive requests for appropriations for fiscal year 1967 for total U.S. foreign assistance activities including military assistance, Public Law 480, international institutions, and the Peace Corps-amount to $5.6 billion. Of this amount, approximately $500 million is for U.S. contributions to international, multilateral financial institutions. This is already in the budget.

In addition to these contributions, there are proposals for amendments to the Foreign Assistance Act to permit the channeling of bi- [ lateral aid funds through multilateral financial institutions. The proposed title VIII, providing for aid to southeast Asia, gives the President discretionary authority to channel whatever funds are appropriated under the open-ended authorization contained in the proposed section 273 of title VIII through any multilateral institutions, without regard to the requirements of the Foreign Aid Act or any other act. Under section 205 of the Foreign Assistance Act of 1961, as amended, the President has had the authority to transfer 15 percent of development loans to the World Bank, the International Develop ment Association, and the International Finance Corporation. This authority has not been used because of an appropriations veto. A section 205-type provision for Alliance for Progress loans has been proposed in amendments suggested on the Senate side. 

We oppose these provisions which permit the channeling of bilateral aid funds to multilateral financial institutions because of the very substantial adverse impact upon the U.S. balance of payments of U.Š. contributions to these institutions.

The United States has contributed 36 percent of the capital of the 

International Development Association (IDA) but received only 13 percent of the identified procurement under IDA credits through 

May 31, 1965.

The United States has contributed 87 percent of the gold and dollar resources of the Inter-American Development Bank (IADB), and our studies, contained in a book which we just published on the U.S. balance of payments, conclude that more than 50 percent of U.S. contributions to the IADB adversely affect the balance of payments. 

This technique also avoids the application of important policies already accepted by the Congress and the President as conditions to bilateral aid such as the "Hickenlooper-Adair" amendment, the requirement of minimum interest rates on development loans-that is, 212 percent after the grace period-the requirement to ship 50 percent of aid cargoes in U.S.-flag vessels, and the congressional directive in sections 202 and 252 to use 50 percent of development loan funds for economic development through private enterprise. 

A transfer of these funds to international agencies to be used under their charter provisions, bypassing the congressional conditions put in the bilateral aid legislation, would give them automatically-would suspend automatically these provisions of U.S. legislation. 

We do not think the conditions that the Congress, in its wisdom, has imposed on bilateral aid should be waived by indirection through transfer of funds to international agencies. This is not to deny the advantages of multilateral organizations, such as those expressed here this morning. We simply feel that they should stand on their own merits and be judged by Congress when they come up for specific authorization and appropriation in their own names. 

5. In my statement I have devoted considerable space to a discussion of the failure of India to make much real economic progress, both in its agricultural and industrial sectors. The statistics are discouraging, particularly since India has been the recipient of about 25 percent of U.S. economic assistance and Public Law 480 shipments in recent years. Since 1948, U.S. assistance to India has amounted to approximately $7 billion-most of it since 1958. Yet progress in 

India has been painfully slow. 

Total grain yields including rice per acre in India are approximately one-quarter of Japan and the United Kingdom. Total grain yields-including rice per acre in India increased only 3 percent in a 59-year period, from 296 kilograms per acre during the 1901-05 period to 305 kilograms per acre during the 1961-63 period.

During the 3-year period 1960-62, India's per capita rate of growth of its real gross domestic product-its GNP was only two-tenths of 1 percent. Per capita gross domestic product rose from $65 in 1953 to $74 in 1962, or about $1 per Indian citizen per year. 

As table 10 indicates, South Korea, Pakistan, Thailand, the Philippines, and Turkey all made considerably greater progress than India in increasing their per capita real gross domestic product. 

The Indian Government's policies and attitude continues to be unsympathetic to the private sector-both foreign and domestic-and these are partly responsible for the slow growth rates in the Indian economy.

These policies include tax rates on private business-including income, super- and sur-taxes-which are quite high-somewhere between 60 and 70 percent. Moreover, the Indian Government preempts certain areas of the economy for exclusive development by Government-owned enterprises.

In addition, there is a requirement of majority equity control by the Indian Government and/or Indian citizens of most foreign investments, discriminatory allocation of foreign exchange to Governmentowned enterprises, and a cavalier attitude toward industrial property rights-all these policies discourage private investment. 

In recent years, while foreign aid to India has been in the neighborhood of that is, international foreign aid-in the neighborhood of $1,300 million a year, annual private investments have been in the neighborhood of between $60 and $70 million a year. 

Indian Government attitudes toward private investment should change before the United States commits large amounts of public aid, other than emergency food aid, which, of course, all of us favor in the largest and quickest way we can bring it to them. 

President Johnson stated in his foreign aid message that only the people and the leaders of aid-recipient countries can "create the climate which will attract foreign investment and keep money at home." 

We recommend that Congress consider amending the authorizing sections for development loans, section 201 (b); for Alliance for Progress loans, section 251(b) of the Foreign Assistance Act of 1961, as amended; and for the proposed title VIII-southeast Asia multilateral and regional programs, section 272, indicating what steps an aid-recipient country should begin to take to improve its climate for private investment. We recommend language along the lines suggested on page 41 of this statement. We are pleased to note that the House 

Agriculture Committee has seen fit to adopt similar self-help criteria in the food-for-freedom legislation. 

6. The protection of industrial property rights is important to encourage invention and investment. Know-how and technology are, along with investable savings, the principal assets the United States possesses which all the rest of the world covets. In many instances today, the industrial property rights owned by a business are of greater value than the total real and personal property which the business owns.

We recommend that section 620 (e) (1) of the Foreign Assistance Act of 1961, as amended, the "Hickenlooper-Adair" amendment, should be further amended to cover specifically industrial property rights. We recommend language similar to that suggested on page 42 of this statement. 

7. We recommend that the specific risk guarantee program be amended so that losses resulting from civil disturbances and riots will be specifically covered. In many less developed countries, it is not possible to obtain coverage against these two specific risks from commercial insurance companies or if it is available, the rates are exorbitant. In countries where general political unrest is prevalent, very often foreign businesses, and particularly U.S. businesses, become the victims of riots or civil disturbances which do not amount to insurrection or revolution. In such cases, it is our understanding that the war,

revolution, and insurrection specific risk insurance of AID is not applicable. We feel that extending the coverage of this specific risk guarantee to riots and civil disturbances would help to diminish the fears and reluctance of U.S. businessmen to invest in such areas. 

8. We recommend that Congress provide funds for adequate staffing of our embassies with industrial experts at the ministerial level to accelerate private development programs in those countries where the investment guarantee program is stimulating large, new investments. 

Since this committee and the Congress amended the Foreign Assistance Act in 1963 to put greater emphasis on private sector activities, notable progress has been made. You will recall that in that year we recommended a $5 billion ceiling for investment guarantees. You were wise to increase the amount that year to $2.5 billion, and in 1965, to $5 billion.

We are glad to support the President's recommendation to raise the limit to $10 billion. We recommended in 1963, and the Congress adopted a principle that foreign aid be withheld from countries that do not sign a guarantee agreement against expropriation and incon vertibility by December 1964. Congress adopted December 1965 as the cutoff point, and last year extended it to December 1966. Several important countries have signed up since, to their own benefit, because substantial amounts of private capital have gone to those countries to help them in their industrial development. 

The President now recommends that this principle be retained in the bill but the power to exercise it should be left to the President. We concur in this recommendation and support it. 

We had recommended in 1963 that AID reduced the combined cost of specific risk guarantees from 12 to 1 percent. Action was withheld by the committee on the representation of the Administrator of AID that they would like to make some actuarial studies before recommending a change. Finally, on March 15, 1966, it was announced by AID that the combined charge will henceforth be 11% percent.

We are glad to see this progress. We hope they will reduce it further. The fact is that the combination of the Hickenlooper-Adair amendment and the guarantee program has, in fact discouraged expropriations. The new self-help criteria we recommend, plus the increase in the face-value guarantees should have the effect of increasing security of investments. This should diminish the risk and reduce the cost of the guarantees.

In conclusion, I wish to thank this committee for its courtesy and attention and to congratulate it for the constructive contributions it has made in improving the foreign aid program. It is in this spirit that we make our further suggestions, because we feel that it is only by continual improvement that the AID program will cease to be a major drain on our balance of payments, as well as on our national budget.

The rest of this statement is devoted to a more comprehensive analysis of the factual support and reasoning behind our recommendations. There is specific wording for proposed amendments. 

I hope the rest of this statement will be printed in the record as read. 

(The remainder of Mr. Danielian's statement is as follows:)

FOREIGN ASSISTANCE AND THE U.S. BALANCE OF PAYMENTS Mr. DANIELIAN. The U.S. balance-of-payments deficits continue to be a serious and vexing problem. Thanks to the cooperation of private business and banking, under the voluntary restraint program, the total U.S. deficit for 1965 was $1.3 billion compared with $2.8 billion in 1964. But the goal of the administration is to eliminate the deficit, and the necessary increase in dollar expenditures in Vietnam makes this difficult to achieve. In addition to the increased outlays for military expenditures and economic aid to Vietnam, the inflationary pressures and general prosperity of the present U.S. economy caused imports in 1965 to rise more than 15 percent, while exports rose only 4 percent. The total 1965 trade surplus was $4.8 billion compared with $6.7 billion in 1964. If imports continue to rise at a much faster pace than exports, the favorable U.S. trade surplus may diminish further, making it increasingly difficult to finance such large Government expenditures abroad. 

Despite the "tying" of U.S. bilateral aid and military offset sales, the deficits on the Government account in the U.S. balance of payments have averaged about $3.4 billion a year over the 1960-64 period, and preliminary estimates indicate that the Government balance-of-payments deficit in 1965 was $2.9 billion. (See table 1.) These figures exclude the proceeds from the sale of medium-term U.S. Government securities to foreign governments and debt prepayments of foreign governments, since these positive items are really techniques of financing the hard-core deficits resulting from U.S. Government activities abroad.

It should be noted that considered together, all private transactions in the U.S. international accounts, including exports and imports, tourism, transportation services, bank loans, direct investments and the earnings thereon, have been in balance for a long time, and in most years have resulted in a slight surplus. These surpluses, however, have not been large enough since 1949 (except for 1957) to overcome the deficits in the Government account.

Total recorded balance-of-payments relationships by major areas of the world indicate that the United States has been in approximate balance or surplus in most recent years with Europe, including all of the expenditures for imports, bank loans, porfolio investments, direct investments, shipping, tourism, and other services. However, the

United States has been in deficit in its recorded transactions with South America, Africa, Asia, including Japan, and international institutions.

The countries of Western Europe have been earning these surpluses, not in their trade and financial dealings directly with the United States, but from their trade and investment income in Asia, South America, and Africa. These are the areas where the United States is experiencing balance-of-payments deficits. These are also the places where the United States is spending the largest amounts for military presence and foreign aid.

The total Government account outflows, which average about $8 billion a year, include military expenditures, foreign aid, food-forpeace shipments, relief, and other Government expenditures abroad.

These sizable expenditures were only partially offset by the value of "tied" exports under foreign aid, Public Law 480, and the sale of military hardware. The Government's foreign accounts have, therefore, fallen consistently short of being in balance. 

The total U.S. foreign aid spending request for fiscal year 1967 continues at a level of $5.6 billion as it has in the past several years. (See table 2.) We are convinced that "tying" bilateral aid to U.S. procurement has not eradicated the substantial adverse balance-of-payments effect of aid disbursements.

The "substitution" of credit sales for cash sales resulting from "program" loans to Latin America, the Near East, and south Asian areas is an acute problem. About 90 percent of development loans (including Alliance for Progress loans) has been extended to those areas in fiscal years 1964 and 1965.¹ In fiscal year 1965, about 58 percent of the aid to these areas was in the form of "program" loans. A "program" or balance-of-payments loan permits the recipient country to use aid funds to purchase U.S. commodities in an approved classification of goods. In the absence of foreign aid, many of these products, however, would have been bought with dollars the country already owns. "Tied" assistance thus enables the aid-receiving country to use the aid funds to pay for ordinary commercial procurement from the United States and to transfer its earned dollars for purchases in other countries. This is what is meant by the substitution effect of "tied" economic assistance.

FOREIGN ASSISTANCE AND U.S. TRADE 

An examination of net nonmilitary assistance and the U.S. trade balance with selected countries which have received between 50 and 60 percent of total U.S. aid for the years 1958-64,3 shows clearly that total net U.S. economic assistance is resulting in substitution, and is financing something other than imports of U.S. commodities. (See table 3). During the period 1958-64 such assistance exceeded the trade deficits of these selected countries with the United States by an amount fluctuating between $1.1 and $1.5 billion a year.

In 1958, U.S. net economic assistance to these 25 countries amounted to $1.7 billion and the U.S. trade surplus with them was only $251 million, leaving a difference of about $1.5 billion. It is important to note that in 1964, when U.S. economic assistance was substantially "tied" to U.S. procurement, the difference between the U.S. trade surplus ($1.1 billion) and U.S. economic assistance ($2.5 billion) with these 25 countries, remained at about the same level-$1.4 billion. 

Thus, it is clear that, though "tied" aid has resulted in increased U.S. exports and a larger U.S. trade surplus with these countries, it has not improved the net difference between U.S. economic assistance and the U.S. trade surplus. This is fairly conclusive evidence that "tied" aid does not end the adverse effects of foreign aid upon the U.S. balance of payments.

In Latin America the case is quite clear. As table 4 indicates, total 

Latin American imports from all sources were up $413 million on an average during the years 1961-64, compared with the 1956-60 period. 

Between these two periods, however, total Latin American imports from the United States were down by an average of $100 million. 

This occurred in spite of the fact that total U.S. bilateral aid disbursements almost doubled from an average of $367 million during 1956-60 to an average of $652 million during 1961-64, on an increasingly "tied" basis; in spite of additional dollar disbursements of $320 million by the Inter-American Development Bank; and in spite of increased 

Latin American export earnings in the United States by an average of $220 million per year over the average for the 1956-60 period.

U.S. AID MUST BE IN REAL RESOURCES FOR PROJECTS 

In the light of these facts, the association's position is that the U.S. aid program must be reoriented to a basis of providing real resources- U.S. goods and technical services for specific incremental projects. 

Program assistance should be phased out as quickly and as completely as possible. Local costs of approved projects must be financed by the recipient governments from local revenues, not dollars. In those instances where excess local currencies are available from Public Law 480, title I sales, they should be utilized for payment of local costs of projects. In this way, the problem of substitution could be substantially eliminated.

In order to achieve this transition, we recommend the following amendments to the authorizing sections for development loans and 

Alliance for Progress loans:

A. Subsection (a) of section 201 is amended to read as follows: 

Section 201 General Authority—(a) The President shall establish a fund to be known as the "Development Fund" to be used by the President to furnish assistance pursuant to the authority contained in this Title. 

Subsection (b) of section 201 should be amended by striking out the first sentence and substituting the following language: (b) The President is authorized to make available, on credit, by purchase in the United States, any commodities, raw materials and capital equipment produced, grown or extracted in the United States and/or technical assistance from the United States for specific projects to promote the economic development of less developed countries and areas with emphasis upon assisting projects contained in a coherent, balanced, long-range economic development program. Credits extended under the authority of this Title are to be payable as to principal and interest in United States dollars on such terms and conditions as the President may determine; provided that, in no case shall the length of such dollar credits exceed 25 years, or be utilized to pay for the local labor costs connected with such projects.

B. Subsection (a) of section 251 is amended by striking out the last sentence and substituting the following language:

The President is authorized to make available, on credit, by purchase in the United States, any commodities, raw materials and capital equipment produced, grown or extracted in the United States and/or technical assistance from the United States for specific projects to promote the economic development of countries and areas in Latin America with emphasis upon assisting projects contained in a coherent, balanced, long-range economic development program. 

Credits extended under the authority of this Title, whether payable as to principal and interest in United States dollars or in local currencies of the recipient country are to be upon such terms and conditions as the President may determine; provided that, in no case shall the length of such credits exceed 25 years or be utilized for local labor costs connected with such projects. 

We must begin to tailor our aid programs to the existing U.S. ballance-of-payments position. This does not necessarily mean that we must decrease the total amount of U.S. aid but it does mean that aid must take a form that does not have an adverse impact upon the 

U.S. balance of payments.

Mr. Chairman, for the past year our association has been working on a book analyzing the U.S. postwar foreign economic experience. 

This book, entitled "The U.S. Balance of Payments-An Appraisal of U.S. Economic Strategy" contains, in chapters X and XI, details of the impact of U.S. bilateral assistance and U.S. contributions to international lending institutions upon the U.S. balance of payments over the 1945-64 period. These chapters support the facts I have summarized here. Page proofs are made available to members of the committee for their information. The book itself will be published in a week or two.

TERMS FOR EXTENDING DEVELOPMENT LOANS AND ALLIANCE FOR PROGRESS LOANS 

The present authority in sections 201 and 251 of the Foreign Assistance Act of 1961, as amended, permits the President to make loans "payable as to principal and interest in U.S. dollars on such terms

and conditions as he may determine." In actual practice, the AID agency and its predecessor agencies have extended loans on terms generally between 30 and 40 years' duration, with up to 10 years of grace period. The interest charged is usually 1 percent during the grace period and 22 percent thereafter.

The association feels that Congress should impose a statutory limit of 25 years on all development loans including Alliance for Progress loans, and on any loans which may be made under the proposed title VIII for aid to southeast Asia. The association's position is based upon the psychological reality that no political leader and no government of any less-developed country can feel any real responsibility for the repayment of loans over a 20or 30-year period, after the initial 10-year grace period. Most of them will not be here to worry about repayment. We believe that a 25-year term would create a greater sense of responsibility in the recipient government toward the economic utilization of dollar-aid funds. Tables 5 and 6 show that the United States is practically the only country that gives loans of more than 30 years' duration.

The vast majority of other OECD countries' bilateral aid is in theform of loans anywhere from 1 to 20 years.

As to conditions of foreign aid, the United States is again practically the only country which made no interest loans, with a nominal service charge of 1 percent or less. As shown in tables 7 and 8, some loans were made by other countries at 3 to 4 percent interest, but the largest amounts carried a charge of 5 percent or more.

It is clear that most other countries made capital available for shorter periods and on much harder terms than the United States. 

Incidentally, this dispels the frequent assertion that some European countries contribute a larger percentage of their gross national product to foreign aid than the United States. The comparison is not valid. Their contribution is still mainly in the form of investment rather than foreign aid as we understand the term. 

There is no quick way of remedying this situation. The U.S. 

Government has made continuing and diligent efforts to get the other OECD countries to extend more aid at lower interest rates and at longer terms than 5, 10, or 15 years. Our association urges the Government to continue working through the Development Assistance Committee (DAC) of the OECD and by direct diplomatic representation to seek to coordinate and share with the other OECD countries the burden of economic aid to developing countries. 

We feel that the whole problem of aid to the developing countries could be more equitably, efficiently, and effectively handled if specific requirements of less developed countries, in terms of capital goods for specific projects and related technical services and general technical assistance were presented to the OECD These requests could then be allocated among the developed member countries of the OECD on the basis of capacity to produce willingness to finance. Hopefully, aid would be extended by the participating OECD countries on comparable terms. We urge consideration of this idea because we feel that total assistance to less developed countries could thereby be increased, without affecting balance-of-payments relationships. 

On our part, we should change the wording of the authorizing sections of the Foreign Assistance Act to furnish only real goods and

services for specific projects, instead of money. This should help persuade other countries that there is no future in their expectation that continuing balance-of-payments loans on soft terms by the United States will continue to favor the repayment of their hard loans, and they may come around to our view.

OPEN ENDED AUTHORIZATIONS FOR CERTAIN SECTIONS AND TITLES 

In the bill proposed by the administration, we note that in a number of sections and in the proposed new title VIII, entitled "Southeast Asia Multilateral and Regional Programs," there is open ended authorization language which reads as follows:

There is hereby authorized to be appropriated to the President for each of the fiscal years 1967 through 1971 such amounts as may be necessary, which amounts are to remain available until expended. 

The association is opposed to such open ended authorizations in principle. There is no sound reason for not placing some maximal authorization limit on these sections and titles. Definite authorization limits should be provided. They can be amended if it proves necessary in the light of concrete, projected development programs and needs.

FIVE-YEAR AUTHORIZATION FOR THE FOREIGN ASSISTANCE PROGRAM 

All the titles and sections of the proposed bill, whether they have "open ended" authorizations or traditional dollar authorizations, are for 5 years covering fiscal years 1967-71. If Congress approves the bill in its present form, there will be no need for the managers of the program to appear annually before the House Foreign Affairs and 

Senate Foreign Relations Committees to justify the authorization. 

The appropriation requests could be submitted directly to the House and Senate Appropriations Committees. These committees, as a matter of congressional rule and tradition, have no jurisdiction over policy issues. Thus, the public would be foreclosed from its only forum in Congress to express its views and observations on the administration of foreign aid.

The argument, so often raised, that this is the only way to achieve long-range planning, is overemphasized. The United States has been extending foreign assistance around the world since 1945-$77 billion in economic assistance and $34 billion in military assistance over the 

1946-64 period. Is it a reasonable conclusion that the United States will suddenly, unilaterally, and capriciously terminate assistance? We think not. The historical facts refute the contention that long-range planning can not be done on an annual authorization and appropriation basis. There has been much comment in Congress about the difficulties and continual controversy attendant to the annual review of foreign aid. Responsibility and often irksome work are the price of power, and having been elected and entrusted with the power of making laws and spending the public's money, Congress could not shirk the responsibility of frequent review of programs. 

Our association opposes, in principle and practice, open-ended, multiyear authorizations for the foreign aid program.

CHANNELING OF BILATERAL AID FUNDS THROUGH MULTILATERAL INSTITUTIONS 

Our association has consistently opposed the diversion of U.S. bilateral aid funds through multilateral financial institutions. This position of our group is not based on any general opposition to multilateral institutions. We recognize that these international organizations have many advantages. We are concerned, however, that the use of this technique is designed to avoid the application of important policies already accepted by the Congress and the President in present law as conditions to bilateral aid, such as the following: (1) U.S. procurement restrictions designed to protect the U.S. balance-of-payments position; (2) The Hickenlooper amendment (sec. 620 (e) (1)), which protects 

U.S. foreign investment by providing for the cessation of U.S. bilateral aid to countries expropriating U.S. property without making prompt, "full value" compensation; (3) Congressional policy directives regarding foreign aid contained in section 620 (a) through 620 (m) which include restrictions on aid to Cuba; (4) The regular authorization and appropriation procedure for making U.S. contributions to multilateral financial institutions which provide the important opportunity for congressional review and public discussion of the effectiveness and value of their activities; and finally, (5) The policy of minimum interest rates on development loans (sec. 201(d)) and cargo preference requirements on aid financed shipments which protect the U.S. balance-of-payments position. 

We believe these are desirable conditions. We have, therefore, opposed section 205 of the Foreign Assistance Act which gives the President discretionary authority to transfer up to 15 percent of the funds appropriated for development loan funds to the International Development Association, the International Bank for Reconstruction and 

Development, or the International Finance Corporation, to be used by them, under their individual criteria and administrative rules and regulations, rather than those governing U.S. bilateral aid programs. 

We therefore oppose also the application of the section 205 principle to the Alliance-for-Progress funds. 

For the same reasons, we now oppose the much broader authority granted to the President in the proposed new title VIII, "Southeast 

Asia Multilateral and Regional Programs," specifically sections 

271(b) and 272(b).*

These sections give the President discretionary authority to channel all funds appropriated under the "open ended" authorization contained in section 273 of title VIII through any multilateral institu- 

4 Section 271. General provisions * 

"(b) The President is authorized to furnish assistance, on such terms and conditions as he may determine, in order to promote social and economic development and stability in Southeast Asia through multilateral institutions and programs and projects serving 

Section 272. Special provisions 

(b) In the event that funds made available under this Act are used by or under the supervision of the International Bank for Reconstruction and Development or the Asian

Development Bank in furtherance of the purposes of this Title, such funds may be used without regard to the requirements of this or any other act." [Emphasis added.]

tion, without regard to the requirements of the Foreign Aid Act or any other act. This is a much broader discretionary authority than is granted in section 205.

Congress very recently approved an authorization of $200 million for the newly created Asian Development Bank for fiscal years 1966-70. Congress is also expected to approve an appropriation of $120 million for fiscal year 1966 and to approve appropriations of $20 million per year during fiscal years 1967-70. If more funds are needed for the Asian Development Bank, the International Development Association, the World Bank, or any other multilateral financial institution, then authorizations and appropriations for them should be requested and considered on their merits. We should keep our bilateral and multilateral programs separate. 

Basic to our position is the fact that our studies indicate the U.S. balance-of-payments deficits are generated in our financial dealings with the Far East and southeast Asia, Latin America, and international institutions.

ECONOMIC AID TO INDIA 

The largest recipient of U.S. aid, in terms of total dollar amounts, is and has been India. Since 1948, U.S. assistance to India has amounted to approximately $7 billion, much of it since 1958. Member countries (including the United States) of the Aid India Consortium organized by the World Bank, are currently committing about $1.3 billion a year in aid to India. There is a request pending to increase this to $1.7 billion a year for the fourth 5-year plan.

In the past several years (1963-65), about 20 percent of U.S. bilateral economic aid ($435 million of $2.1 billion) and about 25 to 30 percent of Public Law 480, title I, sales of agricultural commodities for local currency, have gone to India. In addition, 69 percent of the disbursements and almost half of the total commitments of the 

International Development Association (IDA)-the "soft" loan window of the World Bank-through June 30, 1965, have been made to India (see table 9). The U.S. Government has supplied 36 percent of IDA's total capital resources and 41 percent of its convertible currency resources. Thus, it is clear that India is receiving directly or indirectly a goodly portion of U.S. bilateral aid and a substantial portion of U.S. multilateral aid. 

It is important, therefore, to evaluate India's economic progress to try and determine what U.S. and other countries' aid is achieving. 

Since 1951, India's foreign exchange reserves have dwindled from $1,697 million to $251 million in 1964. While exports (free on board) have risen from $1,146 million in 1951 to $1,749 million in 1964, imports (cost, insurance, and freight) during the same period rose from $1,165 million to $2,610 million, creating a substantial trade deficit. 

The failure of Indian agriculture to meet the food needs of the growing Indian population is painfully evident. Total grain yields (including rice) per acre in India are approximately one-quarter of that of Japan and the United Kingdom. Total grain yields (includ-

ing rice) in India averaged 296 kilograms per acre during 1901-05, 242 kilograms per acre during 1948-53 and 277 kilograms per acre from 1956 to 1960-absolute declines of 18 and 6 percent, respectively. 

From 1961 to 1963, Indian grain yields increased to an average of 305 kilograms per acre. Thus, in a 59-year period, between 1901-05 and 

1961-63, Indian yield per acre in total grains increased only 3 percent. According to the statistics of the Food and Agricultural Organization of the United Nations, Indian per capita food production in 1963 and 1964 was less than it was in 1960, 1961, and 1962, although total food production had increased slightly." The problem of agricultural development and per capita food production in India is most serious. India's population is expected to grow at an annual rate well in excess of 2 percent. From the beginning of the third 5-year plan in 1961 until the end of the fifth 5-year plan in 1976, the increase is expected to amount to $187 million. This means that in 10 years food output must expand enough to feed an additional population complement equal in size to the current population of the United States. 8 

"Increasing World Food Output," by Lester R. Brown, Foreign Agricultural Economic Report No. 25, USDA, Economic Research Service, Foreign Regional Analysis Division, 

April 1965, table 28, p. 126. 

7 "The State of Food and Agriculture," 1965, FAO, p. 217. 

8"Man, Land and Food," by Lester R. Brown, Foreign Agricultural Economic Report No. 11, USDA, Economic Research Service, Regional Analysis Division, November 1965, 

P. 14.

India's planning to date has distinctly favored Government rather than private enterprise. In the third 5-year plan, the foreign exchange requirements were $3,191 million for the public sector compared with $1,072 million for the private sector. Commodity imports from $579 million to $792 million, while the private account remained in 1963-64 displayed a distinct increase in the Government account stable. This trend increased in 1964-65, particularly in machinery imports, with the private account decreasing. Long-term loans continue to favor the Central Government of India and state-owned enterprises. In 1963-65, the Government received loans of $2,079 million, while the private nonmonetary sector received only $102.5 million. 

Both reports of the Senate and House delegations which visited India at the end of the last session of Congress, emphasized the importance of concentration on improving agricultural production and the relaxation of Indian Government controls on the private sector so that its resources could help to solve these problems. The House (Zablocki) report stated the following:

India's greatest economic problem, and the hardest to solve, is the backwardness of its agriculture. Because of ancient techniques, poor seed, and a highly seasonal distribution of rainfall, Indian productivity is among the lowest in the world. For example, the average wheat yield is 12 bushels per acre as compared with 35 in the United States.

The increase in food production during the first two 5-year plan periods (1951-61) kept slightly ahead of population growth. Since 1961, however, food output has failed to keep pace with the increase in population. Estimates are that the current year's food grain production will fall below last year's level by 10 to 12 million tons and the Government has had to introduce both voluntary and 

It has been suggested that one of the principal problems is the need for fertilizers, the increased production of which is hampered by the Indian Government's attitude toward Government control versus private control and ownership. The Indian Government should improve the investment climate, utilize more aid funds in the private sector and allow foreign investors freer access to India for internal development and the building up of the export industry. 

Too many restrictive business practices still exist, such as high tax rates on private business, limitation and allocation of imports to private industry, preemption of certain areas of the economy for future development by Government enterprises only, requirements of Indian majority control and dimunition of industrial property rights, all of which discourage much needed private foreign investment."

I do not mean to give the impression that India's development problems lie only in the agricultural sector. This is manifestly not the case, but India must first master the problem of feeding its ever-growing population. 

It is difficult to measure, statistically, India's progress or lack of it but some measuring device must be used. According to U.N. statistics, India's average annual per capita rate of growth of its real gross domestic product-its GNP-in constant prices was only 1.9 percent during the decade 1950-60; during the 3 years 1960-62 it dropped to twotenths of 1 percent.10

India's total gross domestic product increased from about $24 billion in 1953 to approximately $33 billion in 1963, or by 29 percent. Since these figures are in current prices, they are somewhat inflated and the real growth was less. Per capita gross domestic product rose from $65 in 1953 to about $74 in 1962, or about $1 per Indian citizen per year. 

Report of the Special Study Mission to the Far East, Southeast Asia, India, and 

Pakistan, Nov. 7 to Dec. 12, 1965, 89th Cong., 2d sess., p. 31. 

10 U.N. Yearbook of National Accounts Statistics, 1964, table 4B, p. 373.

It should be noted that during the same decade-1953-63-Thailand's total gross domestic product doubled, Turkey's grew by 84 percent, Republic of Korea's by 87 percent and Japan's by 209 percent from $19 to $60 billion-while, as mentioned, India's grew by only 29 percent.11 It should also be noted that the per capita increase in GNP of all the above enumerated countries was substantially greater than 

India's (see table 10).
