There are no panaceas to India's economic malaise. It is obvious that 

India must make better and far greater utilization of its resources. It also needs greater resources, particularly capital, foreign exchange, and technical know-how. India must and should be able to obtain more direct foreign investment. Unfortunately, it is a fact that total new private long-term investment in India in 1963 and 1964 was approximately $20 and $30 million, respectively, compared with public aid from all sources of approximately $1.3 billion per year. 12 President Johnson, in his foreign aid message, stated that only the people and the leaders of aid-recipient countries can "create the climate which will attract foreign investment and keep local money at home." There is much that Indian leaders can do to improve the investment climate in India. Tax rates on private business in India. (including income taxes, super taxes, and sur taxes) are quite highsomewhere between 60 and 70 percent.13 The Indian Government preempts certain areas of the economy for exclusive development by Government-owned enterprises. The requirement of Indian majority control of most foreign investments and the discriminatory allocation of foreign exchange to Government-owned enterprises-all of these policies discourage private investment. Another cloud on the horizon is the proposed patent bill which would, among other things, enable the Indian Government to- (1) Reduce the term of existing and future patents; (2) Utilize patents for governmental purposes, including publicly owned industrial enterprises, without incurring liability for infringement; and 

11 Ibid., table 6B, p. 391. 

12 Source: IMF Balance-of-Payments Yearbook, vol. 17, September 1965, India; National Advisory Council on International Monetary and Financial Problems, 9th Special Report 

(April 1962 to June 1964), p. 37. 

13 Source: Taxation-Investing in India Series, published in 1964 by the Indian Investment Centre, pp. 26-36 and pp. 96-104.

(3) Issue compulsory licenses and licenses of right without adequate remuneration related to the value of the patent. 

The Indian Government can continue to improve its investment climate by further liberalizing its attitude and policies toward private investment, both domestic and foreign. The foreign exchange which 

India would obtain from increased direct foreign investment would help ameliorate its balance-of-payments deficits and ease its foreign exchange crisis. We should see to it that India eventually transfers the greater part of the burden of Indian economic development from the taxpayers of the United States to the international investment community. 

SPECIFIC SELF-HELP CRITERIA 

This matter of improving the investment climate in India and other less-developed countries is so important that our Association recommends that Congress consider amending the authorizing sections for developing loans (sec. 201(b)), for Alliance for Progress loans (sec. 

251(b)) of the Foreign Assistance Act of 1961, as amended, and for the proposed title VIII: southeast Asia multilateral and regional programs (sec. 272), indicating what steps an aid-receiving country should take to improve its climate for private investment. We suggest something along the following lines:

In section 201 (b), delete "and" before (6) and add a new subsection (7) containing the language below, and in section 251(b) (1) delete "and" before (4), delete the period after 251(b)(1) and add the language below after "involved": "and (5) the President shall seek to encourage the recipient country to improve its climate for private investment as a necessary element in economic development by- (i) providing for maximum private ownership and initiative in new as well as existing industry, including manufacturing, financing, and merchandising; (ii) assuring nondiscriminatory treatment between national and nonnational, and between public and private, enterprises and products; (iii) providing for unrestricted repatriation of amortization of investment, interest and dividend payments; (iv) providing adequate protection for industrial property rights; (v) enacting tax laws with rates of taxation designed to promote maximum utilization of private investment capital, both foreign and domestic."

EXTENSION OF THE PROTECTION OF THE HICKENLOOPER AMENDMENT TO INDUSTRIAL PROPERTY RIGHTS 

The protection of industrial property rights is important to encourage invention and investment. Know-how and technology are, along with investable savings, the principal assets the United States possesses which all the world covets. We should not lightly throw them away.

In many instances today, the industrial property rights owned by a business are of greater value than the total real and personal property which the business owns. 

Our association recommends that section 620 (e) (1) of the Foreign Assistance Act of 1961, as amended, the "Hickenlooper" amendment, should be further amended to cover specifically industrial property

rights. This would offer some protection to U.S. businessmen who have a valuable existing property right-for example, a patent or a trademark-which by action of an aid-recipient country is eliminated, diminished, or attenuated without adequate compensation. Our organization recommends the following amendments:

Section 620 (e) (1) of the Foreign Assistance Act of 1961, as amended, should be further amended by adding a subsection (D) set forth as follows: (D) on or after January 1, 1966, has taken steps to eliminate, nullify, diminish or attenuate industrial property rights, including patents, trademarks, industrial designs, utility models, copyrights, know-how, and trade secrets of any United States citizen or any corporation, partnership or association not less than 30 per centum beneficially owned by United States citizens; has taken steps to authorize the utilization of said industrial property rights by individuals or legal persons without the holder or owner's consent and such authorization does not provide the holder or the owner with means for obtaining prompt, adequate, full-value compensation for such utilization based on development and investment costs and extent of usage, or wrongfully appropriates or transmits to third countries said knowhow and trade secrets.

The subsection should be further amended by changing the language of the next paragraph following the above insertion to read:

Upon the request of the President (within 70 days after such action referred to in subparagraphs (A), (B), (C) or (D)). * * *

This, of course, does not solve the larger and very important problem of improving and standardizing the protection of industrial property rights in the developing nations. Some very fine work of drafting a model industrial property rights law for developing countries has been done by a committee of experts to study industrial property problems of industrially less developed countries. This group met in 

Geneva in late 1964 at the invitation of the Secretariat of the Inter national Union for the Protection of Industrial Property. The com mittee was composed of 22 developing countries, including India. 

We heartily endorse this work and feel that our State Department should continue to give its assistance and encouragement to this effort. 

AID GUARANTEE PROGRAM 

I would like to comment on the investment guarantee program. 

You will recall that in 1963 we recommended reduction of the cost of combined specific risk guarantees to 1 percent. Our association is very pleased that AID has recently reduced the premium for combined specific risk guarantee coverage from 12 to 1% percent. This should encourage more U.S. investments to go abroad at a smaller initial cost. We still hope that the cost may be further reduced to 1 percent. 

We note that the language of section 620 (1), providing for a cessation of U.S. assistance to countries which have not entered into a bilateral guarantee agreement with the United States by December 31, 1966, against the risks of expropriation and inconvertibility, has been amended to leave the invocation of this principle to the discretion of the President. Our association supports this amendment. 

The original section has achieved its purpose; there are now about 69 countries which have entered into such a bilateral guarantee agreement with the United States.

It is to be hoped that those countries which have not yet signed or effectuated a guarantee program will do so because, as demonstrated

in the case of Brazil, the result will surely be a substantial inflow of U.S. direct investment, which will increase the level of employment and productivity, the tax collections and the foreign exchange earnings of these countries.

The guarantee program has proved to be a very successful instrument for increasing the flow of private capital to the less developed countries. After much thought and discussion within our policy committees, we concluded that the specific risk investment guarantee program would be much improved if civil disturbances and riots were covered under AID's guarantee program. In many less developed countries it is not possible to obtain from commercial insurance companies coverage against these two specific risks, or if it is available the rates are exorbitant. In countries where general political unrest is prevalent, very often foreign businesses become the focal point of riots or civil disturbances which do not amount to insurrection or revolution. In such cases, it is our understanding that the war, revolution and insurrection specific risk insurance of AID is not applicable. We feel that extending the coverage of this specific risk guarantee to riots and civil disturbances would help to diminish the fears and reluctance of U.S. business to invest in such areas. We recommend this for the committee's consideration.

We are pleased to note that the President has recommended that the authorized face value of investment guarantees be increased to $10 billion. In 1963 we had recommended $5 billion. The Congress then approved a limit of $2.5 billion, raising it to $5 billion in 1965. 

We are glad to see this new initiative on the part of the administration, and support it. 

We hope that the necessary administrative actions will be taken to provide the increased personnel that such an expanding program will require. I am sure the revenues from the fees would justify such an expansion.

One phase of the administrative problem is to provide top-level industrial experts at the ministerial level in our embassies abroad in those countries where this investment guarantee program is bringing large, new investments to spur the private development programs. Certainly, this function of harnessing private investment in economic development is at least equally as important as Government aid. 

Before I conclude the oral part of the statement, Mr. Chairman, I would like to take this opportunity to present to you and to the committee through you a copy of a book which we have just printed on the U.S. balance of payments, an appraisal of U.S. economic strategy. Chapters 10 and 11 of this book deal with the effect of the foreign aid program both national and international on the U.S. balance of payments.

The book is not yet published. It will be published actually to the public about June 8. Therefore, in the meantime, the committee may be able to use it as part of your library. 

Chairman MORGAN. Thank you, Dr. Danielian. We will certainly place it in our library.. 

Mr. DANIELIAN. Not in the record, I hope, sir; in the library. 

Chairman MORGAN. Dr. Danielian, I see that you are critical of the

Indian Government. On page 7 of your statement, you state the 

Indian Government's policies and attitudes continue to be unsympathetic to the private sector both foreign and domestic. I have a copy

here, part 1 of the hearings of our committee on the pending foreign aid legislation. On page 33 the Secretary of State said:

They are trying to make it clear that- I am speaking of the Government of Indiathey are trying to make it clear there is an improved atmosphere for private investment and that they are interested in private investment. 

I wonder, Dr. Danielian, what basis you have for your statement that appears to contradict the statement made by the Secretary of 

State in his appearance before this committee?

Mr. DANIELIAN. I am glad to comment on this. There is some misunderstanding, some misinterpretation of what is going on, not by the Secretary; I think the Secretary has been told, I am sure, of the few instances where exceptions to policy have been made. I think, in the case of the fertilizer industry, particularly, there has been a tendency on the part of the Government of India in the past several months to permit investments in which they still would like to have 51-percent control, but they are willing to make arrangements where necessary to allow Indian and foreign private investors to have more than 49 percent.

Our investigations indicate that this is an exception to policy in the field of these industries which have to do with agriculture. The policy has not changed. Any reading of the discussions going on in the 

Indian Parliament, and also in the Congress Party proceedings in 

Jaipur recently would show that the Congress Party leadership is not completely reconciled even with these exceptions. 

Chairman MORGAN. Dr. Danielian, we certainly want to thank you.

I have been impressed with your analysis of the failure of the Indian Government to move along with their agricultural program. I think that something must be done to stimulate agricultural production in India. I am glad you have submitted a comparison of Indian production with South Korea, Pakistan, Thailand, the Philippines, and

Turkey. I feel that in considering further requests of aid for India, that we should have assurance that greater effort will be made by that country to increase its agricultural capabilities. 

The Indian Government, in my opinion, has been giving too much emphasis to the industrial field and has been somewhat negligent in agricultural production.

Mr. DANIELIAN. I hope very much, Mr. Chairman, they will take your advice. 

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Adair. 

Mr. ADAIR. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

Mr. Danielian, in your comment upon the emergency food aid to

India, I would like to invite your further comment upon one aspect of it. As I am sure you know, one of the ingredients of this aid is supposed to be tobacco. And several people, including myself, are somewhat at a loss to understand how that is going to feed hungry people in India at this time. 

Mr. DANIELIAN. India, I believe, is the second largest tobacco-producing country in the world. I share your inability to understand some of the anomalies of our aid program. But I am unable to comment on it, because I do not know what are the considerations that lead to including items such as tobacco in the foreign aid program.

Mr. ADAIR. This matter has been brought to the attention of the 

Secretary of Agriculture, and I can assure you that he has as yet to my knowledge failed to give very reasonable excuse as to why- Mr. DANIELIAN. It is said it saves foreign exchange. But I don't suppose India is importing tobacco from abroad, spending foreign exchange; for that would be hard to understand when we have a shortage of foreign exchange, and in light of the fact that they can produce their own tobacco. 

Mr. ADAIR. You make reference to the guarantee program. Are you familiar with the efforts that are being made now in AID to enlarge the extended coverage aspect of the guarantees? 

Mr. DANIELIAN. Yes; I think they have recommended an increase in the extended-coverage face-value limit, I believe, from $300 to $425 million.

Mr. ADAIR. Would you think that might meet some of the questions you have raised about specific quarantees in the field of civil disorders? Would you think that the extended guarantee section ought to be enlarged to go into that field?

Mr. DANIELIAN. My impression of the extended-risk guarantee program has been used in cases where some vital project was involved which the administration and the President agreed was necessary for a country, and they could not find adequate financing without this guarantee.

I don't think it would cover a wide range of investments which really is diversification of investments by private people and their profit motive will be the motivation for economic development. I don't think that the extended-risk guarantee program would be available in such instances.

Mr. ADAIR. You would feel then that we should perhaps make a fourth category of specific guarantees to cover civil insurrections?

Mr. DANIELIAN. It can be made a part of the war revolution and insurrection section. You recall when there were riots in Panama, for example, many of the offices of American companies were destroyed as in Venezuela, when Sears, Roebuck facilities were severely damaged.

Somehow there seems to be a fear in Congress that this will get the guarantee program too far afield. 

Mr. ADAIR. That is correct.

Mr. DANIELIAN. Particularly it might lend itself to fraud. Then we must face up to the fact that scaring private capital is one of the principal ways of delaying economic development in these countries. 

It is obvious that these riots are stimulated by leftist leaders which use mob action to attack American or foreign property. This makes it rather difficult for a vice president in charge of foreign operations to sell his board of directors on a substantial investment in some of these countries where such incidents have happened. 

Mr. ADAIR. You touched upon a couple of these. Could you mention, for the record, perhaps three or four instances where American private property, business property, has been destroyed or damaged in a riot?

Mr. DANIELIAN. I will be glad to supply that instead of going upon my memory at the moment.

Mr. ADAIR. Mr. Chairman, could we have that then included in the record at this point? 60-929-66-pt. 5—9

Chairman MORGAN. Without objection, it is so ordered. 

(The information follows:) 

COUNTRIES IN WHICH PROPERTY OF U.S. BUSINESS HAS BEEN DESTROYED AS A RESULT OF CIVIL DISTURBANCES OR RIOTS SINCE 1960 VENEZUELA 

1 In the early 1960's, Dupont and Sears, Roebuck & Company were severely damaged as a result of civil disturbances. 

COLOMBIA 

1 In 1963, Sears, Roebuck & Company was burned in a riot in Cali.

PANAMA¹ 1 

In January 1964, the property of Pan American Airways, Braniff International, and the Goodyear Tire & Rubber Company plus a number of other small American investors, was severely damaged or destroyed as a result of riots. 

In both Indonesia and Egypt there have been riots and civil disturbances during the last two years in which U.S. Government property was damaged or destroyed but it has not been ascertained that as an incident to these civil disturbances, any property owned by U.S. business was damaged. 

Mr. ADAIR. I have one other question.

You spoke about a reduction possibly in program loans. If we reduce that type of loan substantially or phased it out, would it result in an increase in cash purchases by those countries now receiving those loans, or a decrease; or what effect would you think it would have?

Mr. DANIELIAN. There are the commercial purchases, looking at it from the other country's point of view, imports purchased in the United States. Many of these countries earn dollars here through their exports to the United States. They have in the past used these funds in large part in purchasing supplies from the United States. 

This has been particularly true of South America. The program loans actually have given them additional-through letters of creditbalance-of-payments assistance. There are certain exceptions of products that they cannot charge against these letters of credit, usually luxury goods and some other prohibited items. 

For the rest they can charge anything they procure in the United States. What we observe is that now they are using the credit to charge their regular purchases in the United States to pay for them, and the foreign exchange that they earn through their exports to the United States are more readily available to spend in Europe or Japan and other countries. 

Mr. ADAIR. In other countries, and for other purposes? 

Mr. DANIELIAN. Right. The question you ask is, If we change from program loans to project loans will we retain the markets, usual purchases, and then make the contributions supplemental to the ordinary imports for specific developmental projects? Of course, we cannot control the import and export flow in these countries. They do, but not we. They have special licensing procedures not only for each product, but from the countries from which they can buy. They control it. We cannot from this side. And whether or not they will continue buying their ordinary commercial purchases in the United States will depend certainly on at least two factors. 

One is the price competitiveness of our products, and second, the willingness of their government to grant import licenses. However, 1 Source: U.S. State Department.

if we are there to give them, say, $100 million for project development, whether it is housing, schools, factories, for that matter, hydroelectric projects, and we say to them "We are willing to finance the foreign exchange component of this economic development project, specifically approved and engineered, provided you buy the additional equipment, generators, motors, anything else that you may require, pumps, boilers, in the United States," we have at least assured ourselves the development funds appropriated by the Congress are being used for economic development. We will be assured that they are not being used to sustain current consumption of imports or to sustain nonproductive employees in some of the deficit-ridden industries and departments of the government.

If I could be sure that we could set a condition that in exchange for that $100 million we are willing to give you for your further economic development, we expect that there will be no capricious changes in the allocation of your foreign exchange discriminating against the United States, and that we are going to retain our normal percentage of your trade, then the answer to your question would be "Yes." 

Mr. ADAIR. Thank you.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Roybal. 

Mr. ROYBAL. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

Mr. Danielian, I was very much interested in the statement you made with regard to India. This is due to the fact that the day before yesterday I had the pleasure of having lunch with the Minister of Planning for India. He, of course, disagrees with everything you have said with regard to his country. He told us of food production in India which has increased substantially. But he says, above all, that there is a change in attitude. 

He said plans that his Government has now accepted would not have even been presented to his Government as little as 3 years ago. He was in the United States to try to encourage more private investment. 

If this change is a reality, would you still take the position that we should not commit large amounts of aid money to that country? 

Mr. DANIELIAN. First as to facts. The Minister of Planning is correct, that total agricultural production has increased, I think from about 15 million tons of wheat in-sometime in the early fifties, 1956, to about 18 million tons just before the drought. My figures refer to per-acre production, which I think is a better measure of technological improvement in this particular sector of the economy. 

With regard to the encouragement of the private investment, I accept his statement that in the field of fertilizer there has been some improvement, not altogether complete yet. I heard before the House

Agriculture Committee the president of a company that is setting up a plant there for fertilizer production, and he said a plant that would have taken him 18 months to build here is going to take him 612 years to build in India, and it won't be ready until the middle of next year. 

This is due to the redtape, controls, and requirements of the Indian Government. It is for us somewhat difficult to understand, the complexities of the procedures in an admittedly and professedly socialized society that the Congress Party is trying to create in India. If they move in the direction of greater freedom and initiative, should we continue to give substantial aid? That will depend, I think, on other considerations besides the welfare of India.

First we have to answer, What is the stake of the United States in India? Perhaps this is not the arena to raise this and I am not the authority to express a valid opinion on that subject, but you have to answer this question for yourselves because a commitment of a billion or more dollars a year definitely means tens of billions over the years.

This problem of India is not going to be solved in 10 years or even 

20 years. So you are really confronting a problem of, Are you going to make a major investment of U.S. national resources in India over the next 10, 20, 30 years? 

Mr. ROYBAL. I think you have already given me the answer to the question I was asking when you said that where it takes us 18 months, it takes them 6 years. This means they need help, technical assistance, the various other types of assistance that we have been making available to other underdeveloped countries of the world. 

Therefore, if this is the situation, and since there is this change of attitude, I believe that we should continue to help this endeavor of theirs. It will take 6 years for them to build on this new plan, but perhaps within 10 years India will not need the tremendous amount of money that is being placed at their disposal under this particular bill.

Mr. DANIELIAN. The technical help has been available. These people have taken six and a half years to build a plant yet they are technically very qualified. They have just been hamstrung by one impediment or another. I would like to complete my answer further.

If in the best judgment of this committee and the Congress you decide to continue aid, having answered the question of where our national interest lies, if you decide to continue aid at substantial levels, I would say that India more than any other country deserves the application of the requirements of section 202, that 50 percent of the development loan funds be devoted to economic development through private enterprise.

I think if more capital funds were available in that direction, and some of the controls were eliminated, you are going to see much faster economic development than under the present type controls of the 

Government sector. 

Mr. ROYBAL. According to the Minister of Planning this is not only now being studied by the Government of India, but they are definitely going into a new program, a new approach to government. I think these matters of redtape which you have been talking about are the things that the Minister of Planning is going to attack. If this comes to pass, then the situation which you are now talking about would lend itself to the possibility that we can continue to aid that country so that they can help themselves. 

Mr. DANIELIAN. If they would move in that direction, I think they would be favoring themselves, and of course improving the utilization of the capital resources that they received. 

Mr. ROYBAL. Then you would support our investment in aid to that country, if they did move in that direction?

Mr. DANIELIAN. We have suggested specific self-help criteria in our statement and I hope the committee will incorporate it in the bill, and if these self-help criteria are really applied by U.S. Government administrators we will be much more favorably inclined toward the continuance of the aid program to India.

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Gross. 

Mr. GROSS. Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

Mr. Danielian, I want to commend you for a very well thought out statement and the suggestions you have made to this committee, with which I agree in a large measure. I am glad you pointed out the costs of this foreign aid are well beyond the three billion four hundred million as set forth in the two bills presently before the committee. 

I also want to commend you for pointing out the disappointing results of the international development program in relation to procurement and commend you also for advocating a substantially reduced time period for these Government loans.

I do want to take exception to your suggestion that the risk guarantees be extended to riots and civil disturbances. I think we would be going off the deep end to go that far in protecting investors in foreign countries against riots and civil disturbances. We don't do that in this country. We had a Watts situation in Los Angeles and from the pictures that I have seen there is still much of the rubble and destruction visible there. Apparently investors are slow to go back into Watts.

I imagine this is true in other places where we have had riots in this country. We don't guarantee our domestic investors. I suspect the insurance coverage in the Watts area of Los Angeles would be prohibitive.

I simply can't agree with you on that suggestion for extension of the guarantee program.

I believe that is all, Mr. Chairman, unless the gentleman cares to comment.

Mr. DANIELIAN. I think we have got to make a distinction here between ordinary risks and political risks. It would be my impression that the guarantee program in the aid legislation is designed to cover political risks. I think that many of the riots in other countries are particularly subject to incitement from agents from abroad and are in the category of political risks, and they are designed to slow down or frighten capital.

They are really in the form of sabotage of their economic development programs. I view private investment—and I must really clarify this point to keep my own record clear-we have always favored foreign aid. We have tried to channel it toward more constructive use of funds and we have tried to encourage private participation in this program because we believe that it is more economical and more efficient to stimulate the initiative of people in accomplishing the purposes of foreign economic development of these countries. 

So we view the guarantee programs as part and parcel of foreign aid. 

We just would like to encourage more private capital participation so we could reduce the taxpayer participation in the program. When a USIA office in some country is sacked and burned, the taxpayer loses 100 percent of that investment. The same thing would happen to any project that was built by U.S.-taxpayer funds. 

When you guarantee the private investment against some of these funds and you spread this over many countries and you charge a fee for it, my expectation is that it isn't going to cost anything to the Government. I think in the few instances where they have to pay it will be covered by the insurance fees.

Mr. GROSS. Mr. Danielian, I doubt that you could have a riot or a civil disturbance anywhere without political implications or political overtones. I simply say that I think to extend it to coverages of this kind with all the rioting and civil disturbances there are round the world today, including Latin America, all of Central and South America, that you would be getting into a pretty expensive operation, a pretty dangerous operation to attempt to do this. 

I think, too, speaking of stimulating other countries, I would hope that the day would come when we wouldn't have to spend billions of dollars a year to cause other countries to demonstrate incentive and to provide their own stimulus. This is just beyond my comprehension. 

As far as I know we are still stimulating Norway and one or two other Scandinavian countries to put some money into their own expenditures for defense purposes. This to me is beyond comprehension that we have to, at this late date, after all the money we have expended on them, still stimulate their interest in their own defense. They are closer to Russia than we are. 

Thank you.

Chairman MORGAN. Thank you, Dr. Danielian. 

Mr. ADAIR. Mr. Chairman, could I make a unanimous consent request?

Chairman MORGAN. Mr. Adair. 

Mr. ADAIR. I would like to have included in the record, and I suppose it would be had either from State or AID, the pipeline by country and function showing unliquidated obligations as of June 30, 1965, and estimated unexpended balances, June 30, 1966.

And third, I would like to have AID personnel in Vietnam broken down by contract personnel, direct hire and local, and further broken down by those assigned in Saigon and those assigned in the provinces.

Chairman MORGAN. Our next witness is Dwight D. Townsend, rep resenting the Cooperative League of the United States of America. 

Mr. Townsend, you have a prepared statement, and you may proceed, sir.

STATEMENT OF DWIGHT D. TOWNSEND, DIRECTOR, COOPERATIVE LEAGUE OF THE UNITED STATES 

Mr. TOWNSEND. Thank you, Mr. Chairman.

It is with considerably more assurance and eagerness that we approach this witness table today to discuss with you, as we have done in other years, the foreign aid program and the relation that the Cooperative League of the United States bears to it. I use the terms "greater assurance" and "eagerness" advisedly, because we have had another year during which our organization was privileged to play

a deeper, more meaningful role in this program than has been possible for us in the past.

Therefore, what I am going to tell is based on experience in a variety of the phases of our wide-ranging effort to help the people of other countries realize the promises of life which the 20th century—often in tantalizing fashion-is making to them. 

The first area to which I would direct your attention is one closest to us-Latin America, from which our executive director has just returned. He attended the executive board meeting of the Organization of the Cooperatives of America. He is a former colleague here, Jerry Voorhis.

Two of the chief goals of the U.S.-Latin American policy are being aided through the operations of this organization. First, OCA helps to forge a deep unity between the peoples of the Northern and Southern Americas because it binds their peoples' institutions; namely, their cooperatives, together in one hemispheric organization. 

Second, OCA is hastening the day of strong, independent, national economies in Latin America as it builds economically sound cooperatives and strong cooperative sectors of national economies. I am sure that all of you realize that these developments do not occur minus the evolution of a middle class, an objective long desired by the United 

States in Latin America. 

The charter of OCA had its inception in the United States in the Humphrey amendment to the Foreign Assistance Act of 1961, which, by the way, Mr. Chairman, I have a copy at hand, and I am sure it will be made available to all the members of the committee on request, just published March 21, and it is titled "Technical and Economic 

Assistance to Cooperative Enterprises Under the Foreign Assist ance Act of 1961."

Chairman MORGAN. We have copies. 

Mr. TOWNSEND. Thank you, sir. When OCA was being organized 5 years ago in Bogotá, Vice 

President Humphrey, then a Senator, delivered what has proved to be a prophetic speech about the role of cooperatives in the developing world. At one point he stirred the entire conference when he said: 

Remember cooperatives belong to the people, they serve the people, they are economic democracy in action. They are a free people's response*** to collectivization. Cooperatives are real economic freedom. 

It was not long after this that the Cooperative League, under contract with AID, began to give technical assistance to existing cooperatives in a number of Latin American countries and to groups wishing to form cooperatives. It provided OCA with five Latin fieldmen to aid in establishing substantive programs and carry on OCA's membership drive. With funds from AID and from the Cooperative 

League, OCA is now publishing in two languages-and, by the way, last week was authorized to publish in a third language, now English, Portuguese, and Spanish, a magazine called "Cooperative America,' which circulates throughout the hemisphere.

When we extended a helping hand to the countries south of us, we found that there was a great deal to be done. There were approximately 16,000 cooperatives with between 5 and 6 million members, and only 10 of the 16 countries which met to organize OCA had any type

of national federation of cooperatives. Adequate laws regulating cooperatives were the exception and supervision of cooperative activity was very casual.

So the actual majority of these cooperatives, to be reckoned "true cooperatives" as that term is understood, needed fundamental overhauling.

To address these formidable problems, we have begun programs of training for members and workers, in the first instance, to insure job skills essential to carrying out those purposes. We are bringing to the International Cooperative Training Center in Madison, Wis., a growing number of Latin Americans for specific training that will equip them to cope with their particular problems. 

In this connection, I should add, Mr. Chairman, that we are also training groups of Peace Corps men as cooperative organizers, and they are at work in such countries as Colombia and Panama. The 

OCA fieldmen and Peace Corps volunteers work in collaboration with the AID missions. 

OCA's progress is suggested by the fact that in October 1963, only 6 of the existing national cooperative confederations in Latin America belonged to OCA; yet, by October 1965, that number had climbed to 15. 

In 12 countries there are councils or committees working with OCA fieldmen on mutual problems of cooperative organization. 

Furthermore, OCA personnel have discovered the need for a cooperative adviser as a normal part of the AID mission in most of the development countries. Experience has shown us that this would improve the quality of cooperation with the AID missions. 

Closely related to OCA and its objectives is a program designed to provide finances for Latin America's cooperatives. One of the most basic needs of developing countries is available resources of capital. 

AID and the Inter-American Cooperative Bank Development pro gram are establishing cooperative banking institutions. They are bringing together resources of capital and banking knowledge and making these available to cooperative institutions. This means supervised credit for important grassroots business organizations such as marketing and farm supply cooperatives, consumer cooperative organizations, et cetera. This program is designed to assist the aid missions in their efforts to strengthen the cooperative finance structure of their countries. It helps to mobilize domestic savings and channel them toward economic growth. The program proposes to provide the following services: 

1. Technical assistance in the organization and operation of banks for cooperatives. 

2. Consultation on the preparation of standards and procedures. 

3. Auditing services. 

4. Financing, including discounting of securities, acting as grantor, and serving as fiduciary.

Experts in the field of cooperative banking have recommended a system of cooperative banks with a central institution which could supplement the activities of national banks. To date three banks and one credit union federation have joined the system. Feasibility surveys for banks have been completed in four additional countries and a further one is underway in Bolivia.

May I say in summary, Mr. Chairman, before turning to another part of the world, that there has been measurable and encouraging progress in the field of cooperative development in Latin America. 

We feel that our rate of progress will accelerate even more when the staffs of AID both in the field and in Washington include more key personnel with a cooperative background who can understand from their own experience the problems confronting those seeking to promote cooperative development. Such success as we have had has been greatly facilitated by AID staff people who do have understanding of this area of economic development. 

In September 1965, upon the initiative of the Danish Government, there was held in Fredensborg, Denmark, an international conference on cooperative assistance to developing countries. The particular significance of this conference lay in the fact that it included government agencies, international organizations such as the Interna tional Labor Organization, the Food and Agriculture Organization, and private cooperative organizations such as the Cooperative League of the United States.

Here was recognition that all these groups play an important role in whatever is accomplished toward fostering the cooperative idea in the underdeveloped sectors of the world. Our country was represented by Mr. Frank Coffin, from Government, and Mr. Thomas Keehn, of the 

Cooperative League, from the private sector. 

This conference in Denmark had as its central purpose to explore ways that the resources of both governments and private cooperative organizations can be devoted to the common purpose of helping with the economic advancement of less developed nations through the use of the cooperative technique. 

The need for coordination of available resources and agencies is vividly illustrated by what is happening in the United States-just one, but the most important one-of the countries equipped to play a part in this task of cooperative development. In a remarkably brief span of years there have come into existence here the International Cooperative Training Center, to which I referred earlier at Madison, Wis.; the International Cooperative Development Association; the

Fund for International Cooperative Development, and other entities with programs and objectives which must be coordinated. To avoid duplication and to insure that maximum use is made of what each country contributes, this international coordination is imperative. 

Before concluding my statement, Mr. Chairman, I would like to state briefly some general observations about major features in the revised program of foreign aid which you are considering. Some of the new features have been described as new initiatives and this is an accurate description.

For example, we applaud the decision of the President to propose a 5-year authorization for economic aid which will let those who must implement programs plan ahead on more than a hand-to-mouth basis. 

From our experience in dealing with cooperative leaders in other countries, we know that the quality of their cooperation deepens and their interest increases when they know you are talking to them about a program with continuity and not something you may have to end after a year or two.

In granting this latitude, Congress will continue to review the program, in effect, when it considers on an annual basis the appropriations requests. It will continue to attempt to insure that funds reach the individual recipient by as direct a route as sound administration allows. 

On another point, we are glad the President is drawing a sharper line of delineation between economic and military assistance programs through the submission of separate authorization bills for them. What we say in this testimony, as you will understand, refers exclusively to the economic aspect of foreign aid. We are not qualified to present expert or detailed testimony about military requirements, which is quite another subject, and it is a good thing it is being set apart from the economic phase of foreign aid so that each can be examined on its separate merits or lack thereof.

One ambitious project we have undertaken is to develop in four Latin American countries, cooperatives among the artisan and handicraft workers of the mountain villages, to develop also central marketing cooperative companies in each of these countries for the assembling of the handicraft and for their sale in the United States and elsewhere. 

Our objective is to get for these handicraft workers a fair and decent price for the beautiful work which they so often do. Thus there will be more money for the very poorest people in these four countries, and it will be a direct result of the relationship between these people in Latin America and their sister cooperative organizations in the United States, in this case, the Cooperative League. 

I would like to say there is now more money, and this is now a direct result of this relationship. It has really come into being in the last week or so, a realization of some of this being accomplished. 

In Ecuador, for example, a little town of 15,000 population has orders on hand for more than twice of their budget, and it makes them very happy, of course.

The scale of the foreign aid appropriations being sought, apart from Vietnam, is at the lowest point in recent U.S. history. In fact, it is less than 2 percent of the Federal budget and only one-third of 1 percent of the gross national product. When we consider the returns we are seeking for this small extraction from the American cornucopia, it is a small enough price to pay for the chance it offers for a more stable and orderly world.

The President has said this is a way "to attack the root causes of of world poverty." If that is our noble goal, we must match the resonance of our words with an outlay large enough to make them ring with conviction.

Just now when some other prosperous countries such as Japan, West Germany, and Switzerland are showing increasing interest in helping with the job our foreign aid program is attempting, it is not a time for us to retrench on it ourselves and seem to signal to them that we think the need-instead of increasing as it is-may be on the wane. 

Such a course would be tragically shortsighted and one for which we may be called on to pay in a treasure not so easily replaced as the dollars we extract from our growing GNP. 

The emphasis on self-help in the current foreign assistance message from the White House was never spelled out more eloquently

nor spoken more sincerely. Cooperatives are one of the oldest and most reliable of all forms of economic self-help. They carry with them additional values in the social and human dimensions of man's life. 

They increase human dignity. Cooperatives, by their very nature, give support to the conviction that "man does not live by bread alone." 

Through cooperatives we choose realistically to help those who do the best job and show the most interest in helping themselves. AID has long supported the self-help philosophy. The AID Administrator has pointed out that the 20 countries receiving the bulk of funds from U.S. economic assistance programs spend $6 on their own development for each $1 we supply. This, I think, was testified to earlier this morning, amounting to about 17 percent, a 6-to-1 ratio.

This does not seem like much, Mr. Chairman, until you consider how desperately poor these countries are and what an effort it represents for them to do even this much. Self-help must be a watchword for aid programs and not a catchword because it is conceived in good sense and makes the best yardstick quickly available to determine where our aid dollars will do the most good.

Among the final features of the program on which I would comment is one of the most crucial-agriculture. When coupled with the 

President's food-for-freedom program, the $512 million AID proposes to spend in the agricultural sector takes on new significance. In a generally leaner AID budget this figure represents, wisely we think, an increase of one-third over fiscal year 1965. We would point out that this money is to be spent largely to export our know-how-farming techniques, the use of fertilizer and pesticides, extension services, cooperatives, and sound systems of farm credit. They are the ingredients in the miracle of modern America's farm productivity. Conversely, a backward agriculture is at the root of the depression, despair, and civil unrest which afflict so many of the developing countries. It is far better to export our expertise than to ship products of our own farm abundance.

The second is quickly consumed while the first takes root in the basic economy and brings returns year after year.

In addition, the food-for-freedom program helps our own farmers by providing a way to get at badly needed new markets. 

In other appearances before you on this subject the Cooperative League has spelled out in detail how the agricultural economy of India, to take an example, would be greatly helped by the development of self-liquidating fertilizer plants. With the food-for-freedom proposal before this session of Congress and famine threatening anew in India, it would be timely for our leaders to take a fresh look at this suggestion which we have been proposing in specific terms since 1963. Here is how it would work:

To begin with, we must face frankly that lack of initial development capital in the overseas areas can become the bottleneck dooming many promising proposals before they ever get started.

As an example, cooperatives have been involved in the fertilizer business a long time, so by now we think we know something about it. 

Some time ago some of our directors presented the idea for building cooperative fertilizer plants in developing countries by means of $100

million in loans or stock investments. They suggested that the basic criteria for feasibility include (1) need; (2) available raw material; and (3) use of, if and when possible, the local currencies generated through Public Law 480 to pay the cost of building these plants. 

They estimate that $100 million would build at least 10 new plants which would produce 3,000 tons of anhydrous ammonia per day. 

Agronomists tell us that $1 worth of nitrogen applied where nitrogen is a limiting factor, will produce $4 in additional crops. Interpreted in the light of this specific suggestion, the fertilizer produce under this program with a value of $270,000 per day could create new foodstuffs totaling $1,080,000 per day. Fertilizer produced in 320 workdays per year could create new wealth valued at $345 million in 1 year with a $100 million investment.

These plants would yield about 30 percent on their sales and would, therefore, pay for themselves in approximately 3 years of operation. 

Through this technique the United States investments in fertilizer plants on a loan basis would increase the farmer's income on wheat, corn, and other products very substantially, putting them in a better position to pay their own way economically. In the long run these investments could repay themeslves with interest while performing a national and international service. 

The key to increased food production is increased use of fertilizer. 

In our own country the best kind of fertilizer development we have had has been the farmer-owned cooperative fertilizer plants which now dot our countryside and supply about a quarter of all fertilizer used on U.S. farms.

The same benefits should be spread to other countries. At least a large segment of the fertilizer plants should be so developed that in a few years they will belong to and be managed by the farmers of those countries themselves. 

I think, to quote our previous league president, Mr. Murray Lincoln, who said "This is the place where we ought to find ways to gracefully disengage ourselves in the foreign aid program." 

This can be done with the know-how of U.S. competitive representatives if the basic capital can be supplied through the foreign aid program. Nothing we can think of would contribute more or in a better way to combating world hunger.

We urge the committee to provide for this. 

We urge the committee, too, to write a legislative record in favor of the development of both national and international banks for cooperatives on the pattern of our own Farm Credit System and thus to strengthen a program now being worked at in Latin America by the 

Cooperative League and the Fund for International Cooperative De velopment, under contract with AID.

We urge the committee to write into the bill a directive that in each AID mission there be at least one officer who knows cooperation and cooperatives and mutual economic action. In some mission such men are present. In others there is no one at all with such qualifications, which can lead to indifference or misunderstanding. And, I might add, even some degree of hostility. 

If we do not include here our own views on the impact of the population explosion on this subject, it is not lack of interest, but because we

feel no special competence in this area. Even without expert knowledge, we are convinced that unless the population explosion is defused this threat will make a mockery of the best conceived food program directed by the wisest of men.

I would like to take a moment of the committee's time, Mr. Chairman, to describe some specific things the cooperatives of the United States are doing in this area on their own. The members of the organizations which make up the league now have their own program to give direct financial impetus to the achievement of the food-forfreedom objectives. They call it Worldwide Co-Op Partners.

Through it, using the league as their clearinghouse, members are giving individually thousands of dollars annually of their own money. 

This money is used particularly in doing those things out of reach of Government efforts for administrative or other reasons having to do with expenditure of public funds. But American cooperators do not limit their contribution to money. It is constantly inspiring to see how the members of U.S. cooperatives give of their time, travel long distances, and open up their homes to give visitors from abroad a taste of American home life. 

Mr. Chairman, at a membership meeting of the Cooperative League on February 7, 1966, a great deal of discussion revolved around our responsibility in connection with the world food crisis and the foreign aid program. The following is an excerpt from the minutes of that meeting: 

In addition to our standing resolution in support of the social and economic portion of the Foreign Aid Program, the Cooperative League urges specific sup port to that part of Foreign Aid, related to: Food for Peace, Freedom from Hunger and programs against famine and poverty in all 54 countries now receiving USA aid. Categorically we should support: 

1. Provision of food where needed to alleviate starvation with adequate payment to American farmers. 

2. Assistance to recipient countries for food handling via transportation, storage, processing and distribution. 

3. Assistance to developing countries in increasing their own productionthrough the expertise of extension agents, experienced farmers, farm machinery, irrigation, improved seeds, insecticides and fertilizers. 

4. The size of this effort should be on sufficiently long range terms in whatever phases of each country that can absorb usefully such assistance. 

Following these four points, a motion was made to amend the resolution to include a proposal to try to obtain equity capital for "fertilizer plants in India and like countries." 

The resolution was approved unanimously.

This concludes my formal statement. I want to again thank the committee for hearing me and to repeat our willingness to be of whatever further service that may be required of the Cooperative League of the United States of America. 

Thank you. 

Chairman MORGAN. Thank you, Mr. Townsend. I think you have a very good statement here. You have made some excellent suggestions that the committee will consider during the markup of the bill. 

I will not ask specific questions, because time is short. We have a rollcall in progress on the floor, and the Members are going to have to leave.

Mr. Roybal.

Mr. ROYBAL. Thank you, Mr. Chairman. 

I do not have any questions. I would like to commend you for the statement you have made. I think the recommendations are all excellent. The one, however, that I liked most of all was your recommendation that we write into the bill a directive that each AID mission have an officer who knows cooperation and cooperatives, and mutual economic action.

Mr. TOWNSEND. We have found this method helpful in other programs involving cooperatives since as long ago as the case in 1955, when it was said, "The person who shall be selected for this program shall be fully sympathetic with the purposes of the program.' 

Chairman MORGAN. Thank you, Mr. Townsend. 

Mr. TOWNSEND. Thank you. (Whereupon, at 12: 22 p.m., the committee was adjourned.)
